Figure 3.3
:

The MIs
J_{i}
appearing in the canonical basis
(
3.3.1
).
Squared propagators are represented by dots.

The canonical integrals contain square root prefactors
\lambda_{i}
and
\rho_{i}
defined by

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}=\sqrt{-t}\sqrt{4m_{i}^{2}-t}\,,\quad\rho_{i}=\sqrt{\frac{2m_{i}^{2}-t-\lambda_{i}}{m_{i}^{2}}}\,,\quad i=\ell,A\,.

(3.24)

These roots are simultaneously rationalized by the change of variables

\displaystyle t=-m_{\ell}^{2}\frac{(1-x)^{2}}{x^{2}}\,,\quad m_{A}^{2}=m_{\ell}^{2}\frac{(1-x^{2})^{2}y^{2}}{(1-y^{2})^{2}x^{2}}\,,

(3.25)

with a corresponding inverse map

\displaystyle x=\frac{\sqrt{4-\sigma_{\ell}}-\sqrt{-\sigma_{\ell}}}{2}\,,\quad y=\frac{\sqrt{4-\sigma_{A}}-\sqrt{-\sigma_{A}}}{2}\,,\quad\sigma_{i}=\frac{t}{m_{i}^{2}}\,.

(3.26)

The Euclidean kinematic regime
4
4

4

It is standard to solve Pfaffian systems for Euclidean kinematics
and then analytically continue the resulting GPLs to physical kinematic regimes.

\displaystyle\big\{t<0\big\}\quad\bigcap\quad\big\{0<m_{A}^{2}<m_{\ell}^{2}\quad\text{or}\quad 0<m_{\ell}^{2}<m_{A}^{2}\big\}

(3.27)

is parametrized by

\displaystyle\big\{0<x<1\big\}\quad\bigcap\quad\big\{0<y<x\quad\text{or}\quad 0<x<y\big\}

(3.28)

in the
(x,y)
coordinates.

The basis (
3.3.1
) satisfies a canonical Pfaffian system

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\vec{I}(x,y|\epsilon)=\epsilon\,P(x,y)\cdot\vec{I}(x,y|\epsilon)\,,\quad P(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{12}P_{i}\ \mathrm{d}\log\eta_{i}(x,y)\,,\quad P_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}^{20\times 20}

(3.29)

with letters

\displaystyle\eta_{1}

\displaystyle=x\,,\quad

\displaystyle\eta_{2}

\displaystyle=1+x\,,\quad

\displaystyle\eta_{3}

\displaystyle=1-x\,,

\displaystyle\eta_{4}

\displaystyle=1+x^{2}\,,\quad

\displaystyle\eta_{5}

\displaystyle=y\,,\quad

\displaystyle\eta_{6}

\displaystyle=1+y\,,

(3.30)

\displaystyle\eta_{7}

\displaystyle=1-y\,,\quad

\displaystyle\eta_{8}

\displaystyle=1+y^{2}\,,\quad

\displaystyle\eta_{9}

\displaystyle=x+y\,,

\displaystyle\eta_{10}

\displaystyle=x-y\,,\quad

\displaystyle\eta_{11}

\displaystyle=1+xy\,,\quad

\displaystyle\eta_{12}

\displaystyle=1-xy\,.

The general solution to this Pfaffian system is given by the path-ordered exponential
(
2.62
).
The result takes the form

\vec{I}(x,y|\epsilon)=\left[\sum_{i=0}^{4}M_{i}(x,y)\,\epsilon^{i}+O(\epsilon^{5})\right]\cdot\vec{I}_{0}(\epsilon)\,,

where the matrices
M_{i}(x,y)
contain GPLs built from the letters
\eta_{i}
,
and
\vec{I}_{0}(\epsilon)
is a vector of boundary constants.
The latter is fixed as follows:

•

By normalizing the integration measure as

\displaystyle\left(\frac{m_{\ell}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)^{\epsilon}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{D}}\ell_{i}}{i\pi^{\mathrm{D}/2}\Gamma(1+\epsilon)}\,,\quad i=1,2\,,

(3.31)

then direct integration (via Feynman parametrization) of the tadpoles
\{I_{1},\,I_{2}\}

as well as the factorized integral
I_{6}
gives

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{(1-y^{2})x^{2}}{(1-x^{2})y^{2}}\right)^{\epsilon}

(3.32)

\displaystyle I_{6}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{x^{2}}{(1-x^{2})^{2}}\right)^{\epsilon}\left[1-\zeta_{2}\epsilon^{2}-2\zeta_{3}\epsilon^{3}-\frac{9}{4}\zeta_{4}\epsilon^{4}+O(\epsilon^{5})\right]\,,

where
\zeta_{n}
is the
n
th Riemann zeta value.
These external inputs are fed into the DEQ to give
relations among the remaining boundary constants.

•

Boundary constants for the set of integrals

\{I_{3},I_{4},I_{5},I_{9},I_{10},I_{11},I_{12},I_{14},I_{16},I_{17},I_{18},\allowbreak I_{19}\}

are fixed by requiring regularity at the pseudo-threshold limit
t\to 0
.
In particular for

\{I_{3},I_{4},I_{5},I_{9},I_{10},I_{14},I_{16},I_{17}\},

the prefactors in
(
3.3.1
)
make them vanish in this limit
5
5

5

The vanishing of
I_{14}
as
t\to 0
follows from the results of

[
76
]
.

.
Further,

\{I_{11},I_{12},I_{18},I_{19}\}

are seen to vanish as
t\to 0
by analyzing their DEQs.
Finally,
the factorized double-bubble integral
I_{10}
vanishes in the limit because the

massive
bubble factor
(drawn in
blue
in Figure (
3.3
))
goes to
0
at a faster rate than the divergence of the
massless
bubble factor
(drawn in black).

•

Boundary constants for

\{I_{7},I_{8}\}

are determined by regularity at
m_{A}^{2}\to 0
.
Due to the prefactors in (
3.3.1
),
both integrals vanish in this limit.

•

Regularity at the limit

t\to 4m_{A}^{2}

fixes boundary constants for the final three integrals

\{I_{13},I_{15},I_{20}\}.

Thanks to the prefactors of (
3.3.1
),
they all vanish in this limit.

•

In some of the cases listed above,
the equations for the boundary constants only led to solutions at very high precision,
as opposed to analytic expressions in terms of transcendental constants.
Fortunately,
using the implementation of the

PSLQ
algorithm
[
77
]
in
PolyLogTools

[
67
]
,
we can reconstruct the analytic constants by fitting to an ansatz.

3.3.2 Equal-mass case

The equal-mass topology is shown in Figure (
3.4
).
Its associated FI family is given by (
3.10
)
upon setting

m_{A}^{2}=m_{\ell}^{2}=m^{2}

in
(
3.6
) and (
3.11
).

Figure 3.4
:
Equal-mass Feynman diagram.

IBP reduction reveals 15 MIs.
The Magnus expansion then yields the canonical basis

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllllll}&I_{1}&=&\epsilon^{2}J_{1}&I_{2}&=&\epsilon^{2}\lambda_{m}J_{2}\\
&I_{3}&=&-\epsilon^{2}tJ_{3}&I_{4}&=&\epsilon^{2}\left[\frac{\lambda_{m}+t}{2}J_{3}+\lambda_{m}J_{4}\right]\\
&I_{5}&=&-\epsilon^{2}tJ_{5}&I_{6}&=&\epsilon^{2}m^{2}J_{6}\\
&I_{7}&=&-\epsilon^{2}t\lambda_{m}J_{7}&I_{8}&=&\epsilon^{3}\lambda_{m}J_{8}\\
&I_{9}&=&\epsilon^{2}\left[\frac{4m^{2}-\lambda_{m}-t}{4}(J_{3}+2J_{4})+\ m^{2}(4m^{2}-t)J_{9}\right]&I_{10}&=&\epsilon^{3}\lambda_{m}J_{10}\\
&I_{11}&=&\epsilon^{3}(1-2\epsilon)tJ_{11}&I_{12}&=&\epsilon^{3}t(t-4m^{2})J_{12}\\
&I_{13}&=&\epsilon^{3}\lambda_{m}J_{13}&I_{14}&=&\eqref{dm_equal_mass_MI_I14}\\
&I_{15}&=&-\epsilon^{4}\lambda_{m}tJ_{15}\,,\end{array}

with

\displaystyle I_{14}=\epsilon^{2}\left[(4m^{2}-\lambda_{m}-t)(J_{2}-\epsilon J_{13})+(2\epsilon-1)(4m^{2}-t)J_{14}\right]\,.

(3.41)

Diagrams for the integrals
J_{i}
can be seen in Figure (
3.5
).

(a)

(b)

(c)

(d)

(e)

(f)

(g)

(h)

(i)

(j)

(k)

(l)

(m)

(n)

(o)

Figure 3.5
:

The MIs
J_{i}
appearing in the canonical basis
(
3.3.2
).
Squared propagators are represented by dots.

The canonical integrals contain an algebraic function
\lambda_{m}
given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{m}=\sqrt{-t}\sqrt{4m^{2}-t}\,.

(3.42)

Both of these roots are simultaneously rationalized by the coordinate change

\displaystyle t=-m^{2}\frac{(1-w)^{2}}{w}\,,

(3.43)

whose inverse map is given by

\displaystyle w=\frac{\sqrt{4m^{2}-t}-\sqrt{-t}}{\sqrt{4m^{2}-t}+\sqrt{-t}}\,.

(3.44)

The Euclidean kinematic regime

\displaystyle\big\{t<0\big\}\quad\bigcap\quad\big\{m^{2}>0\big\}

(3.45)

is then given by

\displaystyle 0<w<1\,.

(3.46)

The canonical Pfaffian system for the basis (
3.3.2
) takes the form

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\vec{I}(w,\epsilon)=\epsilon\,P(w)\cdot\vec{I}(w,\epsilon)\,,\quad P(w)=\sum_{i=1}^{3}P_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\log\eta_{i}(w)\,,\quad P_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}^{15\times 15}\,,

(3.47)

where the three letters are

\displaystyle\eta_{1}=w\,,\quad\eta_{2}=1+w\,,\quad\eta_{3}=1-w\,.

(3.48)

This means that the Pfaffian system has a solution in terms

harmonic polylogarithms
(HPLs) denoted by
H(\vec{z}|w)
,
i.e. GPLs with weights
z_{i}\in\{-1,0,1\}

[
78
]
.

Boundary constants are determined as follows:

•

The integrals

\{I_{1},I_{5},I_{6}\}

can be directly integrated,
and are provided as external input.
The first integral is normalized to unity by the measure,
and the second integral is swiftly computed with Feynman parameters,
giving

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=1

(3.49)

\displaystyle I_{5}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{w}{(1-w)^{2}}\right)^{\epsilon}\left[1-\zeta_{2}\epsilon^{2}-2\zeta_{3}\epsilon^{3}-\frac{9}{4}\zeta_{4}\epsilon^{4}+O(\epsilon^{5})\right]\,.

(3.50)

The equal-mass sunrise integral
I_{6}
is computed by following the strategy of

[
79
]
.
Since the square of its external momentum equals its internal masses,

p^{2}=m^{2}
,
this integral evaluates to a number rather than a function,
wherefore it cannot immediately be computed from a Pfaffian system.
The trick is to change two of the propagator masses to
M^{2}
rather than
m^{2}
,
and then solve a Pfaffian system in the auxiliary variable
x=m^{2}/M^{2}
.
Boundary constants are fixed by regularity at
x=0
.
The desired integral is obtained by setting
x=1
,
giving the result

\displaystyle I_{6}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2}\epsilon^{2}+\left[3\zeta_{2}\log(2)-\frac{7}{4}\zeta_{3}\right]\epsilon^{3}

(3.51)

\displaystyle+\left[\frac{31}{4}\zeta_{4}-6\zeta_{2}\log^{2}(2)-\frac{\log^{4}(2)}{2}-12\mathrm{Li}_{4}(\tfrac{1}{2})\right]\epsilon^{4}+O(\epsilon^{5})\,.

•

Boundary values for the integrals

\{I_{2},I_{3},I_{4},I_{7},I_{8},I_{9},I_{11},I_{13},I_{14}\}

are fixed by regularity at the pseudo-threshold
t\to 0
.
In particular, due to the prefactors in (
3.3.2
),
the integrals

\{I_{2},I_{3},I_{4},I_{8},I_{11},I_{13}\}

go to
0
in this limit.
By analyzing the Pfaffian system as
t\to 0
,
we obtain the relations

I_{9}|_{t\to 0}=3I_{6}

and

I_{14}|_{t\to 0}=6I_{6},

which fixes boundary values for
I_{9}
and
I_{14}
because
I_{6}
is known by the formula
(
3.51
).
The integral
I_{7}
vanishes because the
massive
canonical bubble factor
goes to zero faster than the divergence of
massless
bubble factor.

•

For
I_{10}
,
we use regularity at the pseudo-threshold
t\to 4m^{2}
.
Thanks to the prefactor in (
3.3.2
),
the integral vanishes in this limit.

•

Boundary constants for the integral
I_{12}
follow from comparing the
left- and right-hand sides of

I_{12}=I_{2}\times I_{10},

since
I_{2}
and
I_{10}
are known.
This relation can be gleaned from Figure (
3.3.2
) by
recalling that the double-tadpole
I_{1}
is normalized
to unity due to the integration measure.

•

We are able to determine boundary constants for the top sector integral
I_{15}

by comparing our result with
[
80
]
.

The final results for the canonical MIs are quite compact.
For instance,
the top sector integral reads

\displaystyle I_{15}=\epsilon^{4}

\displaystyle\Big[2\zeta_{3}H(0|w)+4\zeta_{2}H(0,0|w)+8H(0,0,-1,0|w)+H(0,0,0,0|w)

(3.52)

\displaystyle+6H(0,0,0,1|w)+4H(0,1,0,0|w)+3\zeta_{4}\Big]+O(\epsilon^{5})\,.

3.3.3 Soft limit

The soft limit
t\to 0
of the form factors in
Section (
3.4.3
) is of phenomenological interest.
For this reason,
we now compute the two-mass MIs in this limit.
The original two-mass topology simplifies in this limit,
as depicted in Figure (
3.6
).

Figure 3.6
:
Diagrammatic representation of the soft limit.

The reason for this simplification is as follows.
Recall that
t=q^{2}
,
where
q^{\mu}
is the momentum of the mediator particle.
Setting
q^{\mu}=0
in the integral family (
3.10
) implies that

D_{2}\to D_{1}\text{ and }D_{5}\to D_{4},

wherefore we only need to consider IBPs and DEQs for the simplified integral family

\displaystyle I_{\nu_{1}\nu_{3}\nu_{4}\nu_{6}\nu_{7}}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{D}}\ell_{1}}{(2\pi)^{\mathrm{D}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{D}}\ell_{2}}{(2\pi)^{\mathrm{D}}}\frac{D_{7}^{\nu_{7}}}{D_{1}^{\nu_{1}}D_{3}^{\nu_{3}}D_{4}^{\nu_{4}}D_{6}^{\nu_{6}}}

(3.53)

having four denominators

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllllll}&D_{1}&=&\ell_{1}^{2}-m_{\ell}^{2}\,,&D_{3}&=&\ell_{2}^{2}-m_{A}^{2}\,,\\
&D_{4}&=&(\ell_{2}+p)^{2}\,,&D_{6}&=&(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+p)^{2}-m_{\ell}^{2}\,,\end{array}

and an ISP
D_{7}=(\ell_{1}-p)^{2}
.
Note that
p^{\prime}
has been set equal to
p
due to momentum conservation.

IBP reduction for the family (
3.53
) reveals 4 MIs.
By the Magnus exponential,
we obtain the canonical basis

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllllll}&I_{1}&=&\epsilon^{2}J_{1}\,,&I_{2}&=&\epsilon^{2}J_{2}\,,\\
&I_{3}&=&\epsilon^{2}m_{\ell}m_{A}\big[J_{3}+2J_{4}\big]\,,&I_{4}&=&\epsilon^{2}m_{A}^{2}J_{4}\,,\end{array}

in terms of the integrals
\{J_{1},J_{2},J_{3},J_{4}\}
shown in Figure (
3.7
).

(a)

(b)

(c)

(d)

Figure 3.7
:

The integrals
J_{1}
appearing in the canonical basis (
3.3.3
).
Squared propagators are depicted by dots.

Defining the mass-ratio variable

\displaystyle z=\frac{m_{A}}{m_{\ell}}\,,

(3.58)

the canonical Pfaffian system takes the form

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\vec{I}(z|\epsilon)=\epsilon\,P(z)\cdot\vec{I}(z|\epsilon)\,,\quad P(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{3}P_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\log\eta_{i}(z)\,,\quad P_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}^{4\times 4}\,,

(3.59)

where the
\eta_{i}
are HPL letters

\displaystyle\eta_{1}=z\,,\quad\eta_{2}=1+z\,,\quad\eta_{3}=1-z\,.

(3.60)

Boundary constants for the solution vector are fixed as follows:

•

The two double-tadpoles
\{I_{1},I_{2}\}
can be directly integrated to

\displaystyle I_{1}=1\quad\text{and}\quad I_{2}=z^{-2\epsilon}\,.

(3.61)

•

Boundary values for the two sunrises
\{I_{3},I_{4}\}
are fixed by regularity at
m_{A}\to 0

as in
[
79
]
.
In particular,
due to the prefactors of (
3.3.3
),
both integrals vanish in this limit.

Section 3.4
Form factor results

We now present form factor results for the following cases:

•

Two masses (
m_{A}\neq m_{\ell}
): scalar and pseudo-scalar.

•

Equal-mass (
m_{A}=m_{\ell}
): scalar and pseudo-scalar.

•

Soft limit (
t\to 0
): scalar and pseudo-scalar.

Let us emphasize again that we only include results for one-body interactions.
The analytic form factors in this section are all rescaled as

\mathcal{F}_{S,P}\to\frac{\pi^{2}}{\alpha_{\mathrm{em}}}\mathcal{F}_{S,P}

for the sake of readability.

3.4.1 Two-mass case

The analytic expressions for the two-mass form factors

\mathcal{F}_{S,P}(t,m_{\ell},m_{A})

would span several pages,
so we omit them here.
Instead,
we showcase the results as numerical plots in Figures
(
3.8(a)
) and (
3.8(b)
).

(a)

Two-mass
pseudoscalar
form factor as a function of momentum transfer
t
.
The nucleon mass
m_{A}
is set equal to the proton mass.
The dashed lines represent individual contributions from the electron (
e
),
the muon (
\mu
),
and the tau lepton (
\tau
).
The solid
red
line shows the sum of all contributions.
The grey shaded band indicates the range of
t
that is expected to be relevant for
DM particles arriving from our local galaxy.

(b)

Two-mass
scalar
form factor as a function of momentum transfer
t
.
See the caption of Figure (
3.8(a)
) for additional details.

3.4.2 Equal-mass case

Define the shorthand notation

G(\vec{z}|w):=G_{\vec{z}}(w).

Scalar form factor

The analytic expression for the equal-mass form factor with a scalar mediator particle is

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{S}(w)={-}\frac{(20\,w^{2}{+}4\,w)}{\,(w{-}1)\,(w{+}1)^{2}}\,G_{0}(w)\;{+}\;\frac{w}{\,(w{-}1)\,(w{+}1)}\,\big[24\,G_{{-}1,0}(w){+}4\,G_{1,0}(w)\big]

\displaystyle{+}\frac{w}{\,(w{-}1)^{2}\,(w{+}1)^{3}}[{-}12\,w^{3}{-}38\,w^{2}{+}8\,w{+}2]\,G_{0,0}(w)\;{-}\;\frac{w}{\pi^{2}\,(w{-}1)^{2}}\,\big[2\,G_{0,0,0}(w){+}4\,\zeta_{3}\big]

\displaystyle{+}\frac{w\,\zeta_{3}}{\,(w{-}1)^{3}\,(w{+}1)}\,[5\,w^{2}{-}6\,w{+}5]\,G_{0}(w)\;{+}\;\frac{w}{\pi^{2}\,(w{+}1)^{2}}\,\left[12\,G_{0,0,1}(w){-}\frac{4\,\pi^{2}}{3}\,G_{0}(w){+}16\,G_{1}(w)\right]

\displaystyle{+}\frac{w}{\,(w{-}1)^{2}\,(w{+}1)^{2}}\,\Big[({-}16\,w^{2}{+}64\,w{-}16)\,G_{0,{-}1,0}(w){+}(8\,w^{2}{-}48\,w{+}8)\,G_{1,0,0}(w){+}16\,w\,G_{0,1,0}(w)\Big]

\displaystyle{+}\frac{w}{\,(w{-}1)\,(w{+}1)^{3}}\Bigg[({-}14\,w^{2}{+}4\,w{-}14)\,G_{0,1}(w){+}\left(\frac{w^{2}}{3}{+}2\,w{+}\frac{1}{3}\right)\,G_{1,0}(w)\,\pi^{2}

(3.62)

\displaystyle{+}(2\,w^{2}{+}12\,w{+}2)\,\big(G_{1,0,0,0}(w)\,{-}\,G_{0,0,1,0}(w)\big){+}\left(2\,w^{2}{-}\frac{20\,w}{3}{+}2\right)\pi^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle{+}\frac{w}{\,(w{-}1)^{3}\,(w{+}1)^{3}}\Bigg[\left(\frac{5\,w^{4}}{6}{-}2\,w^{3}{+}\frac{23\,w^{2}}{3}{-}2\,w{+}\frac{5}{6}\right)\,G_{0,0}(w)\,\pi^{2}

\displaystyle{+}\left(8\,w^{4}{-}32\,w^{3}{+}112\,w^{2}{-}32\,w{+}8\right)\,G_{0,0,{-}1,0}(w){+}\left(\frac{3\,w^{4}}{2}{-}2\,w^{3}{+}9\,w^{2}{-}2\,w{+}\frac{3}{2}\right)\,G_{0,0,0,0}(w)

\displaystyle{+}\left({-}9\,w^{4}{+}12\,w^{3}{-}54\,w^{2}{+}12\,w{-}9\right)\,G_{0,0,0,1}(w){+}\left({-}2\,w^{4}{+}24\,w^{3}{-}76\,w^{2}{+}24\,w{-}2\right)\,G_{0,1,0,0}(w)

\displaystyle{+}\left(\frac{w^{4}}{18}{-}\frac{2\,w^{3}}{45}{+}\frac{11\,w^{2}}{45}{-}\frac{2\,w}{45}{+}\frac{1}{18}\right)\,\pi^{4}\Bigg].

This expression can be turned into an
s
-channel form factor by crossing symmetry.
Then it is related to a form factor for Higgs decay into two heavy quarks
[
81
]
.

Pseudo-scalar form factor

The equal-mass form factor for the pseudoscalar reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{P}(w)={-}\frac{w^{2}}{\,(w{-}1)^{3}\,(w{+}1)}\,\left[\frac{2\pi^{2}}{3}\,G_{0}(w){+}4\,G_{0,0,0}(w)\right]

\displaystyle{+}\frac{w}{\,(w{-}1)^{2}}\,\left[G_{0,0}(w){-}4\,G_{0,0,1}(w){-}4\,G_{0,1,0}(w){+}8\,G_{1,0,0}(w){+}12\,\zeta_{3}{+}\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\right]

(3.63)

\displaystyle{+}\frac{w}{\,(w{-}1)\,(w{+}1)}\,\left[{-}3\,\zeta_{3}\,G_{0}(w){-}\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}\,G_{0,0}(w){-}\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\,G_{1,0}(w){-}\frac{1}{2}\,G_{0,0,0,0}(w)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 89.626pt\left.{+}3\,G_{0,0,0,1}(w){+}2\,G_{0,0,1,0}(w){-}2\,G_{0,1,0,0}(w){-}2\,G_{1,0,0,0}(w){-}\frac{\pi^{4}}{45}\right].

Using crossing symmetry,
this form factor is related to the
s
-channel QCD form factor dubbed
A_{R}(s,m,m)
in

[
74
]
.

3.4.3 Soft limit

The soft limit
t\to 0
is important for phenomenological applications,
as the DM particles are expected to scatter elastically.
It is therefore useful to have simple expressions for the form factors in this limit
that can be easily numerically evaluated.

Scalar form factor

The scalar form factor is finite at
t=0
,
so the result

\mathcal{F}_{S}(t=0,m_{\ell},m_{A})

will only depend on the mass ratio
z=m_{A}/m_{\ell}
.
Writing the form factor as an expansion in terms of FIs as in (
3.9
),
we then IBP reduce those integrals to the soft limit MIs from Section (
3.3.3
).
The smooth
4
-dimensional limit
\epsilon\to 0
finally yields the result

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{S}(t=0,m_{\ell},m_{A})

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{z}\left[1-\frac{\log(z)}{2}+f_{S}(z)+f_{S}(-z)\right]

(3.64)

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad f_{S}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{4+3z+z^{3}}{4z^{2}}\big[\log|z|\log(1+z)+\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-z)\big]\,.

Pseudo-scalar form factor

The pseudo-scalar form factor is logarithmically divergent as
t\to 0
,
so we compute an asymptotic expansion in terms of the variables
(x,y)
from (
3.25
).
Keeping
m_{A,\ell}
fixed,
the soft limit then corresponds to
(x,y)\to(1,1)
.

Our strategy for computing the asymptotic expansion is as follows:

1.

Staring with the full expression for the form factor

\mathcal{F}_{P}(t,m_{\ell},m_{A})

in terms of GPLs,
we use
PolyLogTools
to rewrite every
G(\vec{z}\,|\,v)
such that

\vec{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{M}

and
v=x
or
v=y
.
The weight vectors
\vec{z}
will thereby not contain any
x
’s or
y
’s,
which makes it easier to expand the GPLs.

2.

PolyLogTools
has a built-in function for asymptotically expanding GPLs.
However,
it only supports an expansion around
v\sim 0
,
not
v\sim 1
.
Introducing a dummy variable
v^{\prime}
by
v=1-v^{\prime}
,
we hence seek to rewrite every
G(\vec{z}\,|\,v)
in terms of
G(\vec{z}\,|\,v^{\prime})
.
This is done by employing the well-known recursion

\displaystyle G(z_{1},\ldots,z_{M}|v)

\displaystyle=G(z_{1},\ldots,z_{M}|1-v^{\prime})

(3.65)

\displaystyle=G(z_{1},\ldots,z_{M}|1)+\int_{0}^{v^{\prime}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t-(1-z_{1})}G(z_{2},\ldots,z_{M}|1-t)\,,

which produces GPLs having argument
v^{\prime}
due to the upper integration bound
in the second term.
Experimentally,
we find that this recursion admits a closed-form solution

\displaystyle G(z_{1},\ldots,z_{M}|1-v^{\prime})=\sum_{i=0}^{M}

\displaystyle G(1-z_{1},\ldots,1-z_{i}|v^{\prime})

(3.66)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle G(z_{i+1},\ldots,z_{M}|1)\,\big|_{\text{shuffle reg.}}\,,

where we emphasized the need for
shuffle regularization

to deal with divergent GPLs
[
61
, Section 5.3]
.
We use the convention

G(1-z_{1},\ldots,1-z_{i}|v^{\prime})=1

when
i=0
.

As an aside,
although it is not a deep result,
we have not been able to locate this closed-form solution elsewhere in the literature.

3.

PolyLogTools
can now expand the terms

G(1-z_{1},\ldots,1-z_{i}|v^{\prime})

from (
3.66
) around
v^{\prime}\sim 0

up the desired order

\mathcal{O}\big((1-x)^{n},(1-y)^{m}\big).

The latter notation means that we first expand in
(1-x)
up to
n
th order,
and then in
(1-y)
up to
m
th order.
The reversed expansion order is written as

\mathcal{O}\big((1-y)^{n},(1-x)^{m}\big).

The maximal powers

(n,m)

are chosen so as to give sufficiently accurate numerical results in comparison with
the full form factor

\mathcal{F}_{P}(t,m_{\ell},m_{A})

when the latter is evaluated for small
t
.

A double asymptotic expansion in
(x,y)
will depend on the orderings

x<y\text{ or }y<x,

so we treat these two cases separately.
The orderings correspond to the mass hierarchies

m_{\ell}<m_{A}\text{ and }m_{A}<m_{\ell}

respectively.
By the steps outlined above,
we obtain the following results for the pseudoscalar form factor in the soft limit:

•

Mass hierarchy
m_{\ell}<m_{A}\,(x<y)
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{P}(t\sim 0,\,m_{\ell}<m_{A})=-

\displaystyle 2\zeta_{2}+\frac{(1-x)}{(1-y)}\big[-2\log(1-x)-2\log(2)+3\big]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{(1-x)^{2}}{(1-y)}\left[-\log(1-x)-\log(2)+\frac{1}{2}\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(1-x)^{2}\left[\frac{\log(1-x)}{2}+\frac{\log(2)}{2}+\frac{37\zeta_{2}}{60}-\frac{1}{4}\right]

(3.67)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{6(1-x)}{\zeta_{2}}\left[\log(1-x)+\log(2)+\frac{3\zeta_{2}}{2}-\frac{3}{2}\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\big((1-y),\,(1-x)^{3}\big)\,.

•

Mass hierarchy
m_{A}<m_{\ell}\,(y<x)
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{P}(t\sim 0,\,m_{A}<m_{\ell})=

\displaystyle\frac{(1-x)}{(1-y)}\left[-\frac{3\log(1-x)}{2}-\frac{(1-y)}{2}-2\log(2)+\frac{17}{8}\right]

(3.68)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(1-x)\left[\frac{3\log(1-x)}{4}+\frac{\log(1-y)}{4}+\log(2)+\frac{3\zeta_{2}}{2}-\frac{21}{16}\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\big((1-x)^{2},\,(1-y)\big)\,.

These formulas differ by roughly
1\%
in comparison with the full analytic results evaluated at

t=-10^{-7}\text{GeV}^{2}
.

Part II
\mathcal{D}
-modules

Chapter 4
Introduction to
\mathcal{D}
-modules

The preceding chapters of this thesis dealt with tried and tested methods for evaluating FIs.
We now venture into somewhat uncharted territory from the perspective of the physics literature.

Based on the papers
[
82
,
83
]
,
the next six chapters of this thesis study FIs within the framework of
\mathcal{D}
-
modules
.
A
\mathcal{D}
-module can,
informally,
be thought of as a vector space of linear differential operators that annihilate a given function.
As a supplement to the tools coming from complex
analysis
,

\mathcal{D}
-modules provide
algebraic
machinery to uncover properties of PDEs.
For instance,
one can learn the dimension of the solution space,
construct bases of differential operators,
obtain relations between basis elements,
and more.
We will only scratch the surface of this vast topic,
so we refer to the classic textbooks

[
23
]
and
[
24
]

for exhaustive discussions on
\mathcal{D}
-modules.

Our overarching goal in
Part II
of this thesis is
to build a bridge between these two independently well-studied topics,
namely FIs and
\mathcal{D}
-modules.
But,
as it so often happens in blue skies research,
practical applications will spring out as well.

Section 4.1
What is a
\mathcal{D}
-module?

How might one represent a function?
Take
\sin(x)
as an example.
It can be represented in several ways,
each one having different advantages.
For instance,
it can be analytically represented as a power series,
or geometrically via the unit circle.
In the philosophy of
\mathcal{D}
-modules,

\sin(x)
is represented by the set of linear PDEs which annihilate the function;
in this case simply

\{\partial^{2}+1\}.

We note that this philosophy would not work for non-linear PDEs,
since a differential operator
L
cannot be "pulled out" in the form

L\bullet f=0

if there is a term like
f(x)^{2}
.

Example 4.1.
A striking example of this philosophy comes from calculating roots of an
N
th order polynomial

\displaystyle z_{0}+z_{1}x+z_{2}x^{2}+\cdots+z_{N-1}x^{N-1}+z_{N}x^{N}=(x-r_{1})\cdots(x-r_{N})\,.

(4.1)

Is there an equation for the roots

r_{k}=r_{k}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{N})

in terms of the indeterminate polynomial coefficients
z_{k}
?
There is,
for instance,
a well-known formula for

r_{1,2}(z_{0},z_{1},z_{2})

in the quadratic case
N=2
.

Alas,
Galois theory teaches us that no such formula exists in terms of radicals when
N\geq 5
.
But if we settle for an infinite series representation of
r_{k}
,
then such a formula
does
exist!

Fixing
k
,
it turns out that
r_{k}
is annihilated,
indeed
represented
,
by the following set of partial differential operators:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z_{i}\partial z_{j}}-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z_{k}\partial z_{l}}\quad

\displaystyle\text{whenever}\quad i+j=k+l\,,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N}i\,z_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}+1\quad

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\text{and}\hskip 11.38092pt\quad\sum_{i=0}^{N}z_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}\,.

(4.2)

Many features of polynomial roots can then be learned from studying these differential operators.
For one,
it is possible to construct power series solutions of the form

r_{k}=\sum_{i_{0}\ldots i_{N}}^{\infty}f_{k}(i_{0},\ldots,i_{N})z_{0}^{i_{0}}\cdots z_{N}^{i_{N}},

where the
f_{k}
’s are various combinations of factorials
[
84
]
.

\blacksquare

So,
what is a
\mathcal{D}
-module?
It is a module over a ring of
\mathcal{D}
ifferential operators.
Let us spell this out more formally.

Let
R
be a ring with a unit
"1"
,
and
M
an abelian group w.r.t. addition
"+"
.
Given
r\in R
and
m\in M
,
we assume the existence of a map

\displaystyle\psi:R\times M\to M

\displaystyle\psi(r,m)=r\bullet m\,,

(4.3)

where
r\bullet m
denotes the
action
of
r
on
m
.
For instance,
if
R=\mathbb{C}
and
M=\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
then
\psi
could represent the scalar multiplication of a complex number
r
on a complex vector
m
.

Definition 4.1
.

An abelian group
M
becomes a
left
1
1

1

Right

R
-modules are defined by the same axioms with
r
’s acting from the right,
i.e.
m\bullet r
.

R
-
module

when the following three axioms are fulfilled
2
2

2

We omit
"+"
subscripts of kind

(r_{1}+_{R}r_{2})\bullet m=r_{1}\bullet m+_{M}r_{2}\bullet m

for notational cleanliness.

:

1.

For any
r\in R
and
m_{i}\in M
,
we have

r\bullet(m_{1}+m_{2})=r\bullet m_{1}+r\bullet m_{2}.

2.

For any
r_{i}\in R
and
m\in M
,
we have

(r_{1}r_{2})\bullet m=r_{1}\bullet(r_{2}\bullet m)

and

(r_{1}+r_{2})\bullet m=r_{1}\bullet m+r_{2}\bullet m.

3.

For any
m\in M
,
we have

1\bullet m=m.

If
R
is a commutative field such as
\mathbb{C}
,
then a left
R
-module is nothing but an
R
-vector space.

The
N
th
Weyl algebra

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{N}=\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{N}]\langle\partial_{1},\ldots,\partial_{N}\rangle

(4.4)

is generated by symbols
z_{i}
and
\partial_{i}
subject to the commutation relations

\displaystyle[z_{i},z_{j}]=0\,,\quad[\partial_{i},\partial_{j}]=0\,,\quad[\partial_{i},z_{j}]=\delta_{ij}\,.

(4.5)

We shall often suppress the subscript in
\mathcal{D}_{N}
when the number of variables is understood.
The square brackets
[\ldots]
in
(
4.4
)
mean that the
z_{i}
are thought of as coefficients of the generators
\partial_{i}
.
Note that the last commutator of
(
4.5
)
encodes Leibniz’ rule of differentiation when we insert a dummy function
f(z)
:

\frac{\partial}{\partial z}[zf(z)]-z\frac{\partial}{\partial z}f(z)=f(z).

We therefore interpret
\mathcal{D}
as the collection of all differential operators having polynomial coefficients.
For instance,

(z_{1}^{2}+z_{3}+1)\partial_{1}^{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{3}.

We emphasize that
\mathcal{D}
has the structure of a ring.

We also have the
N
th
rational
Weyl algebra

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{N}=\mathbb{C}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{N})\langle\partial_{1},\ldots,\partial_{N}\rangle\,,

(4.6)

wherein the coefficients are promoted to rational functions.
We shall often shorten
\mathcal{R}_{N}
to
\mathcal{R}
.
In this case,
the commutators are extended to

\displaystyle[z_{i},z_{j}]=0\,,\quad[\partial_{i},\partial_{j}]=0\,,\quad[\partial_{i},r(z)]=\frac{\partial r(z)}{\partial z_{i}}\,,

(4.7)

for a rational function

r(z)\in\mathbb{C}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{N}).

The rational Weyl algebra is also endowed with a ring structure.

Definition 4.2
.

A
\mathcal{D}
-module is a left
R
-module
(see Definition (
4.1
))
with the specific choice of ring
R=\mathcal{D}
.
An
\mathcal{R}
-module is defined similarly for
R=\mathcal{R}
.

We shall be careful in distinguishing between ring
multiplication
and ring
action

"\bullet"
,
because there is a difference between e.g.

\displaystyle\partial_{i}z_{i}=z_{i}\partial_{i}+1\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{i}\bullet z_{i}=1\,.

(4.8)

Definition (
4.2
) can
be instantiated in many different ways because it only specifies the ring
R
.

Particular

\mathcal{D}
- and
\mathcal{R}
-modules follow from further specifying the
abelian group
M
and the action map
\psi
.
Let us list some examples.

Example 4.2.
The Weyl algebras
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{R}
are themselves
\mathcal{D}
- and
\mathcal{R}
-modules respectively.
The abelian group
M
is formed from the addition of differential operators.
The algebras acts on themselves according to
(
4.5
) in the case of
\mathcal{D}

and
(
4.7
) in the case of
\mathcal{R}
,
which specifies
\psi
.

\blacksquare

Example 4.3.

Many function spaces are equipped with the structure of a
\mathcal{D}
-module.
Take
M
to be some suitable space of holomorphic functions on a domain of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Assume that
M
is closed under differentiation,
meaning that if
f(z)\in M
then also
\frac{\partial f}{\partial z_{i}}\in M
.
The addition of such functions indeed forms an abelian group.
The action map

\psi:R\times M\to M

is defined by

\displaystyle\psi(\partial_{i},f)=\partial_{i}\bullet f:=\frac{\partial f}{\partial z_{i}}\,,\quad\psi(z_{i},f)=z_{i}\bullet f:=z_{i}\cdot f\,,

(4.9)

where
"\cdot"
in the last equation stands for ordinary scalar multiplication.

\blacksquare

Example 4.4.
The most important example of a
\mathcal{D}
-module (or
\mathcal{R}
-module) for the study of PDEs
is arguably that of the
quotient
ring
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{I}
(or
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{I}
),
where
\mathcal{I}
is a so-called
left ideal
to be defined shortly.
The abelian group
M
is given by the addition of operators,
and the action is that of the Weyl algebra.

To motivate the notion of a left ideal
\mathcal{I}
,
consider a collection of differential operators

\{L_{1},L_{2},\ldots\}

that annihilate some function
f
:

L_{1}\bullet f=L_{2}\bullet f=\ldots=0.

Note that we still get zero if we act with any other operator
L\in\mathcal{D}
from the left:

LL_{1}\bullet f=LL_{2}\bullet f=\ldots=0.

The definition
3
3

3

This is more of a ”physicist’s definition”.
Formally,
a left ideal
\mathcal{I}
of a ring
R
satisfies
1) it is a subgroup under addition,
and 2) for
r\in R
and
L\in\mathcal{I}
we have
r\,L\in\mathcal{I}
.

of a left ideal
\mathcal{I}
in the Weyl algebra encapsulates this fact:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}=\langle L_{1},L_{2},\ldots\rangle=\{LL_{1},\,LL_{2},\ldots\ |\ \forall\ L\in\mathcal{D}\}\,.

(4.10)

The notation
\langle L_{1},L_{2},\ldots\rangle
means that the
L
’s
generate

\mathcal{I}
.

The quotient ring

\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{I}

is then given by all operators
L\in\mathcal{D}
modulo the equivalence relation
\sim
determined by

\displaystyle L\sim K\iff L=K+K_{\mathcal{I}}\quad\text{where}\quad K\in\mathcal{D}\,,\ K_{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{I}\,.

(4.11)

Take,
for instance,
the left ideal generated by

\mathcal{I}=\langle\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\rangle\,.

Define two operators by

\displaystyle L\

\displaystyle=\ \partial_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad

\displaystyle K\

\displaystyle=\ \partial_{1}z_{1}\partial_{2}\ \overset{\eqref{Weyl_algebra_commutators}}{=}\ (1+z_{1}\partial_{1})\partial_{2}\ =\ \partial_{2}+z_{1}\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\,.

(4.12)

L
and
K
represent
the same
element inside

\mathcal{D}_{2}/\mathcal{I}

because they only differ by a term
z_{1}\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\in\mathcal{I}
.
We say that
L
and
K
lie in the same
equivalence class
,
written as

[L]=[K].

We shall see that the algebraic structure of the
\mathcal{D}
-module
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{I}

carries a wealth of information about the functions that are annihilated by the ideal
\mathcal{I}
.
This
\mathcal{D}
-module hence embodies the intuition,
argued for in the beginning of this section,
that a function can be represented by the differential operators that annihilate it.

\blacksquare

Section 4.2
Holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-modules

A
\mathcal{D}
-module
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{I}
has particularly nice features when it is
holonomic
.
Holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-modules describe FIs,
trigonometric functions,
various probability distributions,
hypergeometric functions,
Bessel functions,
and GPLs.
It is necessary to introduce a few concepts before we can define the notion of holonomicity
(we refer to the seminal text
[
85
]
for many more details).

•

Using the Weyl algebra commutation relations,
any operator
L\in\mathcal{D}
can be uniquely expressed in the
normally ordered form

\displaystyle L\quad=\quad\sum_{(p,q)\,\in\,\mathrm{supp}\left(L\right)}c_{p,q}\,z^{p}\,\partial^{q}\,,\quad c_{p,q}\in\mathbb{C}\,.

(4.13)

The summation range
\mathrm{supp}\left(L\right)
denotes a finite set of non-zero multi-indices

(p,q)=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{N},q_{1},\ldots,q_{N}),

and we introduced the multi-index exponent notation

\displaystyle z^{p}=z_{1}^{p_{1}}\cdots z_{N}^{p_{N}}\quad\text{and}\quad\partial^{q}=\partial_{1}^{q_{1}}\cdots\partial_{N}^{q_{N}}\,,

(4.14)

which will be used frequently throughout this text.

•

For a given a vector
(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2N}
,
the
(u,v)
-weight
of
z^{p}\,\partial^{q}
is defined by

u\cdot p+v\cdot q.

•

The differential operator
\mathrm{in}_{(u,v)}(L)
is constructed by only
keeping the largest
(u,v)
-weight terms in
L
.

•

The
initial form
is defined by
\mathrm{in}_{(u,v)}(L)
for the specific choice

u=(0,\ldots,0)=\mathbf{0}

and

v=(1,\ldots,1)=\mathbf{1}.

I.e. one assigns weight
0
to all
z
’s,
and weight
1
to all
\partial
’s.
The standard notation for the initial form is

\displaystyle\mathrm{in}_{(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1})}(L)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(p,q)\,\in\,\mathrm{supp}\left(L\right),\\
|q|\text{ is max}\end{subarray}}c_{p,q}\,z^{p}\,\xi^{q}\,,

(4.15)

where

|q|=q_{1}+\ldots+q_{N}.

The
\partial
’s have here been replaced by commuting
\xi
variables,
but this replacement may only be done
after
writing
L
in normally ordered form.
As an example,
take the normally ordered operator

\displaystyle L=z_{1}^{2}+2\partial_{1}^{2}\partial_{2}+z_{2}^{3}\partial_{1}\partial_{2}+z_{1}z_{2}\partial_{1}^{3}.

(4.16)

From left to right,
the terms have weights

(0,3,2,3).

So we only keep the 2nd and 4th terms,
giving

\displaystyle\mathrm{in}_{(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1})}(L)=2\xi_{1}^{2}\xi_{2}+z_{1}z_{2}\xi_{1}^{3}\,.

(4.17)

•

Given a left ideal
\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{D}_{N}
,
we set

\displaystyle\mathrm{in}_{(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1})}(\mathcal{I})=\mathbb{C}\cdot\{\mathrm{in}_{(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1})}(L)\ |\ L\in\mathcal{I}\}\,,

(4.18)

i.e. the ideal generated by the highest

(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1})\text{-weight}

terms in
\mathcal{I}
.
It is a
polynomial
ideal since it is written
in terms of commuting variables
(z,\xi)
.

•

The
characteristic variety
of an ideal is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{char}\!\left(\mathcal{I}\right)=\left\{(z,\xi)\ |\ p(z,\xi)=0\ \text{ for all }\ p\,\in\,\mathrm{in}_{(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1})}(\mathcal{I})\right\}\ \subset\ \mathbb{C}^{2N}\,.

(4.19)

Namely,
it the zero-set of all the polynomials in the ideal

\mathrm{in}_{(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1})}(\mathcal{I}).

The computation of
\mathrm{char}\!\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
typically requires knowing a

Gröbner basis
for
\mathcal{I}

(to be defined in the next section).

With all of these notions in place,
we can finally state

Definition 4.3
.

An ideal
\mathcal{I}
is
holonomic
when its characteristic variety
\mathrm{char}\!\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
has dimension
4
4

4

According to a result called Bernstein’s inequality,
all connected components of
\mathrm{char}\!\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
have dimension at least
N
.
An ideal is hence holonomic when its characteristic variety
has the smallest possible dimension
(the maximal dimension would be
2N
).

N
.
A
\mathcal{D}
-module
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{I}
is holonomic if and only if
\mathcal{I}
is holonomic
5
5

5

There is actually an independent definition of holonomicity for
\mathcal{D}
-modules,
depending on so-called Bernstein filtrations.
The fact that holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-modules coincide with
holonomic ideals is then a non-trivial theorem.
See
[
86
, Chapter 6]
for details.

.
The
holonomic rank
is defined as the following vector space dimension over
\mathbb{C}(z)
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\!\left(\mathcal{I}\right)

\displaystyle=\dim_{\mathbb{C}(z)}\big(\mathbb{C}(z)[\xi]\,/\,\mathbb{C}(z)[\xi]\cdot\mathrm{char}\!\left(\mathcal{I}\right)\big)\,.

The above definition of
\mathrm{rank}\!\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
relies on the following important fact:
a holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-module carries the structure of a
finite-dimensional
vector space
[
86
,
85
]
.
It follows that there exists a
basis
of differential operators for the vector space
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{I}

having exactly
\mathrm{rank}\!\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
many elements.
Quite remarkably,

\mathrm{rank}\!\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
also counts the number of independent holomorphic solutions to the system of PDEs
defined by
\mathcal{I}
.
When
\mathcal{I}
is the annihilating ideal for a FI,
then
\mathrm{rank}\!\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
is indeed the number of MIs!

4.2.1 Regular holonomic

Throughout the rest of this thesis,
when we say an ideal
\mathcal{I}
is holonomic we shall always mean
regular holonomic
.
The extra adjective "regular" signifies,
loosely speaking,
that the solutions to the PDEs dictated by
\mathcal{I}
only have mild singularities.

Suppose that
z=0
is a singularity of a solution
f(z)
in the univariate case.
It is a
regular singularity
if there exists a
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that
|z|^{k}f(z)\to 0

as
z\to 0
at a fixed angle
\mathrm{arg}(z)
in the complex plane.

The definition of regular holonomic is much more complicated in the multivariate case.
We refer to
[
85
, Section 2.4]

[
23
, Chapter 6]
for details.
A working definition for our purposes is to say that the solutions to
\mathcal{I}
admit
logarithmic series expansions near the singular hypersurfaces.
Equivalently,
the Pfaffian system born from
\mathcal{I}
,
to be discussed in Section (
4.4
),
will have at most simple poles
(higher-order poles can appear,
but they are "fake" in the sense they can be turned into simple poles via a gauge transformation).

Section 4.3
Gröbner bases

How does one compute a basis for a holonomic
\mathcal{D}
- or
\mathcal{R}
-module?
The answer,
as with almost any computational question in commutative and non-commutative algebra,
is via
Gröbner bases
.
A couple of extra concepts are needed before the definition can be stated.

•

Given two monomials
\partial^{p}
and
\partial^{q}
,
a
term order

\prec
discerns which of them is the largest one.
We refer to
[
86
]
for the technical definition,
and content ourselves with giving an example,
namely the
graded reverse lexicographic order
6
6

6

The speed with which Gröbner bases are calculated
can heavily depend on the chosen term order
(of which there are many).
The graded reverse lexicographic order tends to be the fastest.

.
Setting
|p|=p_{1}+\cdots+p_{N}
,
then

\partial^{p}\prec\partial^{q}

iff

\displaystyle|p|<|q|\quad\text{or}\quad|p|=|q|\ \text{and the \emph{last} non-zero entry of}\ q-p\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}\ \text{is \emph{negative}.}

So the monomial with the largest degree wins,
unless there is a tie,
in which case the one with the smallest variable index wins.
In the case of
N=3
variables and degree less than 3,
we have

\displaystyle 1\prec\partial_{3}\prec\partial_{2}\prec\partial_{1}\prec\partial_{3}^{2}\prec\partial_{2}\partial_{3}\prec\partial_{1}\partial_{3}\prec\partial_{2}^{2}\prec\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\prec\partial_{1}^{2}\,.

(4.20)

•

Consider an operator

L=\sum_{q\in\mathrm{supp}\left(L\right)}c_{q}(z)\,\partial^{q}\in\mathcal{R}

in normally ordered form.
Fixing a term order,
the
initial monomial
7
7

7

The similarity between this notation and
\mathrm{in}_{(u,v)}
from
(
4.15
) is not coincidental.
There is indeed a strong connection between comparing monomials according to weights

(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2N}

and according to term orders
\prec

[
85
, Chapter 1]
.

\mathrm{in}_{\prec}(L)
is defined as the largest monomial
\partial^{q}

(stripped of its rational function
c_{q}(z)\in\mathbb{C}(z)
)
w.r.t.
\prec
.

•

The
initial ideal
of
\mathcal{I}
is the ideal generated by its initial monomials:

\displaystyle\mathrm{in}_{\prec}(\mathcal{I})=\mathbb{C}\cdot\big\{\mathrm{in}_{\prec}(L)\ |\ L\in\mathcal{I}\big\}\ \subset\ \mathbb{C}[\partial_{1},\ldots,\partial_{N}]\,.

(4.21)

Definition 4.4
.

A finite subset

G=\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{M}\}\in\mathcal{R}

is called a
Gröbner basis
for the ideal
\mathcal{I}
w.r.t. the term order
\prec
iff

1.

G
generates
\mathcal{I}
,
i.e.
\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{M}\rangle=\mathcal{I}
.

2.

\mathrm{in}_{\prec}(\mathcal{I})=\langle\mathrm{in}_{\prec}(g_{1}),\ldots,\mathrm{in}_{\prec}(g_{M})\rangle.

A Gröbner basis containing the minimal number of generators
g_{i}
is called
reduced
,
and is unique for a given term order.
A monomial
\partial^{q}
is called a
standard monomial
w.r.t.
G

when it does
not
belong to
\mathrm{in}_{\prec}(\mathcal{I})
.

Absent any other methods,
the standard monomials only become known at the end of a Gröbner basis computation.

We can now answer the question posed in the very beginning of this section:
there are exactly
\mathrm{rank}\!\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
many standard monomials,
and they form an operator basis for the
\mathcal{D}
-module
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{I}
!

Gröbner basis computations are typically performed using Buchberger’s algorithm
8
8

8

Bruno Buchberger was in fact himself the inventor of Gröbner bases,
but he named them after his doctoral supervisor Wolfgang Gröbner as a sign of respect.

[
86
]
.
The intensity of such calculations grows significantly with the number of variables.
For this reason,
the main theme of the next chapter will be to develop a more efficient algorithm that avoids Gröbner bases.

Section 4.4
Pfaffian systems

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a holonomic ideal born from a system of PDEs,
and suppose
f=f(z)
is a solution to this system.
Assume a basis of standard monomials is known for the
\mathcal{R}
-module
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{I}
.
It is here denoted by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Std}=\{\partial^{q_{1}},\partial^{q_{2}},\ldots,\partial^{q_{R}}\}\,,

(4.22)

where
R:=\mathrm{rank}\!\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
and each
q_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}_{\geq 0}
.
Without loss of generality,
we may set
\mathrm{Std}_{1}=1
.
A basis of
functions
for the PDE solution space is then given by the action of each
standard monomial on
f
:

\displaystyle e=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{Std}_{1}\bullet f=f\\
\mathrm{Std}_{2}\bullet f\\
\vdots\\
\mathrm{Std}_{R}\bullet f\end{array}\right]\,.

We are interested in finding a set of first-order PDEs satisfied by
e
.
To this end,
consider the Weyl algebra element
\partial_{i}\mathrm{Std}_{j}
.
Since the standard monomials form a basis for
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{I}
,
it must be possible to write

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\mathrm{Std}_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{R}p_{ij}^{k}(z)\,\mathrm{Std}_{k}\quad\text{mod}\ \mathcal{I}\,,\quad p_{ij}^{k}(z)\in\mathbb{C}(z)\,.

(4.27)

The notation
"\text{mod}\ \mathcal{I}"
means that this equation holds modulo the ideal
\mathcal{I}
,
in the sense of (
4.11
).
Most of the time we leave the
"\text{mod}\ \mathcal{I}"
as implicit.

Combining (
4.4
) with (
4.27
),
we then have

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\bullet e=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}(\partial_{i}\mathrm{Std}_{1})\bullet f\\
(\partial_{i}\mathrm{Std}_{2})\bullet f\\
\vdots\\
(\partial_{i}\mathrm{Std}_{R})\bullet f\end{array}\right]=P_{i}(z)\cdot e\quad\text{mod}\ \mathcal{I}\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,,

where
P_{i}(z)
is an
R\times R
matrix whose
(j,k)
th entry equals the rational function
p_{ij}^{k}(z)
.
The set of first-order PDEs (
4.4
) is dubbed a
Pfaffian system
.
By manipulating the standard monomials,
it can be shown
[
86
]
that the
Pfaffian matrices

P_{i}
satisfy the

integrability condition

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\bullet P_{j}+P_{i}\cdot P_{j}=\partial_{j}\bullet P_{i}+P_{j}\cdot P_{i}\,.

(4.32)

The next chapter will be dedicated to deriving Pfaffian systems from the point of view of
\mathcal{D}
-modules.
Such a calculation requires two steps:

1.

Find a set of standard monomials
\mathrm{Std}
.

2.

Derive the decomposition (
4.27
).

Both steps are possible when a Gröbner basis is known
(the second step is then performed via an algorithm analogous to that of polynomial division).
However,
as mentioned earlier,
we shall seek to avoid Gröbner bases as their computation often requires huge resources.
As an aside,
if a Pfaffian system is known by other means,
then one can immediately construct a Gröbner basis too
[
82
, Appendix B]
.

We end this section with the following observation:
if
f
were a single FI,
then (
4.4
) is exactly the kind of Pfaffian systems
that we studied in
Part I
of this thesis!
The decomposition of
\partial_{i}\mathrm{Std}_{j}
in (
4.27
) is then analogous to an IBP reduction.

Chapter 5
Macaulay Matrices

Based on
[
82
]
,
this chapter presents a novel algorithm for the computation of Pfaffian systems.
It avoids the use of Gröbner bases in favor of linear algebra methods,
making it quite efficient.

Throughout this chapter we assume that

a set of standard monomials
\mathrm{Std}=\{\partial^{q_{i}}\}_{i=1,\ldots,R}
is given,

(5.1)

where the cardinality
|\mathrm{Std}|=R
equals the holonomic rank of a given left ideal
\mathcal{I}
.
While this is a strong assumption,
it will actually be fulfilled for the so-called GKZ system that we study in Chapter (
6
).

Section 5.1
From Pfaffian to Macaulay matrix

Let

\mathcal{I}=\langle d_{1},\ldots,d_{D}\rangle

be a holonomic ideal generated by a collection of
D
operators
d_{i}\in\mathcal{R}
.
In this section we suppose to know the Pfaffian matrices
P_{i}
associated to
\mathcal{I}
.
It holds that

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\mathrm{Std}_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{R}(P_{i})_{jk}\,\mathrm{Std}_{k}\quad\text{in}\quad\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{I}\,.

(5.2)

This expression can be lifted from the quotient ring
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{I}
to the whole ring
\mathcal{R}
by writing

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\mathrm{Std}_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{R}(P_{i})_{jk}\,\mathrm{Std}_{k}\hskip 5.69046pt+\hskip 5.69046pt\sum_{k=1}^{D}\Delta_{jk}\,d_{k}\quad\text{in}\quad\mathcal{R}\,,

(5.3)

where each
\Delta_{jk}\in\mathcal{R}
.
Our short-term goal is to aptly rewrite the second sum above,
which will culminate in (
5.7
).
We warn that this will require some index gymnastics,
but the final result does simplify.
To ease the notation in intermediate expressions,
we shall keep the index
i
fixed
(otherwise we would have to write
\Delta_{ijk}
etc.).
The index
i
will be reinstated at the very end.

To start,
we bring the operator
\Delta_{jk}
into normally ordered form:

\displaystyle\Delta_{jk}\hskip 5.69046pt=\sum_{q\,\in\,\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathrm{Der}\right)}\delta_{jkq}(z)\,\partial^{q}\,,\quad\partial^{q}\in\mathrm{Der}\,,

(5.4)

where

\delta_{jkq}\in\mathbb{C}(z)^{R\times D\times|\mathrm{Der}|}

is a newly introduced rank-3 tensor of rational functions.
The set
\mathrm{Der}
contains all the derivative monomials appearing on the RHS.

If we substitute the operators
\Delta_{jk}
from
(
5.4
) into the second sum of (
5.3
),
then the monomials
\partial^{q}\in\mathrm{Der}
will act on the generators
d_{i}

(which potentially contain rational functions in
z
as prefactors).
In normally ordered form,
this action is written as

\displaystyle\partial^{q}\,d_{k}=\sum_{p\,\in\,\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathrm{Mons}\right)}M_{qkp}(z)\,\partial^{p}\,,\quad\partial^{p}\in\mathrm{Mons}\,.

(5.5)

Here we introduced a rank-3 tensor of rational functions

M_{qkp}\in\mathbb{C}(z)^{|\mathrm{Der}|\times D\times|\mathrm{Mons}|}

and defined a set
\mathrm{Mons}
whose elements consists of all the monomials
\partial^{p}
appearing on the RHS.
Upon concatenation of the first two indices
q
and
k
,
the rank-3 tensor
M_{qkp}
turns into the
Macaulay matrix

[
87
]

\displaystyle M_{op}(z):=M_{(qk)p}(z)\in\mathbb{C}^{(|\mathrm{Der}|\cdot D)\times|\mathrm{Mons}|}\,.

(5.6)

The combined index
o=(qk)=(kq)
runs over the Cartesian product of sets

\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathrm{Der}\right)\times\{1,\ldots,D\}.

Plugging equations
(
5.4
), (
5.5
) and (
5.6
)
into the second sum of (
5.3
),
this term now takes the form of a triple matrix product:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{D}\Delta_{jk}\,d_{k}\quad=\quad\sum_{o,\,p}\delta_{jo}(z)\,M_{op}(z)\,\partial^{p}\quad=\quad\big(C\cdot M\cdot\mathrm{Mons}\big)_{j}\,.

(5.7)

C\in\mathbb{C}(z)^{R\times(|\mathrm{Der}|\cdot D)}

stands for the coefficient matrix

C_{jo}=\delta_{j(kq)}(z),

when
\mathrm{Der}
is regarded as a column vector in (
5.4
).
The set
\mathrm{Mons}
is here also regarded as a column vector.

Some of the monomials in
\mathrm{Mons}
may be standard,
but all need not be.
The non-standard monomials in
\mathrm{Mons}
shall henceforth be called
exterior monomials
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ext}=\mathrm{Mons}\setminus\mathrm{Std}\,.

(5.8)

The disjoint union

\displaystyle\mathrm{Mons}=\mathrm{Ext}\ \sqcup\ \mathrm{Std}

(5.9)

then induces a partition on the columns of the Macaulay matrix:

\displaystyle M=\Big[\ M_{\mathrm{Ext}}

\displaystyle\ \Big|\ M_{\mathrm{Std}}\ \Big]\,,\quad\text{with}\quad

(5.10)

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{Ext}}\in\mathbb{C}(z)^{(|\mathrm{Der}|\cdot D)\times|\mathrm{Ext}|}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad M_{\mathrm{Std}}\in\mathbb{C}(z)^{(|\mathrm{Der}|\cdot D)\times|\mathrm{Std}|}

Thinking of the set
\mathrm{Std}
as a column vector,
we further decompose the LHS of (
5.3
) as

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\mathrm{Std}\quad=\quad\Big[\ C_{\mathrm{Ext}}\ \Big|\ C_{\mathrm{Std}}\ \Big]\cdot\mathrm{Mons}\quad=\quad C_{\mathrm{Ext}}\cdot\mathrm{Ext}\hskip 5.69046pt+\hskip 5.69046ptC_{\mathrm{Std}}\cdot\mathrm{Std}\,,\quad\text{with}\quad

(5.11)

\displaystyle\hskip 62.59596ptC_{\mathrm{Ext}}\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathrm{Std}|\times|\mathrm{Ext}|}\qquad\text{and}\qquad C_{\mathrm{Std}}\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathrm{Std}|\times|\mathrm{Std}|}\,.

In other words,

C_{\mathrm{Ext}}
and
C_{\mathrm{Std}}
are two binary matrices that encode whether the monomials in

\partial_{i}\mathrm{Std}

are exterior or standard.

Finally,
inserting equations
(
5.7
), (
5.10
) and (
5.11
)
into the Pfaffian equation (
5.3
),
we arrive at

\displaystyle(C_{\mathrm{Ext}}-C\cdot M_{\mathrm{Ext}})\hskip 5.69046pt+\hskip 5.69046pt(C_{\mathrm{Std}}-C\cdot M_{\mathrm{Std}})\quad=\quad P_{i}\cdot\mathrm{Std}\,.

(5.12)

But,
because
\mathrm{Ext}
and
\mathrm{Std}
are linearly independent in
\mathcal{R}
,
this is equivalent to the system

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{Ext}}^{(i)}-C^{(i)}\cdot M_{\mathrm{Ext}}

\displaystyle=0

(5.13)

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{Std}}^{(i)}-C^{(i)}\cdot M_{\mathrm{Std}}

\displaystyle=P_{i}\,.

(5.14)

Here we also reinstated the dependence on the index
i
,
as promised earlier in this section.
These two matrix equations are central to the algorithm for computing Pfaffian matrices
that we propose in the next section.

Section 5.2
From Macaulay to Pfaffian matrix

Now we reverse the logic in the derivation leading to equations
(
5.13
) and (
5.14
).
Although we started by assuming to know the Pfaffian system (
5.2
),
we now regard it as the goal.
Supposing that

C_{\bullet}^{(i)}\ \text{and}\ M_{\bullet}

in (
5.13
) and (
5.14
) are easily calculable (
\bullet=\mathrm{Ext},\,\mathrm{Std}
),
then
C^{(i)}
and
P_{i}
are the only unknowns.
The purported strategy is this:

\displaystyle\text{solve \eqref{MM_Ext_eq} for the rational matrix $C^{(i)}$}\implies\text{insert $C^{(i)}$ into \eqref{MM_Std_eq} to obtain $P_{i}$.}

Let us formalize this strategy into a proper algorithm.
To begin,
define the set of all derivative monomials bounded by a degree
Q\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Der}_{Q}=\{\partial^{q}\}_{|q|\leq Q}\,.

(5.15)

In analogy with
\mathrm{Mons}
from (
5.5
),
we let
\mathrm{Mons}_{Q}
denote the derivative monomials appearing in the normally order form of the set

\big\{\partial^{q}\,d_{k}\big\}_{\partial^{q}\,\in\,\mathrm{Der}_{Q}}^{k=1,\ldots,D}.

The Macaulay matrix
M_{Q}
of degree
Q
is defined as in (
5.6
),
but with
\mathrm{Der}
and
\mathrm{Mons}
replaced with
\mathrm{Der}_{Q}
and
\mathrm{Mons}_{Q}
.
It thus holds that

\displaystyle\Big\{\partial^{q}\,d_{k}\Big\}_{\partial^{q}\,\in\,\mathrm{Der}_{Q}}^{k=1,\ldots,D}\quad

\displaystyle=\quad\sum_{p\,\in\,\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathrm{Mons}_{Q}\right)}(M_{Q})_{(qk)p}\,\partial^{p}

(5.16)

\displaystyle=\quad M_{Q}\cdot\mathrm{Mons}_{Q}\,.

(5.17)

The set on the LHS of (
5.16
)
is here viewed as a column vector of operators with rational function coefficients,
and
M_{Q}=M_{Q}(z)
is the coefficient matrix of the monomial derivatives
\mathrm{Mons}_{Q}
.
The set
\mathrm{Der}_{Q}
can be thought of as "seeding" the Macaulay matrix.

The idea is now to adjust the degree
Q
until (
5.13
) admits a solution for
C^{(i)}
:

Input
:
Standard monomials
\mathrm{Std}
,
generators
\{d_{1},\ldots d_{D}\}
of a holonomic ideal
\mathcal{I}
,
direction
i
.

Output
:
Pfaffian matrix
P_{i}
.

1:

Q=0

2:

M=M_{0}=\big[\ M_{\mathrm{Ext}}\ \big|\ M_{\mathrm{Std}}\ \big]

3:

\mathrm{Mons}=\mathrm{Mons}_{0}

4:

while

C_{\mathrm{Ext}}^{(i)}=C^{(i)}\cdot M_{\mathrm{Ext}}

is not solvable w.r.t.
C^{(i)}

do

5:

Q++

6:

M\leftarrow M_{Q}

7:

\mathrm{Mons}\leftarrow\mathrm{Mons}_{Q}

8:

end

while

9:

Solve
C_{\mathrm{Ext}}^{(i)}=C^{(i)}\cdot M_{\mathrm{Ext}}
for
C^{(i)}

10:

return

P_{i}=C_{\mathrm{Std}}^{(i)}-C^{(i)}\cdot M_{\mathrm{Std}}

Algorithm 1

: Pfaffian matrix by the Macaulay matrix method

There are a couple of immediate optimizations to this algorithm.
First,
notice that (
5.13
) has a solution when the rows of
C_{\mathrm{Ext}}^{(i)}

lie in the row space of
M_{\mathrm{Ext}}
.
The condition in the
while
statement in line 4 of Algorithm (
1
)
then translates into the requirement

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}M_{\mathrm{Ext}}\\
\hline\cr C_{\mathrm{Ext}}^{(i)}\end{array}\right]=\mathrm{rank}\big[M_{\mathrm{Ext}}\big]\,.

It is quick to check this equality by setting every parameter
in the problem to a number in
\mathbb{Q}
or a finite field

\mathbb{F}_{p}=\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}

where
p
is prime.

Second,
to solve the system (
5.13
),
it often suffices to pick only a subset of the rows in
M_{\mathrm{Ext}}
.
The reason is that some rows may be linearly dependent.
Such a subset can be identified by replacing parameters with numbers and row reducing.

Lastly,
since every matrix in the problem is defined over the rational function field
\mathbb{C}(z)
,
it is vastly more efficient to employ
rational reconstruction

algorithms rather than Gaussian elimination.
These algorithms reconstruct analytic expressions
for rational functions by repeated evaluations over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
One need not even reconstruct the intermediate "dummy matrix"
C^{(i)}
,
as we are ultimately only interested in the Pfaffian matrix
P_{i}
.
I.e.,
rational reconstruction can be postponed until all the matrix operations have been carried out in
(
5.14
).
This thesis employs the rational reconstruction software
FiniteFlow

[
88
]
,
because it allows one to easily chain together several operations
(such as matrix multiplication and addition of matrices),
with reconstruction postponed until the final step.
See
[
89
,
90
,
91
,
92
]
for alternative implementations.

Example 5.1.
Consider the holonomic ideal

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}=\langle z\partial-\beta\rangle\,,

(5.20)

where

z_{1}=z,\,\partial_{1}=\partial

and
\beta\in\mathbb{C}
is a parameter.
Although this example is too simple for an interesting application of the Macaulay matrix method,
we include it to showcase the many definitions in the above text.

To build the Macaulay matrix,
we must first choose the seeds
\mathrm{Der}_{Q}
defined in (
5.16
).
It turns out that
Q=0
is a sufficient degree,
but for the sake of illustration let us pick
Q=1
.
Then

\mathrm{Der}_{Q}=\{1,\partial\}.

Following (
5.16
),
we apply the monomials in
\mathrm{Der}_{Q}
to the generator of
\mathcal{I}
:

\displaystyle 1(z\partial-\beta)=z\partial-\beta

(5.21)

\displaystyle\partial(z\partial-\beta)=\partial z\partial-\beta\partial=(1+z\partial)\partial-\beta\partial=z\partial^{2}+(1-\beta)\partial\,.

(5.22)

The set
\mathrm{Mons}_{Q}
is formed from all the derivative monomials appearing above:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Mons}_{Q}=\{\partial^{2},\partial,1\}=\mathrm{Ext}\ \sqcup\ \mathrm{Std}=\{\partial^{2},\partial\}\ \sqcup\ \{1\}\,.

(5.23)

The Macaulay matrix can now be constructed as

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{c}1(z\partial-\beta)\\
\partial(z\partial-\beta)\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}M_{\mathrm{Ext}}&M_{\mathrm{Std}}\end{array}\right]\cdot\mathrm{Mons}_{Q}

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc|c}0&z&-\beta\\
z&1-\beta&0\end{array}\right]\cdot\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\partial^{2}\\
\partial\\
1\end{array}\right]\,.

Next we build the two matrices
C_{\mathrm{Ext}}
and
C_{\mathrm{Std}}
as per (
5.11
):

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Std}

\displaystyle=\partial\big[1\big]=\big[\partial\big]

(5.32)

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}C_{\mathrm{Ext}}&C_{\mathrm{Std}}\end{array}\right]\cdot\mathrm{Mons}_{Q}

\displaystyle=C_{\mathrm{Ext}}\cdot\mathrm{Ext}\hskip 5.69046pt+\hskip 5.69046ptC_{\mathrm{Std}}\cdot\mathrm{Std}

(5.34)

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\end{array}\right]\cdot\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\partial^{2}\\
\partial\end{array}\right]\hskip 5.69046pt+\hskip 5.69046pt\big[0\big]\cdot\big[1\big]\,.

At this point we have all ingredients present in (
5.13
) and (
5.14
).
The first equation is

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{Ext}}-C\cdot M_{\mathrm{Ext}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\end{array}\right]-\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}C_{11}&C_{12}\end{array}\right]\cdot\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&z\\
z&1-\beta\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\end{array}\right]\,.

Its solution is

C=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{z}&0\end{array}\right]\,.

Inserting this into (
5.14
),
we get

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{Std}}-C\cdot M_{\mathrm{Std}}=\big[0\big]-\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{z}&0\end{array}\right]\cdot\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-\beta\\
0\end{array}\right]=\left[\frac{\beta}{z}\right]=P\,,

where
P
is the Pfaffian matrix
(albeit just a scalar in this case).
In other words,
we have derived that

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Std}=P\cdot\mathrm{Std}\implies\big[\partial\big]=\left[\frac{\beta}{z}\right]\,,

(5.46)

which is exactly the relation dictated by the ideal (
5.20
)!
So we have obtained a trivial result,
but we can at least be satisfied that everything is self-consistent.

\blacksquare

Example 5.2.

Let

\mathcal{I}=\langle d_{1},d_{2},d_{3},d_{4}\rangle

be the ideal generated by

\displaystyle d_{1}

\displaystyle=z_{1}\partial_{1}+z_{2}\partial_{2}+z_{3}\partial_{3}+z_{4}\partial_{4}-\beta_{1}

\displaystyle d_{2}

\displaystyle=z_{2}\partial_{2}+z_{4}\partial_{4}-\beta_{2}

(5.47)

\displaystyle d_{3}

\displaystyle=z_{3}\partial_{3}+z_{4}\partial_{4}-\beta_{3}

\displaystyle d_{4}

\displaystyle=\partial_{1}\partial_{4}-\partial_{2}\partial_{3}\,.

In Section (
6.5
),
we show that this is the annihilating ideal for Gauss’
{}_{2}F_{1}
hypergeometric function.
The
\mathcal{D}
-module
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{I}
turns out to have a basis of standard monomials given by

\mathrm{Std}=\{\partial_{4},1\}

(later on, in Section (
6.4
), we explain how to easily find such a basis).
The goal of this example is to find the Pfaffian system in direction
z_{4}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{4}\mathrm{Std}=\partial_{4}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{4}\\
1\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{4}^{2}\\
\partial_{4}\end{array}\right]=P_{4}\cdot\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{4}\\
1\end{array}\right]\,.

To compute
P_{4}
,
we need to resolve the higher-order monomial
\partial_{4}^{2}
in terms of
\partial_{4}
and
1
.
But
\partial_{4}^{2}
does not appear in the generators
d_{i}
from (
5.2
),
so the seeding set
\mathrm{Der}_{Q=0}=\{1\}
is insufficient.
It turns out that the degree
Q=1
monomials

\mathrm{Der}_{1}=\{1,\partial_{1},\partial_{2},\partial_{3},\partial_{4}\}

lead to a solvable system.

It is rather tedious to compute the Macaulay matrix by hand given this seed.
Instead,
we use the newly developed package
mt_mm
written by
Nobuki Takayama and Saiei J. Matsubara-Heo in the CAS called
asir
.

asir
can be built from source by cloning the
git
repository

https://github.com/openxm-org/OpenXM
.
Source code and documentation for
mt_mm
can be found in the directories

OpenXM/src/asir-contrib/packages/src/mt_mm
OpenXM/doc/asir-contrib/en.

For the case at hand,
the Macaulay matrix is obtained by entering the following commands into
asir
1
1

1

We call the generators
Di
rather than
di

since most lower-case objects are name-protected in
asir
.

:

⬇

D1

=

z1
*
dz1

+

z2
*
dz2

+

z3
*
dz3

+

z4
*
dz4

-

b1
;

D2

=

z2
*
dz2

+

z4
*
dz4

-

b2
;

D3

=

z3
*
dz3

+

z4
*
dz4

-

b3
;

D4

=

dz1
*
dz4

-

dz2
*
dz3
;

Ideal

=

[
D1
,

D2
,

D3
,

D4
];

Std

=

[
dz4
,

1];

mt_mm
.
find_macaulay
(
Ideal
,

Std
,

[
z1
,

z2
,

z3
,

z4
]);
