\displaystyle f_{\beta}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=\int_{(0,\infty)^{2}}(z_{1}x_{1}+z_{2}x_{2}+z_{3}x_{1}x_{2})^{\beta_{0}}x_{1}^{-\beta_{1}}x_{2}^{-\beta_{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x}\,.

(8.9)

The annihilating ideal
\mathcal{I}=H_{A}(\beta)
is solely generated by the homogeneity operators

\displaystyle E_{0}

\displaystyle=z_{1}\partial_{1}+z_{2}\partial_{2}+z_{3}\partial_{3}-\beta_{0}

\displaystyle E_{1}

\displaystyle=z_{1}\partial_{1}+z_{3}\partial_{3}-\beta_{1}

(8.10)

\displaystyle E_{2}

\displaystyle=z_{2}\partial_{2}+z_{3}\partial_{3}-\beta_{2}\,.

We are interested in the annihilating ideal for the function
f_{\beta}(1,1,z_{3})
.
By an affine linear coordinate change

\{z_{1}\to z_{1}+1\,,z_{2}\to z_{2}+1\,,z_{3}\to z_{3}\},

we hence have the affine subspace given by

Y^{\prime}=\{z_{1}=z_{2}=0\}.

A variant of the restriction algorithm presented in Oaku’s seminal work
[
148
]

has been implemented in the
asir
package
nk_restriction

[
149
]
.
Inputting the data above,
one finds the annihilating ideal

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=\langle-z_{3}\partial_{3}-\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}+\beta_{3}\rangle\,.

(8.11)

One can check that this indeed annihilates the function

\displaystyle f_{\beta}(1,1,z_{3})=\frac{\Gamma(-\beta_{0}+\beta_{1})\Gamma(-\beta_{0}+\beta_{2})\Gamma(\beta_{0}-\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})}{\Gamma(-\beta_{0})}z_{3}^{-\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}}\,.

(8.12)

\blacksquare

The discussion above was directed at
\mathcal{D}
-modules,
i.e. when the action is given by operators with polynomial coefficient functions.
We are,
more broadly,
interested in
\mathcal{R}
-modules having rational function coefficients,
because this is what appears in Pfaffian systems.
An
\mathcal{R}
-module naturally arises from a given
\mathcal{D}
-module
\mathcal{M}
by taking a tensor product:

\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{Y}}\mathcal{M}.

Definition 8.2
.

The
rational restriction
of a
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module
\mathcal{M}
onto
Y^{\prime}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{Y^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{Y^{\prime}}}\iota^{*}\mathcal{M}\,,

(8.13)

where
\iota^{*}\mathcal{M}
is the restriction module (
8.8
).

The restriction algorithms presented in this chapter will output Pfaffian systems
that live in the space (
8.13
).

8.1.3 Normal form

Let
\partial_{i}\bullet e(z)=P_{i}\cdot e(z)
be a Pfaffian system that is
regular singular

(cf. Section (
4.2.1
)).
Loosely speaking,
this means that the solutions vector
e(z)
is free of essential singularities such

\exp(1/z)

at
z=0
.
It is expected that FIs fall into this category,
as the poles in
\epsilon
-factorized Pfaffian systems always appear to be simple.

We focus on a single pole of the system,
which can be written as
Y^{\prime}=\{z_{1}=0\}
by a change of coordinates.
Theorems by Deligne
[
150
,
151
]
ensure
that the Pfaffian system can be brought into
normal form
w.r.t.
Y^{\prime}
.
Namely,
there exists another set of Pfaffian matrices
\widetilde{P}_{i}(z)
such that

1.

\widetilde{P}_{1}
has a simple pole on
Y^{\prime}

and

\{\widetilde{P}_{2},\ldots,\widetilde{P}_{N}\}
are finite on
Y^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=-1}^{\infty}\widetilde{P}_{1,n}(z^{\prime})z_{1}^{n}\,,\quad\text{and}

(8.14)

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}_{i}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\widetilde{P}_{i,n}(z^{\prime})z_{1}^{n}\,,\hskip 8.5359pt\quad\text{for}\quad i=2,\ldots,N\,,

(8.15)

where the truncated list of variables is

\displaystyle z^{\prime}=(z_{2},\ldots,z_{N})\,.

(8.16)

Note that the expansion of
\widetilde{P}_{1}
starts at
n=-1
,
and the expansions for
\widetilde{P}_{i\,=\,2,\ldots,N}
start at
n=0
.

2.

The spectrum of the
residue matrix

\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
is
non-resonant
,
meaning that
1) no two distinct eigenvalues of
\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
have an integral difference,
and
2) the only integral eigenvalue, if it exists, is
0
.

The matrices
\widetilde{P}_{i}(z)
are related to the original ones
P_{i}(z)
via a gauge transformation.
By the work of Moser
[
152
]
,
and later Barkatou et al.
[
153
,
154
]
,
there exist explicit
Moser reduction
algorithms for constructing such a gauge transformation
(see
[
83
, Appendix B]
for a review).
Moser reduction has been implemented in software such as

Fuchsia

[
155
]
and
Isolde

[
156
]
.

It can be computationally expensive to enforce a non-resonant spectrum on a Pfaffian system,
as this step of the algorithm requires the calculation of Jordan decompositions
1
1

1

In particular,
one is required to know the sizes of the Jordan cells
[
83
, Appendix B.2]
.

.
However,
if we only seek to perform Moser reduction w.r.t. a single pole
z_{1}=0
rather than many,
then the algorithm can readily be applied to rather large systems.
Moreover,
since the Jordan decomposed matrix does not depend on the
z
-variables

[
157
, Theorem 12.1]
,
it is possible speed up the computation by fixing the variables to generic numbers.

Section 8.2
Restriction at the level of Pfaffian systems

In this section,
we present a restriction algorithm which takes a Pfaffian system on
Y=\mathbb{A}^{N}
as input
and outputs a new Pfaffian system that holds true on an affine subspace
Y^{\prime}\subset Y
.
As has been mentioned,
the original motivation was to calculate restrictions of GKZ systems.
However,
the algorithm turns out to work for any regular holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-module.
So it can,
rather serendipitously,
also be applied to Pfaffian systems for FIs obtained by conventional means,
such as IBPs in momentum space.

As it will turn out,
the algorithm can also be used to compute logarithmic series solutions to Pfaffian systems.
This is helpful for calculating asymptotic expansions for FIs,
such as threshold and small-mass expansions.

8.2.1 Restriction of a logarithmic connection

Suppose we are given a Pfaffian system on the "big" space
Y
.
We want to restrict it onto a "smaller" space
Y^{\prime}=\{z_{1}=0\}
.
Further,

z_{1}=0
should constitute a pole in the Pfaffian system.
There might poles elsewhere on
Y
,
but we imagine working "locally" around this single pole
2
2

2

The notion of ”local” is here meant w.r.t. the Zariski topology.
So to be rigorous,
we ought to formulate everything in terms of
Zariski open subsets
Y_{0}
of
Y
as in
[
83
, Section 3]
.
Many such mathematical details are neglected here in order to take the shortest
path towards defining the practical algorithms.

.
Assume that the Pfaffian system is in normal form w.r.t.
Y^{\prime}

(cf. Section (
8.1.3
)).
Then it is possible to write it in a peculiar way:

\displaystyle z_{1}\partial_{1}\bullet e(z)

\displaystyle=P_{1}\cdot e(z)

(8.17)

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\bullet e(z)

\displaystyle=P_{i}\cdot e(z)\,,\quad i=2,\ldots,N\,,

(8.18)

where
e(z)
is an
R
-dimensional vector of functions.
We would like to use this Pfaffian system to construct a certain

\mathcal{D}
-module
\mathcal{M}
consisting of
regular
functions.
The action of the derivative
\partial_{i}
should be well-defined on such a space,
in the sense of not creating unwanted singularities.
We have therefore guarded ourselves in two ways to ensure well-definedness:
1) by working locally around
Y^{\prime}
we can thereby ignore singularities elsewhere,
and 2) by moving
1/z_{1}
from the RHS of (
8.17
) to get
z_{1}\partial_{1}
on the LHS.
We are thence motivated to define a subring of
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{Y}(\log Y^{\prime})=\mathbb{C}\langle z_{1},\ldots,z_{N},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}z_{1}\partial_{1}},\partial_{2},\ldots,\partial_{N}\rangle\,,

(8.19)

and the abelian group

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\big\{R\text{-dimensional vectors of functions on}\ Y\ \text{that are regular near}\ Y^{\prime}\big\}\,.

(8.20)

The action of the ring
\mathcal{D}_{Y}(\log Y^{\prime})
onto
\mathcal{M}
is well-defined.
The generators
z_{i}
act by scalar multiplication,
and the generators
\{z_{1}\partial_{1},\partial_{2},\ldots,\partial_{N}\}
act according to the RHSs of the
Pfaffian system (
8.17
)-(
8.17
).
This turns
\mathcal{M}
into a
\mathcal{D}_{Y}(\log Y^{\prime})
-module.
In technical jargon,
such an
\mathcal{M}
is called a
logarithmic connection

[
110
, Section 11.4]

[
23
, Section 5.2.2]
.
We now proceed to study the rational restriction

\mathcal{R}_{Y^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{Y^{\prime}}}\iota^{*}\mathcal{M}

of the logarithmic connection
\mathcal{M}
.

To start,
let
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
be the residue matrix
(cf. (
8.14
))
associated to
P_{1}(z)
defined in (
8.17
),
where
z^{\prime}=(z_{2},\ldots,z_{N})
.
The entries of
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
live in the field
\mathcal{K}_{Y^{\prime}}
consisting of rational functions on
Y^{\prime}
.
The
R
rows of
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
generate a subspace of
\mathcal{K}_{Y^{\prime}}^{R}
which we call
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
.
The following quotient space is important for our construction of the rational restriction:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{K}_{Y^{\prime}}^{R}\,/\,\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\,.

(8.21)

As a set,
this consists of
R
-dimensional vectors of rational functions on
Y^{\prime}
,
and we identify two vectors if they differ by another vector lying in the row space of
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
.

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
can actually be turned into an
\mathcal{R}_{Y^{\prime}}
-module.
The action of the derivatives
\{\partial_{2},\ldots,\partial_{N}\}
on an

R
-dimensional equivalence class
[m]\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\bullet[m]=P_{i,0}(z^{\prime})\cdot[m]\quad\text{for}\quad i=2,\ldots,N\,,

(8.22)

where the matrices
P_{i,0}(z^{\prime})
come from the leading terms in the
z_{1}
-expansion (
8.15
).

With all of this build-up,
we can finally state the following crucial isomorphism of
\mathcal{R}_{Y^{\prime}}
-modules
3
3

3

The formula (
8.23
)
shows the rational restriction of
\mathcal{M}
when it equals a space of functions as a set.
Recalling the discussion from Section (
4.4
),
this isomorphism can also be employed when
\mathcal{M}
is of the form

\mathcal{D}_{Y}/\mathcal{I}

for some annihilating ideal of differential operators
\mathcal{I}
.

:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{Y^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{Y^{\prime}}}\iota^{*}\mathcal{M}\ \simeq\ \mathcal{M}^{\prime}\,.

(8.23)

We refer to
[
83
, Proposition 3.4]
for many more mathematical details as well as a proof.

The takeaway message up until now is this.
We are given some Pfaffian matrices
\{P_{1}(z),\ldots,P_{N}(z)\}
on
Y
which define
\mathcal{M}
.
We would like to know the Pfaffian matrices
\{Q_{2}(z^{\prime}),\ldots,Q_{N}(z^{\prime})\}
on the smaller space
Y^{\prime}
.
The matrices
Q_{i}(z^{\prime})
are associated to the rational restriction module of
\mathcal{M}
.
Because of the isomorphism (
8.23
),
the matrices
Q_{i}(z^{\prime})
are equally well associated to the quotient space
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.
Fortunately,
we have a lot of information about
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
:
it is completely specified by the residue matrix
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
!
In the following subsection,
we use this information to provide an explicit formula for the Pfaffian matrices
Q_{i}(z^{\prime})
.

8.2.2 Constructing the restricted Pfaffian system

Let
R^{\prime}
denote the holonomic rank of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.
In general
R^{\prime}<R
,
i.e. the holonomic rank drops when we fix
z
-variables to special values.
Given an element
m\in\mathcal{K}^{R}_{Y^{\prime}}
,
we write
[m]
for its equivalence class in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

We define three sets of Pfaffian systems:

1.

We suppose the existence of a
special
basis

f=f(z^{\prime})=\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{R}\}

obeying the Pfaffian system

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\bullet f=\widetilde{Q}_{i}(z^{\prime})\cdot f\quad\text{for}\quad i=2,\ldots,N\,.

(8.24)

We do not yet know
f
nor
\widetilde{Q}_{i}(z^{\prime})
.

2.

The basis
f
is special for the following reason:
we assume that

\{f_{R^{\prime}+1},\ldots,f_{R}\}

is a basis of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
.
The set

[f]=\{[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{R^{\prime}}]\}

is then a basis for
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
,
with an associated Pfaffian system

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\bullet[f]=Q_{i}(z^{\prime})\cdot[f]\quad\text{for}\quad i=2,\ldots,N\,.

(8.25)

By stipulation,
the first
R^{\prime}\times R^{\prime}
block of
\widetilde{Q}_{i}(z^{\prime})
equals
Q_{i}(z^{\prime})
.
We do not yet know
[f]
nor
Q_{i}(z^{\prime})
.

3.

Let

e=e(z^{\prime})=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{R}\}

be the basis associated to the Pfaffian system

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\bullet e=P_{i,0}(z^{\prime})\cdot e\quad\text{for}\quad i=2,\ldots,N\,.

(8.26)

The matrices
P_{i,0}(z^{\prime})

are
assumed to be
known via the formula (
8.15
).
In contrast to
\widetilde{Q}_{i}(z^{\prime})
,
the matrices
P_{i,0}(z^{\prime})
have no nice block structure.

We are hence in the following situation:
we know
P_{i,0}(z^{\prime})
(for the basis
e
),
but we do not know
\widetilde{Q}_{i}(z^{\prime})
(for the special basis
f
).
We would like to know
\widetilde{Q}_{i}(z^{\prime})
,
because then we would automatically get the Pfaffian matrices
Q_{i}(z^{\prime})

for the rational restriction module
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.
We shall achieve this goal by building a gauge transformation mapping
e
to the special basis
f
.
This is done by through three new matrices
\{\mathsf{B},\mathsf{R},\mathsf{M}\}
,
which we define now.

•

Recall the basis

[f]=\{[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{R^{\prime}}]\}

of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.
Each representative of these equivalence classes can be expanded in the basis
e
via some

R^{\prime}\times R

matrix
\mathsf{B}
having entries in
\mathcal{K}_{Y^{\prime}}
:

\displaystyle f_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{R}\mathsf{B}_{ij}\,e_{j}\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,\ldots,R^{\prime}\,.

(8.27)

The entries of
\mathsf{B}
are not known at this point.

•

By the definition of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
,
the residue matrix
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
has exactly
R-R^{\prime}
independent rows.
Define an
(R-R^{\prime})\times R
matrix by

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}=\texttt{RowReduce}\!\left[P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})\right]\,,

(8.28)

where
RowReduce
includes the deletion of zero-rows.
Then

\displaystyle f_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{R}\mathsf{R}_{ij}\,e_{j}\quad\text{for}\quad i=R^{\prime}+1,\ldots,R

(8.29)

forms a basis of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
.

•

Aligning the row of
\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{R}
produces an invertible
R\times R
matrix
\mathsf{M}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0.5742,0.0938,0.0938}\mathsf{B}}\\
\hline\cr{\color[rgb]{0.3242,0.0156,0.4766}\mathsf{R}}\end{array}\right]=\vbox{\hbox{ \includegraphics[scale]{Figs/M_mat.pdf} }}\ .

Note that
\mathsf{R}
is known by stipulation,
and the matrix
\mathsf{B}
can be anything as long as
\mathsf{M}
becomes invertible.
We shall give a simple recipe for generating
\mathsf{B}
later on.

In vector notation,
we now have that
f=\mathsf{M}\cdot e
.
This means that the unknown matrices
\widetilde{Q}_{i}(z^{\prime})

(associated to
f
)
are obtained from the known matrices
P_{i,0}(z^{\prime})

(associated to
e
)
by gauge transforming with
\mathsf{M}^{-1}
.
Because the sought after matrix
Q_{i}(z^{\prime})
is given by the first
R^{\prime}\times R^{\prime}
block of
\widetilde{Q}_{i}(z^{\prime})
,
we can finally write the key formula

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{i}(z^{\prime})\quad=\quad\big[\partial_{i}\bullet\mathsf{M}\hskip 5.69046pt+\hskip 5.69046pt\mathsf{M}\cdot P_{i,0}(z^{\prime})\big]\cdot\mathsf{M}^{-1}\quad=\quad\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}Q_{i}(z^{\prime})&\star\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{0}&\star\end{array}\right]\,.

See

[
83
, Appendix C.2.1]

for an explanation of the zero block in the lower left corner.
Obtaining this block is a useful check for practical computations.

Before closing this section,
let us observe that the integer
N
does not have to be the "
N
" of the GKZ system.
In fact,
we did not use any notions related to the GKZ system to derive (
8.2.2
),
so the preceding discussion holds true for any (regular singular) Pfaffian system!

8.2.3 Holomorphic restriction of solutions

The previous section focused on the matrices associated to Pfaffian systems.
In the following two subsections,
we draw our attention to the
solution vectors
of Pfaffian systems,
in particular how to obtain actual series representations using restriction.
This topic can be viewed as singular boundary value problem
(see
[
157
]

[
158
]
and references therein).

To emphasize the connection to MIs,
let us use the notation
\vec{I}(z)
for the solution vector of a Pfaffian system

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\bullet\vec{I}(z)=P_{i}(z)\cdot\vec{I}(z)\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,.

(8.34)

Suppose that we are interested in solutions that are
holomorphic
on
Y^{\prime}=\{z_{1}=0\}
.
This means that
\vec{I}(z)
enjoys a Taylor expansion

\displaystyle\vec{I}(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\vec{I}\,^{(n)}(z^{\prime})\,z_{1}^{n}\,,

(8.35)

where as usual
z^{\prime}=(z_{2},\ldots,z_{N})
.
The issue at hand is how compute the expansion coefficient functions
\vec{I}\,^{(n)}
up to some appropriate order

\mathcal{O}(z_{1}^{k}).

Assume that the matrices
P_{i}(z)
are in normal form.
Insert the
z_{1}
-expansions of
P_{i}(z)
and
\vec{I}(z)
,
given by
(
8.14
)-(
8.15
) and (
8.35
),
into the Pfaffian system (
8.34
).
Relations among the
\vec{I}\,^{(n)}
are then obtained by comparing powers of
z_{1}^{n}
on both sides of the equality

[
157
, Chapter 4]

[
83
, Appendix D.6]
.
Comparing the leading-order terms in
z_{1}
,
the following relations are found:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rl}&\partial_{i}\bullet\vec{I}\,^{(0)}(z^{\prime})\ =\ P_{i,0}(z^{\prime})\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0)}(z^{\prime})\,,\quad i=2,\ldots,N\\[10.0pt]
&\text{Rank jump constraint:}\quad P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0)}(z^{\prime})\ =\ 0\,.\end{array}

The higher-order terms in
z_{1}
yield the recursion relation
4
4

4

The matrix in front of the sum is indeed invertible because the spectrum of

P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
is non-resonant.
More precisely,
the only potential integral eigenvalue of
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
is
0
,
and the diagonal is shifted by some
n>0
,
wherefore the determinant of the full expression is non-zero.

\displaystyle\vec{I}\,^{(n)}(z^{\prime})=\big[n\mathbf{1}-P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})\big]^{-1}\ \cdot\ \sum_{i=0}^{n-1}P_{1,n-i-1}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(i)}(z^{\prime})\,,\quad n>0\,.

(8.38)

The set of PDEs (
8.2.3
) is dubbed the

holomorphic restriction

of the Pfaffian system (
8.34
).
The first equation of (
8.2.3
) shows that
\vec{I}\,^{(0)}
plays
the role of the basis
e
in (
8.26
).
The second equation of (
8.2.3
) shows why the residue matrix
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})

played such a central role in the construction of Section (
8.2.1
),
wherein we modded out by the rows of
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
to obtain the rational restriction module.

At the level of solutions for Pfaffian systems,
we now have a clear interpretation for the
rank jump
from
R
to
R^{\prime}
:
the equation

P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0)}(z^{\prime})=0

imposes constraints among the entries of
\vec{I}\,^{(0)}(z^{\prime})
in the limit
z_{1}\to 0
,
meaning that some of them become linearly dependent on each other.
For FIs,
these relations turn out to be the new IBP relations among MIs which arise when the limit

z_{1}\to 0

is taken at the
integrand
level.
An example of this phenomenon is shown in Section (
9.2
).

Observe that once
\vec{I}\,^{(0)}
is determined from the PDEs (
8.2.3
),
then (
8.38
) fixes all higher-order terms
\vec{I}\,^{(n)}
via simple matrix multiplication
(one can even use finite field reconstruction in this step for efficiency).
The PDEs (
8.2.3
) are,
however,
written in a redundant way due to the residue matrix constraint.
We thus seek to "mod out" by this constraint,
in order to write the system in a minimal basis.
To set this up,
begin by writing
\vec{I}\,^{(0)}
in terms of some dummy symbols,
say

\displaystyle\vec{I}\,^{(0)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}I_{1}\\
\vdots\\
I_{R}\end{array}\right]\,.

Recall the definition of the
\mathsf{R}
-matrix from (
8.28
).
Because of the second equation in (
8.2.3
),
we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0)}=0.

(8.42)

This equation can be solved to determine which of the
I_{i}
symbols are linearly dependent on each other.
Removing those linearly dependent entries from
\vec{I}\,^{(0)}
,
the remaining ones are collected into a new
R^{\prime}
-dimensional basis

\displaystyle\vec{J}\quad=\quad\left[\begin{array}[]{c}I_{i_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
I_{i_{R^{\prime}}}\end{array}\right]\quad=\quad\mathsf{B}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0)}\,.

The newly introduced
\mathsf{B}
-matrix has dimension
R^{\prime}\times R
and entries
\mathsf{B}_{ij}\in\{0,1\}
.
In fact,
this is precisely the
\mathsf{B}
-matrix from (
8.27
).
Therefore,
both blocks of the
\mathsf{M}
-matrix (
•
‣
8.2.2
) are now known.
By construction,
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{B}\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{R}\end{array}\right]\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\vec{J}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{0}\end{array}\right]\,.

The Pfaffian matrices
Q_{i}(z^{\prime})
for the basis
\vec{J}

now immediately follow from (
8.2.2
),
giving the desired, restricted Pfaffian system

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\bullet\vec{J}(z^{\prime})=Q_{i}(z^{\prime})\cdot\vec{J}(z^{\prime})\quad\text{for}\quad i=2,\ldots,N\,.

(8.50)

This system is
simpler
to solve than the original Pfaffian system
(
8.34
) for
\vec{I}(z)
because
1) it has a smaller rank
R^{\prime}<R
,
and 2) it contains one less variable.
Once
\vec{J}
is solved for,
then
\vec{I}\,^{(0)}
is determined by multiplying equation (
8.2.3
) with
\mathsf{M}^{-1}
.

Algorithm (
3
) summarizes the preceding discussion.
Note that an initial Moser reduction step is required if the input is not in normal form.

Input
:

•

Pfaffian system in normal form of rank
R
:

\partial_{i}\bullet\vec{I}(z)=P_{i}(z)\cdot\vec{I}(z)

for

i=1,\ldots,N.

•

Integer
k>0
.

Output
:
The first
k
coefficient functions of

\vec{I}(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\vec{I}\,^{(n)}(z^{\prime})\,z_{1}^{n}.

1:

Set

\mathsf{R}=\texttt{RowReduce}[P_{1,-1}]

and

R^{\prime}=R-\mathrm{rank}\!\left(\mathsf{R}\right)
.

2:

Write
\vec{I}\,^{(0)}=\big[I_{1},\ldots,I_{R}\big]^{T}
.
Eliminate linearly dependent dummy symbols
I_{i}
by solving
\mathsf{R}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0)}=0
.
Collect linearly independent ones into
\vec{J}
.
Fix
\mathsf{B}
via

\vec{J}=\mathsf{B}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0)}.

3:

Set

\mathsf{M}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{B}\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{R}\end{array}\right].

4:

For each
i=2,\ldots,N
,
compute

\big(\partial_{i}\bullet\mathsf{M}+\mathsf{M}\cdot P_{i,0}\big)\cdot\mathsf{M}^{-1}

and save the upper left
R^{\prime}\times R^{\prime}
block matrices dubbed
Q_{i}
.

5:

Solve

\partial_{i}\bullet\vec{J}=Q_{i}\cdot\vec{J}

for
\vec{J}
and set

\vec{I}\,^{(0)}=\mathsf{M}^{-1}\cdot\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\vec{J}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{0}\end{array}\right].

6:

For each
n=1,\ldots,k
, calculate
\vec{I}\,^{(n)}
using the recursion (
8.38
).

7:

return

\{\vec{I}\,^{(0)},\ldots,\vec{I}\,^{(k)}\}
.

Algorithm 3

: Holomorphic series expansion

A geometric interpretation of the holomorphic restriction is illustrated in
Figure (
8.1
).
There are three axes,
one for the variable
z_{2}
and two for the vector components of

\vec{I}\,^{(0)}(z^{\prime})=[I_{1}(0,z_{2}),\,I_{2}(0,z_{2})]^{T},

where
I_{1}(z_{1},z_{2})
and
I_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})
represent a basis before restriction.

The
red
surface depicts all possible solutions to the rank jump constraint,
i.e. the nullspace of the residue matrix
P_{1,-1}(z_{2})
.
For fixed
z_{2}
,
this nullspace spans a 1-dimensional vector space shown as the
red
lines.
As
z_{2}
varies,
the lines trace out the whole
red
surface
5
5

5

Such a family of vector spaces is naturally formalized in terms of vector bundles.
.
The solutions living on the surface constitute a basis,
determined by the row space of the
\mathsf{B}
-matrix (
8.27
),
for the restricted Pfaffian system.
Since the nullspace is 1-dimensional,
the size of this basis is
R^{\prime}=R-1=1
.

For fixed
z_{2}
,
each
purple
line represents the 1-dimensional span of the row vectors in
P_{1,-1}(z_{2})
.
Varying
z_{2}
,
then these lines trace out the
purple
surface.
This surface is thus a depiction of the
\mathsf{R}
-matrix (
8.28
).

Since the
red
and
purple
surfaces are orthogonal to each other,
together they span a 2-dimensional vector space.
This implies that the
\mathsf{M}
-matrix (
•
‣
8.2.2
) is full rank and therefore invertible.

The
yellow
curve illustrates a
particular
solution vector
\vec{I}\,^{(0)}(z_{2})
,
given some initial condition
\vec{I}\,^{(0)}(0)
.
Observe that this solution is constrained to lie on the
red
surface -
this is analogous to the situation in classical mechanics where one finds the solution to
some equations of motion given a holonomic constraint.
According to (
8.2.3
),
the "flow" of the solution vector is governed by the Pfaffian matrix
P_{2,0}(z_{2})
:
given some point
\vec{I}\,^{(0)}(\overset{\circ}{z_{2}})
on the curve,
then the solution is evolved a small step in
z_{2}
by

\displaystyle\vec{I}\,^{(0)}(\overset{\circ}{z_{2}}+\Delta z_{2})

\displaystyle\ \simeq\ \vec{I}\,^{(0)}(\overset{\circ}{z_{2}})\ +\ \Delta z_{2}\Big[\partial_{2}\bullet\vec{I}\,^{(0)}(z_{2})\Big]\Big|_{z_{2}=\overset{\circ}{z_{2}}}

\displaystyle\ =\ \vec{I}\,^{(0)}(\overset{\circ}{z_{2}})\ +\ \Delta z_{2}\Big[P_{2,0}(\overset{\circ}{z_{2}})\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0)}(\overset{\circ}{z_{2}})\Big]\,.

(8.51)

Figure 8.1
:

The holomorphic restriction of a rank
R=2
solution vector

\vec{I}(z_{1},z_{2})=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}I_{1}(z_{1},z_{2})\\
I_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})\end{array}\right]

as
z_{1}\to 0
.
The
purple
and
red

surfaces depict
\mathsf{R}
and
\mathsf{B}
respectively.

8.2.4 Logarithmic restriction of solutions

Now suppose that the solution vector
\vec{I}(z)
to the Pfaffian system (
8.34
) is
logarithmically singular rather than holomorphic on
Y^{\prime}=\{z_{1}=0\}
.
Our goal is to state an algorithm for calculating the logarithmic series expansion of
\vec{I}(z)
.

The first step is to write down the most general ansatz to a Pfaffian system with regular singularities.
When the Pfaffian system is in normal form,
it is known that the ansatz decomposes into a sum over unique eigenvalues of the residue matrix

[
157
, Chapter 4]
.
Write the spectrum of the residue matrix as

\displaystyle\mathrm{spec}\left(P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})\right)=\Big\{\underbrace{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{1}}_{\Lambda_{1}}\ ,\ \underbrace{\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{2}}_{\Lambda_{2}}\ ,\ldots\Big\}\,,

(8.52)

where
\Lambda_{i}
is the multiplicity of eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
.
Even though
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
depends on
z^{\prime}
,
the integrability relations (
4.32
) actually dictate that the
eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
are independent of
z^{\prime}

[
157
, Theorem 12.1]
.
For FIs in DR,
it means that
\lambda_{i}
can only depend on
\epsilon
.
In all examples known to us,
the dependence is even linear in
\epsilon
:

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}=a_{i}+b_{i}\epsilon\quad\text{for}\quad a_{i},b_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}\,.

(8.53)

We are not aware of a proof of the above statement.

The ansatz for a logarithmically divergent solution vector can now be represented as

\displaystyle\vec{I}(z)\quad=\quad\sum_{\lambda\ \in!\ \mathrm{spec}\left(P_{1,-1}\right)}z_{1}^{\lambda}\ \times\ \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\ z_{1}^{n}\ \times\ \sum_{m=0}^{M_{\lambda}}\ \vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,n,m)}(z^{\prime})\ \times\ \log^{m}(z_{1})\,,

(8.54)

where "
\in!
" means "unique element of".
In DR,

\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,n,m)}
can be further expanded in
\epsilon
.
Note that there are two sources of logarithms in that case:
from the
\epsilon
-expansion of

z_{1}^{\lambda_{i}}=z_{1}^{a_{i}+b_{i}\epsilon}

and from the finite sum over
m
.

The integer
M_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
denotes the maximal power of
\log^{m}(z_{1})
.
Its value depends on the Jordan form of the residue matrix.
Decomposing this matrix into blocks labeled by unique eigenvalues

\displaystyle\texttt{JordanDecomposition}\!\left[P_{1,-1}\right]=\bigoplus_{\lambda\ \in!\ \mathrm{spec}\left(P_{1,-1}\right)}\,\mathsf{J}^{(\lambda)}\,,

(8.55)

then
6
6

6

Recall that a Jordan block has the eigenvalue
\lambda
on the diagonal,
and
1
’s on the superdiagonal.

0
’s on the superdiagonal correspond to Jordan blocks of size
1
.

\displaystyle M_{\lambda}=\big[\text{size of the largest Jordan block of}\ \mathsf{J}^{(\lambda)}\big]-1\,.

(8.56)

The ansatz (
8.54
) can now be inserted into the Pfaffian system
(
8.34
)
together with the
z_{1}
-expansions of
P_{i}(z)
from
(
8.14
)-(
8.15
).
One thereby obtains relations among the coefficient functions
\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,n,m)}
by comparing the coefficients of

z_{1}^{n}\,\log^{m}(z_{1})

on both sides of the equality
[
157
, Chapter 4]
.
For fixed
\lambda
,
among these relations one finds

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}&\partial_{i}\bullet\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,0,0)}(z^{\prime})\ =\ P_{i,0}(z^{\prime})\ \cdot\ \vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,0,0)}(z^{\prime})\qquad\text{for}\qquad i=2,\ldots,N\\[5.0pt]
&\text{Rank jump constraint}:\quad\big[P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})-\lambda\mathbf{1}\big]^{M_{\lambda}+1}\cdot\ \vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,0,0)}(z^{\prime})\ =\ 0\,.\end{array}

We call this the
logarithmic restriction
of the solution vector.
If
\lambda=0
,
which is the unique integral eigenvalue for a system in normal form,
then (
8.2.4
) reduces to the holomorphic restriction (
8.2.3
).

The second equation of (
8.2.4
) represents a constraint on the vector
\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,0,0)}
;
it must be a generalized eigenvector for
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
.
Recall that we modded out by the rows of
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
in the holomorphic case because of the constraint

P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0)}=0

(cf. (
8.21
)).
For fixed
\lambda
,
in the logarithmic case we instead mod out the rows of

\big[P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})-\lambda\mathbf{1}\big]^{M_{\lambda}+1}

(this would therefore supplant the
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
in (
8.21
)).

The vector
\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,0,0)}
hence plays the role of the basis
e
in (
8.26
),
and we can repeat the construction of Section (
8.2.1
) by replacing
\mathsf{R}
with

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}^{(\lambda)}=\texttt{RowReduce}\!\left[\big(P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})-\lambda\mathbf{1}\big)^{M_{\lambda}+1}\right]\,.

(8.59)

This matrix can be inserted into the formula (
8.2.2
)
together with some basis matrix
\mathsf{B}^{(\lambda)}
.
The result is a restricted Pfaffian system of size

{R\,^{\prime}}^{(\lambda)}

for a basis
\vec{J}\,^{(\lambda)}(z^{\prime})
:

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\bullet\vec{J}\,^{(\lambda)}(z^{\prime})\ =\ Q_{i}^{(\lambda)}(z^{\prime})\ \cdot\ \vec{J}\,^{(\lambda)}(z^{\prime})\quad\text{for}\quad i=2,\ldots,N\,.

(8.60)

The rank of this system is precisely the eigenvalue multiplicity of
\lambda
,
which also equals the size of the block matrix
\mathsf{J}^{(\lambda)}
in
(
8.55
).
That is,

\displaystyle{R\,^{\prime}}^{(\lambda)}=\Lambda\,.

(8.61)

The higher-order terms
\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,n,m)}
follow from recursion relations given
\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,0,0)}
as input.

•

Shifting powers of
\log^{m}(z_{1})
.
Set

\displaystyle\Phi^{(\lambda,m)}=\frac{P_{1,-1}-\lambda\mathbf{1}}{m}\,.

(8.62)

Then

\displaystyle\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,0,m)}\quad=\quad\Phi^{(\lambda,m)}\ \cdot\ \vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,0,m-1)}\quad\text{for}\quad 0<m\leq M_{\lambda}\,.

(A)

The terminating condition is

\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,0,M_{\lambda}+1)}=0.

•

Shifting powers of
z_{1}^{n}
.
Define

\displaystyle\Psi^{(\lambda,n)}=\big[(\lambda+n)\mathbf{1}-P_{1,-1}\big]^{-1}\,.

(8.63)

It holds that

\displaystyle\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,n,m)}\quad=\quad\Psi^{(\lambda,n)}\ \cdot\ \left(\sum_{\nu=0}^{n-1}P_{1,n-\nu-1}\ \cdot\ \vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,\nu,m)}\hskip 5.69046pt-\hskip 5.69046pt(m+1)\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,n,m+1)}\right)\,.

(B)

The last term drops out when
m=M_{\lambda}
,
leading to
7
7

7

(
B
) in fact also reduces to (
A
) when
n=0
.

\displaystyle\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,n,M_{\lambda})}\quad=\quad\Psi^{(\lambda,n)}\ \cdot\ \sum_{\nu=0}^{n-1}P_{1,n-\nu-1}\ \cdot\ \vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,\nu,M_{\lambda})}\,.

(C)

The index
n
runs up to
\infty
,
so this recursion ought to be truncated based on a
numerical accuracy condition for the full solution vector
\vec{I}(z)
.

Starting from
\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,0,0)}
,
we present a flowchart showing how to use the recursion relations
(
A
), (
B
) and (
C
)
to compute
\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,n,m)}
for all

0\leq n\leq N_{\lambda}

and

0\leq m\leq M_{\lambda}.

The flowchart is adapted from
[
157
, Page 42]
,
and the tuple
(n,m)
represents
\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,n,m)}
.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccccccccc}(0,0)&\overset{\eqref{m_shift}}{\longrightarrow}&(0,1)&\overset{\eqref{m_shift}}{\longrightarrow}&\cdots&\overset{\eqref{m_shift}}{\longrightarrow}&(0,M_{\lambda}-1)&\overset{\eqref{m_shift}}{\longrightarrow}&(0,M_{\lambda})\\[10.0pt]
&&&&&&&&\hskip 8.5359pt\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\overset{\eqref{n_shift_simple}}{\longrightarrow}$}\\[10.0pt]
(1,0)&\overset{\eqref{n_shift}}{\longleftarrow}&(1,1)&\overset{\eqref{n_shift}}{\longleftarrow}&\cdots&\overset{\eqref{n_shift}}{\longleftarrow}&(1,M_{\lambda}-1)&\overset{\eqref{n_shift}}{\longleftarrow}&(1,M_{\lambda})\\[10.0pt]
&&&&&&&&\hskip 8.5359pt\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\overset{\eqref{n_shift_simple}}{\longrightarrow}$}\\
&&&&&&&&\vdots\\[10.0pt]
&&&&&&&&\hskip 8.5359pt\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\overset{\eqref{n_shift_simple}}{\longrightarrow}$}\\[10.0pt]
(N_{\lambda},0)&\overset{\eqref{n_shift}}{\longleftarrow}&(N_{\lambda},1)&\overset{\eqref{n_shift}}{\longleftarrow}&\cdots&\overset{\eqref{n_shift}}{\longleftarrow}&(N_{\lambda},M_{\lambda}-1)&\overset{\eqref{n_shift}}{\longleftarrow}&(N_{\lambda},M_{\lambda})\end{array}

Example 8.1.
Fix
\lambda
and drop the
\lambda
-index from
\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,n,m)}
,
\Phi^{(\lambda,m)}
and
\Psi^{(\lambda,n)}
.
We showcase the recursion for
0\leq n\leq 2
and
0\leq m\leq 2
.

1.

n=0
: Using (
A
) twice,

\displaystyle\vec{I}\,^{(0,1)}

\displaystyle=\Phi^{(1)}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0,0)}

(8.71)

\displaystyle\vec{I}\,^{(0,2)}

\displaystyle=\Phi^{(2)}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0,1)}\,.

(8.72)

2.

n=1
: Using (
C
) once,

\displaystyle\vec{I}\,^{(1,2)}=\Psi^{(1)}\cdot P_{1,0}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0,2)}\,.

(8.73)

Using (
B
) twice,

\displaystyle\vec{I}\,^{(1,1)}

\displaystyle=\Psi^{(1)}\cdot\Big(P_{1,0}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0,1)}-2\vec{I}\,^{(1,2)}\Big)

(8.74)

\displaystyle\vec{I}\,^{(1,0)}

\displaystyle=\Psi^{(1)}\cdot\Big(P_{1,0}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0,0)}-1\vec{I}\,^{(1,1)}\Big)\,.

(8.75)

3.

n=2
: Using (
C
) once,

\displaystyle\vec{I}\,^{(2,2)}=\Psi^{(2)}\cdot\Big(P_{1,1}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0,2)}+P_{1,0}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(1,2)}\Big)\,.

(8.76)

Using (
B
) twice,

\displaystyle\vec{I}\,^{(2,1)}

\displaystyle=\Psi^{(2)}\cdot\Big(P_{1,1}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0,1)}+P_{1,0}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(1,1)}-2\vec{I}\,^{(2,2)}\Big)

(8.77)

\displaystyle\vec{I}\,^{(2,0)}

\displaystyle=\Psi^{(2)}\cdot\Big(P_{1,1}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(0,0)}+P_{1,0}\cdot\vec{I}\,^{(1,0)}-1\vec{I}\,^{(2,1)}\Big)\,.

(8.78)

Note that this recursion can be performed over a finite field for the sake of efficiency.

\blacksquare

The discussion above is summarized in the following algorithm.

Input
:

•

Pfaffian system in normal form:

\partial_{i}\bullet\vec{I}(z)=P_{i}(z)\cdot\vec{I}(z)

for

i=1,\ldots,N.

•

Integer
k>0
.

Output
:
The series expansion coefficients
\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,n,m)}
from (
8.54
) up to order
\mathcal{O}(z_{1}^{k})
.

1:

Compute

\texttt{JordanDecomposition}\!\left[P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})\right]

with
z^{\prime}
fixed to generic numbers.
Save

•

unique eigenvalues

\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots\}

•

eigenvalue multiplicities

\{\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},\ldots\}

•

integers

\{M_{\lambda_{1}},M_{\lambda_{2}},\ldots\}

from (
8.56
).

2:

for
each unique
\lambda

do

3:

Repeat steps 1. to 5. in Algorithm (
3
) with the replacements

•

\mathsf{R}\leftarrow\mathsf{R}^{(\lambda)}=\texttt{RowReduce}\!\left[\big(P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})-\lambda\mathbf{1}\big)^{M_{\lambda}+1}\right]

•

R^{\prime}\leftarrow\Lambda

•

\vec{I}\,^{(0)}\leftarrow\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,0,0)}

and save the result for
\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,0,0)}
.

4:

for

0\leq n\leq k
and
0\leq m\leq M_{\lambda}

do

5:

Input
\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,0,0)}
into the recursion relations
(
A
), (
B
), (
C
)
to compute
\vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,n,m)}
.

6:

end

for

7:

end

for

8:

return
The coefficient functions

\bigcup_{\lambda}\ \bigcup_{0\,\leq\,n\,\leq k}\ \bigcup_{0\,\leq\,m\,\leq M_{\lambda}}\ \vec{I}\,^{(\lambda,n,m)}

Algorithm 4

: Logarithmic series expansion

We expect this to be an efficient method for computing series expansions of MIs.
Its efficiency stems from trading a "big" Pfaffian system
(satisfied by the full solution vector
\vec{I}(z)
)
for several "small" Pfaffian systems
(satisfied by the restricted bases
\vec{J}\,^{(\lambda)}(z^{\prime})
),
each one containing one less variable.
For instance,
if the "big" system includes unrationalizable square roots and elliptic functions,
then it is likely that the "small" systems only contain simple GPLs.

The catch is that the algorithm does not produce results valid in the entire phase space;
there must exist a variable
z_{1}\ll 1
that can be used for the expansion.
Luckily,
there are many such situations in high-energy physics:

z_{1}
could be a small mass,
a threshold variable (e.g.
s-4m^{2}
),
an angle between two collinear momenta (e.g. in Regge theory),
a small or large transverse momentum (
z_{1}=p_{T}
or
z_{1}=1/p_{T}
),
a soft momentum variable in Post-Newtonian/Minkowskian gravity,
and so forth.

The
method of regions
is the classic approach
to asymptotic expansions
[
159
,
160
,
161
]
.
More recent approaches include

[
162
,
163
,
164
,
165
,
166
]
.
It would be interesting to import some of the wisdom from these studies
in order to enhance the logarithmic restriction approach presented here.

Section 8.3
Restriction at the level of Macaulay matrices

While the protocol of the previous section is applicable to any regularly singular Pfaffian system,
it does require an unrestricted system as input,
which might be hard to obtain.
It should also be mentioned that this method is only conjectured to yield the rational restriction
of the twisted cohomology group (
6.55
) when it is viewed as a
\mathcal{D}
-module

[
83
, Remark 3.5]
.

Building on the Macaulay matrix method introduced in Chapter (
5
),
this section presents a holomorphic restriction protocol which works entirely at the level of

\mathcal{D}
-modules.
Theorems from
[
83
, Section 4]
ensure that the method yields correct results.
Some steps of the construction are technical,
so we refer to the previous reference for details.

8.3.1 Restriction to a hyperplane

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a holonomic ideal in
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
.
This could e.g. be the annihilating ideal for the GKZ system.
The goal is to compute a Pfaffian system for the rational restriction module onto the hyperplane given by

Y^{\prime}=\{z_{1}=\cdots=z_{N^{\prime}}=0\}.

The first question to answer is that of a basis for the restriction module.
In
[
83
, Algorithm 4.7]
,
a systematic search algorithm is formulated which succeeds when the dimension
of the basis matches the expected holonomic rank
R^{\prime}
of the restriction module.
The algorithm requires
R^{\prime}
as input,
but this is easily obtained for the case of Euler integrals by computing the
Euler characteristic
\chi
via the likelihood equations (
6.56
).
Moreover,
this algorithm relies entirely on numerical linear algebra,
with all parameters fixed to generic numbers,
meaning that it will terminate with modest computing power even for relatively large Pfaffian systems.

The resulting standard monomials for the restriction module,
call them
\mathrm{RStd}
,
now serve as input for the Macaulay matrix
M
in Algorithm (
1
).
Recall that
M
is built by acting with "seed" monomials on the generators of
\mathcal{I}
.
Since
\mathcal{I}
contains unrestricted variables defined on
Y
,
so will
M
.
The key observation is this:
all the entries of
M
are polynomial in
z\in Y
,
so we can simply fix
z_{1}=\ldots=z_{N^{\prime}}=0
in
M
,
and then proceed with the usual Macaulay matrix algorithm.

Input
:

•

The restricted basis
\mathrm{RStd}
.

•

Generators
\{d_{1},\ldots,d_{D}\}
of a holonomic ideal
\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
.

•

Direction
i
.

Output
:
Pfaffian matrix
Q_{i}(z^{\prime})
for the restriction

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{Y^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{Y^{\prime}}}\mathcal{D}_{Y}/\big(\mathcal{I}+z_{1}\mathcal{D}_{Y}+\ldots+z_{N^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{Y}\big)\,.

(8.79)

1:

Call Algorithm (
1
) with the replacements

•

\mathrm{Std}\leftarrow\mathrm{RStd}

•

M\leftarrow\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}M_{\mathrm{Ext}}&M_{\mathrm{RStd}}\end{array}\right]\Big|_{z_{1}=\ldots=z_{N^{\prime}}=0}

2:

return

Q_{i}(z^{\prime})=C_{\mathrm{RStd}}^{(i)}-C^{(i)}\cdot M_{\mathrm{RStd}}

Algorithm 5

: Rationally restricted Pfaffian system by the Macaulay matrix

Let us remark that the basis
\mathrm{RStd}
can also be guessed,
assuming
R^{\prime}
is known,
by executing Algorithm (
5
) several times with all parameters set to numbers
until it succeeds.

Algorithm (
5
) has been implemented in the
asir
package

mt_mm
by Nobuki Takayama,
as showcased in the following example.

Example 8.1.

Let

\mathcal{I}=\langle d_{1},d_{2}\rangle

be the ideal generated by

\displaystyle d_{1}

\displaystyle=z_{1}(1-z_{1})\partial_{1}^{2}-z_{1}z_{2}\partial_{1}\partial_{2}+(c_{1}-z_{1}(a+b_{1}+1))\partial_{1}-b_{1}z_{2}\partial_{2}-ab_{1}

(8.80)

\displaystyle d_{2}

\displaystyle=z_{2}(1-z_{2})\partial_{2}^{2}-z_{1}z_{2}\partial_{1}\partial_{2}+(c_{2}-z_{2}(a+b_{2}+1))\partial_{2}-b_{2}z_{1}\partial_{1}-ab_{2}\,,

(8.81)

which annihilates Appell’s
F_{2}
function

\displaystyle F_{2}(a,b_{1},

\displaystyle b_{2},c_{1},c_{2}|z_{1},z_{2})=\frac{\Gamma(c_{1})\Gamma(c_{2})}{\Gamma(b_{1})\Gamma(b_{2})\Gamma(c_{1}-b_{1})\Gamma(c_{2}-b_{2})}\ \times

\displaystyle\int_{(0,1)^{2}}(1-x_{1})^{c_{1}-b_{1}-1}(1-x_{2})^{c_{2}-b_{2}-1}(1-z_{1}x_{1}-z_{2}x_{2})^{-a}x_{1}^{b_{1}}x_{2}^{b_{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x}\,.

(8.82)

This function appears often in the FI calculus
[
167
,
168
]
.

The following
asir

.

The inputs of

merit explanation.
The list
IdealNumeric
contains generators with
z_{2}
and exponent parameters fixed to generic numbers.
The integers

and

refer to the
\gamma
and

of
the basis search algorithm
[

, Algorithm 4.7]
.
The value

means that the function is executed over a finite field of order

p=100000007
,
which is more efficient than a symbolic calculation.

\displaystyle\partial_{2}\,\mathrm{RStd}=Q_{2}\cdot\mathrm{RStd}

(8.83)

given by

\displaystyle\partial_{2}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
