\end{array}\right]\,.

The restriction we compute is

\displaystyle A\quad\xrightarrow[1\leq i\,\leq\,18\ \text{and}\ 19\,\leq\,j\,\leq\,22]{z_{i}=1\ \text{and}\ z_{j}=t/s}\quad\parbox[c]{75.33043pt}{\includegraphics[]{Figs/2L_diagonal_box.pdf}}\,,

(9.200)

where
s
and
t
stand for Mandelstam variables.
To be clear,
after a linear shift of coordinates,
this is a restriction onto the affine hyperplane

\displaystyle Y^{\prime}=\big\{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{22})\in\mathbb{A}^{22}\,\big|\,z_{1}=\ldots=z_{18}=0\ \text{and}\ z_{19}=z_{20}=z_{21}=z_{22}=z\ \text{is generic}\big\}\,.

Using homogeneity
(cf. Section (
6.5
))
we can immediately restrict
z_{i}=0
for
i\in\{1,2,5,7,9,11,13,18\}
.
This means that Algorithm (
5
) should be applied to restrict

z_{i}=0
for
i\in\{3,4,6,8,10,12,14,15,16,17\}
and
z_{i}=z=t/s
for
i\in\{19,20,21,22\}
.

9.4.2 Restriction

For generic values of the analytic regulators
\epsilon
and
\delta
,
the GKZ system is of rank
R=115
.
Computing the Euler characteristic by counting the number of
critical points of (
6.56
) on
Y^{\prime}
,
we find a rank drop to
R^{\prime}=7
.
This number is verified by counting the number of MIs using IBPs.

We must find a basis of the restriction module before calling Algorithm (
5
).
Executing
[
83
, Algorithm 4.7]
as in Example (
8.3.1
) with parameters

\gamma=3,\,k=3,\,p=100000007
and
z=1/7
,
we obtain the
7
-dimensional basis
8
8

8

This took 40.56s on a machine with the following specs:

Intel(R) Core(TM) i7-10700K CPU @ 3.80GHz, 8G memory
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{RStd}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{21}\partial_{22}\\
\partial_{22}^{2}\\
\partial_{6}\\
\partial_{17}\\
\partial_{21}\\
\partial_{22}\\
1\end{array}\right]\,.

A valid Macaulay matrix
M_{1}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}M_{\mathrm{Ext}}&M_{\mathrm{RStd}}\end{array}\right]

is then found
9
9

9

It took 65.76s on the same machine to compute this Macaulay matrix.

for degree
Q=1
,
meaning that the equation (
5.13
) given by

C_{\mathrm{Ext}}-C\cdot M_{\mathrm{Ext}}=0

has a solution for the matrix
C
.
The matrix
M_{\mathrm{Ext}}
has dimension
1305\times 2763
,
so there are
2763
exterior monomials
\mathrm{Ext}
.
The
1305
rows are not all independent;
by row reducing
M_{\mathrm{Ext}}
with
(\epsilon,\delta,z)
fixed to generic numbers,
we identify
912
linearly independent equations.
It then takes less than
3
minutes on a laptop to determine
C
via
FiniteFlow
.
The
7\times 7
Pfaffian matrix
P_{z}
finally results from

P_{z}=C_{\mathrm{RStd}}-C\cdot M_{\mathrm{RStd}}.

This becomes a Pfaffian matrix for the proper 2-loop diagonal-box topology after multiplying by
the LP prefactors from (
7.7
) and sending
\delta\to 0
.

As an aside,
note how large the discrepancy is between the ranks

R=115
and
R^{\prime}=7
for the GKZ system and the proper FI.
So even though we benefit from being able to immediately write down an
annihilating ideal and a basis in the GKZ setting,
the additional complexity brought about by unspecified
z
-variables may quickly become too much to handle.

Part III
Numerical Integration

Chapter 10
Tropical Integration

This thesis has,
so far,
predominantly been an
analytic
study of Euler and Feynman integrals.
In particular,
we have focused on the DEQs obeyed by said integrals.
The ideal situation is that
1) these DEQs can be built with reasonable computer resources,
and
2) they can be solved symbolically in terms of special functions.
For sufficiently difficult problems -
e.g. the evaluation of 2- or 3-loop diagrams with several kinematic scales -
either or both of these steps become insurmountable with the presently available analytical methods.
These difficulties are in many cases unavoidable if the accuracy of theoretical predictions
is made to compete with the experimental precision at the LHC.
For instance,
the slow convergence rate of the QCD perturbative series
(stemming from the coupling constant being
\alpha_{s}(M_{Z})\sim 0.1
near the weak scale)
often requires 3-loop results to get cross sections at
\%
-level accuracy.
Furthermore,
the study of Higgs phenomenology typically requires the calculation
of diagrams with many internal masses that do not evaluate to GPLs,
which complicates the determination of canonical bases in the method of DEQs for FIs.

In cases where analytical methods fall short,

numerical
methods may instead offer a way forward.
The past few years have indeed seen a surge in the development of
public codes tailored to the numerical evaluation of FIs.
Using series of expansions of Pfaffian systems,
the codes

AMFlow

[
34
]
,

DiffExp

[
173
,
174
]

and

SeaSyde

[
175
]

are able to to achieve phenomenal accuracy.
They derive Pfaffian systems by fixing parameters to numbers
before
solving the IBPs,
which yields a significant speed boost.
The
pySecDec
codebase
[
176
]
,
employing
Monte Carlo
integration in combination with
sector decomposition
,
has been a staple of high-energy physics calculations for many years,
and was recently upgraded in
[
177
]
.
Sophisticated manipulations of momentum space integrals via the
Loop-tree duality

led to efficient and stable numerical integration schemes in
[
178
,
179
]
.
The authors of
[
180
]
presented a high-precision
numerical algorithm by interpreting FIs as
period integrals
.
Further promising studies were carried out in
[
181
,
182
,
183
]
,
though so far without publicly available codes.

Part III
of this thesis is a contribution to this flourishing research program.
The present chapter describes a Monte Carlo integration scheme called
tropical integration
.
It was established by Borinsky in
[
40
]

(see also
[
184
]
),
and subsequently generalized by Borinsky, Tellander and the present author in
[
185
]
.
A public code named
Feyntrop
was developed alongside the aforementioned article.

Feyntrop
is able to swiftly numerically evaluate FIs at high loop-order with many mass scales
at ‰-level accuracy given modest computer resources.
The next chapter gives a tutorial on how to use the code,
and showcases several state-of-the-art examples.
The present chapter explicates the theoretical background.

Section 10.1
Tropical Monte Carlo sampling, in brief

Tropical integration is a special instance of Monte Carlo (MC) integration.
MC methods deal with integrals of the form

\displaystyle I_{f}=\int_{\Gamma}f(x)\mu\,,

(10.1)

where
f(x)
is a square-integrable function integrated over an
n
-dimensional domain
\Gamma
,
and
\mu
is a probability measure that is positive on
\Gamma
and normalized to unity,

\int_{\Gamma}\mu=1.

By sampling many independent random points

\{x^{(1)},\ldots,x^{(N)}\}

from
\mu
,
it follows from the central limit theorem that

\displaystyle I_{f}\simeq I_{f}^{(N)}\quad\text{where}\quad I_{f}^{(N)}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f\big(x^{(i)}\big)\,.

(10.2)

Here
I_{f}^{(N)}
is simply the average of the integrand
f
evaluated at the random points
x^{(i)}
.
For sufficiently large
N
,
the expected error of this approximation is

\displaystyle\sigma_{f}=\sqrt{\frac{I_{f^{2}}-I_{f}^{2}}{N}}\quad\text{where}\quad I_{f^{2}}=\int_{\Gamma}f(x)^{2}\mu\,.

(10.3)

This error can itself be estimated by

\displaystyle\sigma_{f}\simeq\sigma_{f}^{(N)}\quad\text{where}\quad\sigma_{f}^{(N)}=\sqrt{\frac{I_{f^{2}}^{(N)}-\left[I_{f}^{(N)}\right]^{2}}{N-1}}\quad\text{and}\quad I_{f^{2}}^{(N)}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f\big(x^{(i)}\big)^{2}\,.

(10.4)

A common choice for
\mu
is just the uniform measure

\mu=\mathrm{d}^{n}x

on the
n
-dimensional unit cube
\Gamma=[0,1]^{n}
.
Tropical integration,
instead,
makes use of polytopal and tropical geometry
(see
[
35
,
186
]
for seminal textbooks on these topics)
to build a more sophisticated
tropical measure

\mu^{\mathrm{tr}}
.
This measure is tailored to estimating integrals of functions

f(x)=\frac{g(x)^{a}}{h(x)^{b}}

given by homogeneous polynomials
g,h\in\mathbb{C}[x]
and rational numbers
a,b\in\mathbb{Q}
.
A good example hereof is the evaluation of the integral

\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}x\wedge\mathrm{d}y}{x+y}=2\log 2
.
"Ordinary" MC fails because the variance of
\frac{1}{x+y}
is infinite w.r.t. the uniform measure.
It is, however, easily evaluated with the tropical measure.
See
[
187
, Section 3.1.1]
for a detailed discussion.

10.1.1 Tropical approximation

As we are ultimately interested in integrating homogeneous polynomials,
let us now define the notion of
real projective space

\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
;
this which will serve as an integration domain
1
1

1

In practice, this domain can be parametrized as a simplex polytope.

later on.

\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
is defined by the set of points in

\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}

modulo the equivalence relation

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\begin{array}[]{rcl}(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1})&\sim&(y_{0},\ldots,y_{n-1})\\
&\Updownarrow&\\
\exists\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\,:\,(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1})&=&(\lambda y_{0},\ldots,\lambda y_{n-1})\,.\end{array}

We denote the coordinates on
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
by

[x_{0}:\ldots:x_{n-1}].

Two points such as

[1:0:2]

and

[\tfrac{1}{2}:0:1]

would be equivalent in
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
because they are related by

(1,\,0,\,2)=(2\cdot\tfrac{1}{2},\,2\cdot 0,\,2\cdot 1).

Positive
projective space is further given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n-1}_{+}=\{x=[x_{0}:\cdots:x_{n-1}]\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\,|\,x_{i}>0\}\,.

(10.8)

Above we made the unusual choice of starting the index at
0
rather than
1
.
This convention allows for seamless interoperability with the
python
programming language,
to be used in the next chapter.

Let
p
be a homogeneous polynomial.
In multi-index notation,

\displaystyle p(x)=\sum_{k\in\mathrm{supp}\left(p\right)}c_{k}\,x^{k}\ ,

(10.9)

where

x^{k}=x_{0}^{k_{0}}\cdots x_{n-1}^{k_{n-1}}

and
|k|=k_{0}+\ldots+k_{n-1}
is equal for all multi-indices
k
.
The
tropical approximation

[
39
]
to
p
is
the central concept in tropical integration:

\displaystyle p^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)=\max_{k\in\mathrm{supp}\left(p\right)}x^{k}\,.

(10.10)

If,
for instance,

p(x)=x_{0}x_{1}x_{2}^{2}-2x_{1}^{4}+42ix_{0}^{2}x_{1}^{2},

then

p^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)=\max\big\{x_{0}x_{1}x_{2}^{2},\,x_{1}^{4},\,x_{0}^{2}x_{1}^{2}\big\}.

In other words,
the tropical approximation neglects the monomial coefficients,
and only cares about the largest monomial.
The set of monomial exponents
\mathrm{supp}\left(p\right)
is hence the essential data for
p^{\mathrm{tr}}
.
Interpreting each multi-index in
\mathrm{supp}\left(p\right)
as a vector in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{\geq 0}
and taking the convex hull,
we see that
p^{\mathrm{tr}}
is simply a function avatar of the
Newton polytope

\mathbf{N}[p]
from
(
6.71
).

Suppose that
\mathbf{N}[p]
lives in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as parametrized by
x
.
By virtue of being a polytope,

\mathbf{N}[p]
is given by a finite intersection of
half-spaces
,
each of the form

\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,|\,v\cdot x\leq c\}

for some scalar
c\in\mathbb{R}
.
A subset
F\subset\mathbf{N}[p]
is called a
face
of
\mathbf{N}[p]
iff

1.

There exist
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathbf{N}[p]
is contained in

\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,|\,v\cdot x\leq c\}.

2.

F
is the intersection of
\mathbf{N}[p]
with

\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,|\,v\cdot x=c\}.

The polynomial
p
truncated to a face
F
is given by

\displaystyle p_{F}(x)=\sum_{k\,\in\,F\,\cap\,\mathrm{supp}\left(p\right)}c_{k}\,x^{k}\,.

(10.11)

p
is called
completely non-vanishing
on a domain
X

if for each face
F
of the Newton polytope we have

p_{F}(x)\neq 0,\,x\in X.

Example 10.1.
Consider the polynomial

\displaystyle p(x)=x_{0}+x_{1}+2x_{0}^{2}+x_{1}^{2}+3x_{0}x_{1}+2x_{0}^{2}x_{1}+x_{0}x_{1}^{2}+x_{0}^{3}\,.

(10.12)

It is non-homogeneous,
but that is inconsequential for the purpose of this example.
Its associated Newton polytope
\mathbf{N}[p]
is shown in Figure (
10.1
).
As an intersection of half-spaces,
the Newton polytope is represented by

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}[p]=\big\{x_{0}\geq 0\big\}\,\cap\,\big\{x_{1}\geq 0\big\}\,\cap\,\big\{x_{1}\leq 2\big\}\,\cap\,\big\{x_{0}+x_{1}\geq 1\big\}\,\cap\,\big\{x_{0}+x_{1}\leq 3\big\}\,.

(10.13)

Consider a face
F
of
\mathbf{N}[p]
,
for example the bottom line segment touching the
x_{0}
-axis given by

F=\{(x_{0},x_{1})\,|\,1\leq x_{0}\leq 3\ \text{and}\ x_{1}=0\}.

F
is indeed a face since
1)
\mathbf{N}[p]
is contained in the half-space

\{x_{1}\geq 0\},

and
2)

F
is given by an intersection

F=\mathbf{N}[p]\,\cap\,\{x_{1}=0\}.

The polynomial truncated to this face is given by

\displaystyle p_{F}(x)=x_{0}+2x_{0}^{2}+x_{0}^{3}\,.

(10.14)

Because each monomial coefficient of
p
is positive,
we have that
p
is completely non-vanishing on,
say,

X=\mathbb{R}^{2}_{>0}
.

\blacksquare

Figure 10.1
:

Newton polytope for the polynomial
p(x)
in (
10.12
).
The black dots represent the monomial exponents of
p(x)
,
and the pink area is their convex hull in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
as parametrized by
(x_{0},x_{1})
.

Borinsky proved that there is really a sense in which
p^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)

approximates
the values of
p(x)
;
this is the backbone behind tropical integration.
Take a homogeneous polynomial
p\in\mathbb{C}[x]
that is completely non-vanishing on
positive projective space
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}_{+}
.
Then there exist two constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that
[
40
, Theorem 8]

\displaystyle C_{1}\ \leq\ \frac{|p(x)|}{p^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)}\ \leq\ C_{2}\quad\text{for all}\quad x\in\mathbb{P}^{n-1}_{+}\,.

(10.15)

In other words,
the tropical approximation of
p
bounds the original polynomial from above and from below.

We can now describe the intuition behind tropical integration
(a precise algorithm is given in Section (
10.3.2
)).
Consider a convergent integral

\displaystyle I=\int_{\mathbb{P}^{n-1}_{+}}\frac{g(x)^{a}}{h(x)^{b}}\,\Omega

(10.16)

given by two homogeneous polynomials
g
and
h
that are completely non-vanishing on the integration domain,
and
a,b\in\mathbb{Q}
.
We insert a clever way of writing the number "
1
" into the integrand:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllll}I&=&\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{P}^{n-1}_{+}}\frac{g(x)^{a}}{h(x)^{b}}\,\Omega&\times&\displaystyle\left(\frac{g^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)}{g^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)}\right)^{a}\displaystyle\left(\frac{h^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)}{h^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)}\right)^{b}\\
&=&\displaystyle\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{P}^{n-1}_{+}}\frac{g^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)^{a}}{h^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)^{b}}\,\Omega}_{\propto\ \text{tropical measure}\ \mu^{\mathrm{tr}}}&\times&\underbrace{\displaystyle\left(\frac{g(x)}{g^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)}\right)^{a}\displaystyle\left(\frac{h^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)}{h(x)}\right)^{b}}_{\text{bounded due to }\eqref{tropical_approximation_property}}\,.\end{array}

The integrand has been factored into something resembling a probability measure
(after being suitably normalized)
multiplied by a function that is square-integrable as a consequence of being bounded.
This is now a suitable setup for MC integration:
we sample a large number of random points
x^{(i)}
from
\mu^{\mathrm{tr}}
,
and then take the average of the function
(g/g^{\mathrm{tr}})^{a}\,(h^{\mathrm{tr}}/h)^{b}
evaluated at these points.

Section 10.2
Projective Feynman integrals

As we will present a numerical code later on,
let us be explicit about our conventions for FIs.
Take
G
to be a one-particle irreducible Feynman diagram with edge set
E
and vertex set
V
.
With our index convention,
we have

E=\{0,1,\ldots,|E|-1\}

and

V=\{0,1,\ldots,|V|-1\}.

Every edge
e\in E
is equipped with a mass
m_{e}
and an edge weight
\nu_{e}
.
Every vertex
v\in V
comes with an incoming momentum vector
p_{v}
.
Internal vertices are distinguished by having vanishing incoming momenta;
i.e.,
writing

V=V_{\text{ext}}\,\sqcup\,V_{\text{int}},

then
p_{v}=0
if
v\in V_{\text{int}}
.

The
incidence matrix

\mathcal{E}
of
G
is defined by choosing an arbitrary orientation for the edges,
and setting

\mathcal{E}_{v,e}=\pm 1

if edge
e
points to/from vertex
v
and

\mathcal{E}_{v,e}=0

if
e
is not incident to
v
.

In terms of this data,
we write the FI associated to
G
as

\displaystyle I_{G}=\int\prod_{e\in E}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{D}}\ell_{e}}{i\pi^{\mathrm{D}/2}}\left(\frac{-1}{\ell_{e}^{2}-m_{e}^{2}+i\varepsilon}\right)^{\nu_{e}}\prod_{v\in V\setminus\{v_{0}\}}i\pi^{\mathrm{D}/2}\delta^{(\mathrm{D})}\Big(p_{v}+\sum_{e\in E}\mathcal{E}_{v,e}\,\ell_{e}\Big)\,.

(10.19)

The loop momenta
\ell_{e}
are integrated over
\mathrm{D}
-dimensional Minkowski space with mostly-minus signature,

\ell_{e}^{2}=\left(\ell^{0}_{e}\right)^{2}-\left(\ell^{1}_{e}\right)^{2}-\left(\ell^{2}_{e}\right)^{2}-\ldots,

and an overall momentum-conserving
\delta
-function

\delta(p_{v_{0}}+\ldots+p_{v_{|V|-1}})

has been extracted by removing a vertex
v_{0}\in V
.

To evaluate
I_{G}
numerically,
we use an equivalent parametric representation
(see
[
44
, Section 2.5]
for a derivation starting from momentum space):

\displaystyle I_{G}

\displaystyle=\Gamma(\omega)\int_{\mathbb{P}^{|E|-1}_{+}}\phi\qquad\text{with}

(10.20)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{e\in E}\frac{x_{e}^{\nu_{e}}}{\Gamma(\nu_{e})}\right)\frac{1}{\mathcal{U}(x)^{\mathrm{D}/2}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}(x)-i\varepsilon\sum_{e\in E}x_{e}}\right)^{\omega}\Omega\,.

This is an integral over positive projective space
\mathbb{P}^{|E|-1}_{+}
,
defined in (
10.8
),
w.r.t. the volume form

\displaystyle\Omega=\sum_{e=0}^{|E|-1}(-1)^{|E|-e-1}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{0}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{\mathrm{d}x_{e}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{|E|-1}}{x_{1}\cdots\widehat{x}_{e}\cdots x_{|E|-1}}\,,

(10.21)

where hats denote omission.
The superficial degree of divergence of the graph
G
is given by

\omega=\sum_{e\in E}\nu_{e}-\mathrm{D}\cdot L/2

where
L=|E|-|V|+1
is the number of loops.

\mathcal{U}(x)
is the first Symanzik polynomial,
previously defined in (
7.1
).
Recalling also the second Symanzik polynomial
\mathcal{F}(x)
from (
7.2
),
we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(x)=\frac{\mathcal{F}(x)}{\mathcal{U}(x)}\,.

(10.22)

Since

\mathcal{U}(\lambda x)=\lambda^{L}\mathcal{U}(x)

and

\mathcal{F}(\lambda x)=\lambda^{L+1}\mathcal{F}(x),

one can quickly verify that the integrand
\phi
is invariant under the coordinate rescaling

x\to\lambda x,

wherefore it is appropriate to call (
10.20
) a

projective integral representation
.

The graph-theoretic formulas
(
7.1
) and (
7.2
) for
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{F}

are in fact not so efficient for numerically evaluating millions of points during MC integration.
The reason is that the number of monomials grows quickly as a function of
|E|
and
L
,
making it slow to add up all terms during each numerical evaluation.
Let us therefore present more efficient formulas in terms of matrices
that scale linearly with the complexity of
G
.
To start,
define the
(|V|-1)\times(|V|-1)

reduced Laplacian matrix
component-wise by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{uv}=\sum_{e\in E}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{u,e}\,\mathcal{E}_{v,e}}{x_{e}}\quad\text{for}\quad u,v\,\in\,V\setminus\{v_{0}\}\,.

(10.23)

As this matrix is symmetric,
it is only necessary to explicitly calculate the diagonal and the upper triangular block.
The lower triangular block can then be copied from the upper one.
It turns out that
\mathcal{L}
is positive definite.

Let

\mathcal{P}_{uv}=p_{u}\cdot p_{v}

be the
(|V|-1)\times(|V|-1)
matrix containing Minkowski scalar products between all independent momenta.

We then have the identities

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(x)

\displaystyle=\det\big[\mathcal{L}(x)\big]\prod_{e\in E}x_{e}

(10.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}(x)\left[-\sum_{u,v\,\in\,V\setminus\{v_{0}\}}\big(\mathcal{L}^{-1}(x)\big)_{uv}\,\mathcal{P}_{uv}+\sum_{e\in E}m_{e}^{2}\,x_{e}\right]\,.

(10.25)

Note that the first sum in
\mathcal{F}
can be expressed as

\text{tr}\left[\mathcal{L}^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{P}\right].

Determinants and traces are computationally cheap,
but the matrix inverse
\mathcal{L}^{-1}
appears to be expensive at first glance.
However,
notice that we are only interested in the combination

\mathsf{X}=\mathcal{L}^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{P},

not
\mathcal{L}^{-1}
itself.
Owing to the fact that
\mathcal{L}
is positive definite,
then
\mathsf{X}
can be swiftly evaluated using
Cholesky decomposition
.
More precisely,
in
O(|V|^{3})
time,
it is possible to decompose

\mathcal{L}=\mathsf{L}\cdot\mathsf{D}\cdot\mathsf{L}^{T}

in terms of a lower triangular matrix
\mathsf{L}
and a diagonal matrix
\mathsf{D}
.
Afterwards,
the solution
\mathsf{X}
to the linear system

\mathcal{L}\cdot\mathsf{X}=\mathcal{P}

is immediately obtained by solving

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}\cdot\mathsf{Y}_{1}=\mathcal{P}\,,\quad\mathsf{D}\cdot\mathsf{Y}_{2}=\mathsf{Y}_{1}\,,\quad\mathsf{L}^{T}\cdot\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{Y}_{2}

(10.26)

in cascade.

Later on,
we will actually require
\mathcal{L}
to take on complex values rather than real ones.
Then
\mathcal{L}
is no longer Hermitian,
wherefore Cholesky decomposition fails.
In this case,
one must use the less efficient LU (lower-upper) decomposition.

10.2.1
i\varepsilon
-prescription via contour deformation

For the purposes of this thesis,
say that a FI computation is in the
Euclidean regime
if

\mathcal{F}(x)\geq 0

for all

x\in\mathbb{P}^{|E|-1}_{+}

(a more refined notion of the Euclidean regime is given in
[
185
, Section 2.2]
).
Then it is only the case that
\mathcal{F}(x)=0
on the boundary
x\to 0
of the integration domain,
which is assumed to be an integrable singularity.
In our convention,
being in the Euclidean regime is analogous to having a FI with scalar products computed in the
Euclidean all-minus metric.
Life is simple in the Euclidean regime because one avoids the singularities in
(
10.19
) coming from setting the time-components equal to

\ell^{0}=\pm\sqrt{\vec{\ell}\cdot\vec{\ell}+m^{2}}

in the propagators.
Consequently,
Feynman’s
i\varepsilon
-prescription can be dropped,
and the resulting FI will be purely real.

Alas,
life is not so simple in Minkowski space.
Let us say that we are in the
Minkowski regime
when we are not in the Euclidean regime.
In this case,
the monomial coefficients of
\mathcal{F}(x)
can be both positive and negative.
The
i\varepsilon
regulates singularities coming from having
\mathcal{F}(x)=0
inside the integration domain.
In momentum space,
the
i\varepsilon
regulates the
\ell^{0}
-singularities by pushing them slightly away from
the real axis.
An alternative description is to deform the integration contour given by

\ell^{0}\in(-\infty,\infty)

to one that goes around the poles
2
2

2

With two poles there are four possible choices for how to go around them,
but only one is consistent with the notion of causality.
This contour corresponds to a propagator

\ell^{2}-m^{2}+i\varepsilon
.
Two non-casual examples are

(\ell^{0}+i\varepsilon)^{2}-\vec{\ell}\cdot\vec{\ell}-m^{2}

and

\ell^{2}-m^{2}-i\varepsilon
.
The correct contour goes
below the singularity at

-\sqrt{\vec{\ell}\cdot\vec{\ell}+m^{2}}

and above the one at

+\sqrt{\vec{\ell}\cdot\vec{\ell}+m^{2}}.

I.e.

in the complex
\ell^{0}
-plane.

.

The
i\varepsilon
-prescription only dictates on which side of the poles we integrate.
But for the purpose of numerical integration we must make an
explicit
choice for the contour,
preferably with decent numerical stability.
Moreover,
the deformation ought to preserve the projective symmetry of the integral
in order to play well with tropical MC sampling.
We find that the following deformation into the complex plane proposed in

[
188
,
189
]

fulfils both of these criteria:

\displaystyle X_{e}(x)=x_{e}\exp\left[-i\lambda\frac{\partial\mathcal{V}(x)}{\partial x_{e}}\right]\quad\text{for}\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\,.

(10.27)

Because
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{F}
are homogeneous polynomials of degrees
L
and
L+1
respectively,
then
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U}
is a rational function of degree
1
,
wherefore
\partial_{e}\mathcal{V}=\partial\mathcal{V}(x)/\partial x_{e}
has homogeneous degree
0
.
Thus,
(
10.27
) preserves projective symmetry.

The value of parameter
\lambda
is finite but small.
This is in contrast to the infinitesimal
\varepsilon
of the
i\varepsilon
-prescription.
As long as
\lambda
is is small enough that no poles of the integrand
\phi
are hit,
then Cauchy’s theorem guarantees that the deformation is valid.
Note that
\lambda
must have a mass dimension in order to cancel that of
\partial_{e}\mathcal{V}
in the exponential.
In practice,
we shall choose
\lambda
to be of order
\mathcal{O}(1/\Lambda^{2})
,
where
\Lambda^{2}
is the largest kinematic scale in
\mathcal{V}
.

To see why (
10.27
) is consistent with the
i\varepsilon
-prescription,
let us consider the
Schwinger representation
of a FI
[
44
, Section 2.5.2]
:

\displaystyle I=\int_{0}^{\infty}\ldots\exp\left[i\left(-\mathcal{V}(x)+i\varepsilon\sum_{e\in E}x_{e}\right)\right]\,.

(10.28)

Here we ignored factors in the integrand which are irrelevant for the present argument.
Observe that the
i\varepsilon
term cures the divergence at
x_{e}\to\infty
by gifting an exponential damping

\sim\exp\left[-\varepsilon x_{e}\right].

Notice that a similar damping is achieved by adding a

positive
imaginary part to the first term
-\mathcal{V}(x)
.
In that case the
i\varepsilon
could be dropped.
Indeed,
changing coordinates according to (
10.27
),
then a Taylor expansion in
\lambda
shows that

\displaystyle-\mathcal{V}(X)=-\mathcal{V}(x)+i\lambda\sum_{e\in E}x_{e}\left[\frac{\partial\mathcal{V}(x)}{\partial x_{e}}\right]^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})\,.

(10.29)

Assuming that 1) there are no solutions to the
Landau equations

\displaystyle x_{e}\frac{\partial\mathcal{V}(x)}{\partial x_{e}}=0\quad\text{for each}\quad e\in E\quad\text{and for any}\quad x\in\mathbb{P}^{|E|-1}_{+}\,,

(10.30)

and 2)
\lambda
is small enough such that the
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
terms to not change the sign
of the
\mathcal{O}(\lambda)
term,
then (
10.29
) has a positive imaginary part,
as desired.
For the remaining part of this thesis,
it is assumed that the Landau equations have no solutions.

A short calculation shows that the
|E|\times|E|

Jacobian matrix associated to (
10.27
) is

\displaystyle(\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}(x))_{eh}=\delta_{eh}-i\lambda x_{e}\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{V}(x)}{\partial x_{e}\partial x_{h}}\quad\text{for}\quad e,h\in E\,.

(10.31)

Coming back to the projective FI (
10.20
),
its deformed version now reads

\displaystyle I_{G}=\Gamma(\omega)\int_{\mathbb{P}^{|E|-1}_{+}}\left(\prod_{e\in E}\frac{X_{e}^{\nu_{e}}}{\Gamma(\nu_{e})}\right)\frac{\det\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}(x)}{\mathcal{U}(X)^{\mathrm{D}/2}\ \times\ \mathcal{V}(X)^{\omega}}\,\Omega\,,

(10.32)

where

X=(X_{0},\ldots,X_{|E|-1})

with
X_{e}
given by (
10.27
).

10.2.2
\epsilon
-expansion for quasi-finite integrals

We have so far not made any assumptions regarding the finiteness of the FI
(
10.32
)
as the DR parameter
\epsilon
tends to zero.
Recall that
\epsilon
appears inside

\mathrm{D}=\mathrm{D}_{0}-2\epsilon

and

\omega=\sum_{e\in E}\nu_{e}-\mathrm{D}L/2

in this representation.

We shall restrict our attention to the category of integrals dubbed as

quasi-finite
in
[
190
]
.
This means that
I_{G}
in (
10.32
) can have overall
\epsilon
-poles coming
from the prefactor
\Gamma(\omega)
,
but the integral
\int_{\mathbb{P}^{|E|-1}_{+}}(\ldots)
is itself finite as
\epsilon\to 0
.
Note that this is less restrictive than requiring the whole FI to be finite.

Given this,
the
\epsilon
-expansion of
I_{G}
is derived by merely Taylor expanding the integrand.
Writing

\omega_{0}=\sum_{e\in E}\nu_{e}-\mathrm{D}_{0}L/2,

the expansion becomes

\displaystyle I_{G}

\displaystyle=\Gamma(\omega_{0}+\epsilon L)\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\epsilon^{k}}{k!}\ \times

(10.33)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{P}^{|E|-1}_{+}}\left(\prod_{e\in E}\frac{X_{e}^{\nu_{e}}}{\Gamma(\nu_{e})}\right)\frac{\det\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}(x)}{\mathcal{U}(X)^{\mathrm{D}_{0}/2}\ \times\ \mathcal{V}(X)^{\omega_{0}}}\log^{k}\left[\frac{\mathcal{U}(X)}{\mathcal{V}(X)^{L}}\right]\Omega\,.

If the integral is finite for
k=0
,
then the
\log^{k}
factors cannot spoil the convergence for higher orders in
k
.

Our ultimate goal is to numerically evaluate the integrals appearing in the
\epsilon
-expansion above.
To this end,
it is important that the tropical approximation property (
10.15
) holds true,
but this is now complicated by the fact that
X
depends on
\lambda
.
So we make the following
assumption
:
there exist
\lambda
-dependent constants
C_{1}(\lambda),C_{2}(\lambda)>0
such that for small enough
\lambda>0
,

\displaystyle C_{1}(\lambda)\ \leq\ \left|\left(\frac{\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)}{\mathcal{U}(X)}\right)^{\mathrm{D}_{0}/2}\left(\frac{\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)}{\mathcal{V}(X)}\right)^{\omega_{0}}\right|\ \leq\ C_{2}(\lambda)\quad\text{for all}\quad x\in\mathbb{P}^{|E|-1}_{+}\,.

(10.34)

We have checked this assumption through extensive numerical testing,
but lack a mathematical argument for its validity.

The statement (
10.34
) suggest an optimal value of
\lambda
:
choose it such that the bounds are as tight as possible.
This is an interesting research direction,
as it would constitute the first
canonical
contour deformation without any free parameters.

10.2.3 Convergence of Euler integrals

It was proven by Berkesch, Forsgård and Passare
that a quasi-finite representation can always be found
[
191
, Theorem 2.4]
.
The proof of this theorem gives a concrete prescription for factoring out
\epsilon
-poles in front of
finite integrals,
but we have not yet implemented this protocol
3
3

3

Their method requires expanding out derivatives of
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
,
which can lead to a huge proliferation of terms if done symbolically.
This problem is naturally avoided in the setting of numerical MC integration.
Indeed,
we shall provide efficient formulas in Section (
10.3.3
)
for derivatives of
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
that could be used in a future implementation.

.

Assuming a quasi-finite basis of MIs is known,
one can alternatively employ a combination of dimensional recurrence relations and IBPs
as in
[
190
]
to write a given FI as linear combination of said basis.

To find a quasi-finite basis,
one ought to have test for finiteness of Euler integrals
(in contrast to the GKZ setting studied earlier in this thesis,
we are now allowing the monomial coefficients of the Euler integrands
to be fixed to special values).
Convergence theorems for Euler integrals were first discovered in the mathematics literature by
Berkesch, Forsgård, Nilsson and Passare

[
192
, Theorem 1]

[
191
, Theorem 2.2]
.
In the physics literature,
convergence criteria were later established by Panzer, Schabinger and von Manteuffel in

[
190
]
,
followed by works of Arkani-Hamed et al.
[
193
, Claim 1]

and Borinsky

[
40
, Theorem 3]
.

The most general, proven statement is roughly as follows.
Suppose

\{f_{1}(x),\ldots,f_{l}(x)\}

are completely non-vanishing polynomials on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{>0}

(an equivalent statement can be formulated in projective space).
Let

s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{l})\in\mathbb{C}^{l}

and

\nu=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}

be complex parameters.
Assume
\mathrm{Re}(s_{i})>0
.
The integral

\displaystyle I=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{>0}}\frac{x_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\nu_{n}}}{f_{1}^{s_{1}}\cdots f_{l}^{s_{l}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x}

(10.35)

is convergent iff
4
4

4

An additional, weak assumption is that the Minkowski sum of Newton polytopes

\mathbf{N}[f_{1}]+\ldots+\mathbf{N}[f_{l}]

should have dimension
n
.

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{Re}(\nu_{1})\\
\vdots\\
\mathrm{Re}(\nu_{n})\end{array}\right]\ \in\ \mathrm{int}(\mathbf{N}(s))\quad\text{where}\quad

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}(s)=\mathrm{Re}(s_{1})\cdot\mathbf{N}[f_{1}]+\cdots+\mathrm{Re}(s_{l})\cdot\mathbf{N}[f_{l}]\,.

•

The notation

s\cdot\mathbf{N}[f]

means that the Newton polytope associated to
f
is rescaled by a constant

s\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
When
\mathbf{N}[f]
is determined by a collection half-spaces

\{v_{i}\cdot x\leq c_{i}\},

then
s\cdot\mathbf{N}[f]
has the effect of rescaling the
c
’s,
that is

\{v_{i}\cdot x\leq s\,c_{i}\}.

•

The
Minkowski sum
of two polytopes
\mathbf{N}[f_{A}]
and
\mathbf{N}[f_{B}]
is given by

\mathbf{N}[f_{A}]+\mathbf{N}[f_{B}]=\big\{a+b\,\big|\,a\in\mathbf{N}[f_{A}]\ \text{and}\ b\in\mathbf{N}[f_{B}]\big\}.

•

A point
x
is in the
interior
of
\mathbf{N}(s)
,

x\in\mathrm{int}(\mathbf{N}(s))
,
if there exists a small ball
(w.r.t. the Euclidean metric)
centered at
x
that is completely contained in
\mathbf{N}(s)
.

The condition (
10.2.3
) can be checked in practice as follows.
First compute the half-space representation of
\mathbf{N}(s)

(for example with
sage
or
polymake
).
This gives a set of inequalities of the form

\{v\cdot x\leq c\}

for the hyperplanes that cut out
\mathbf{N}(s)
.
For FIs we have
s_{1}=\mathrm{D}/2
and
s_{2}=\omega
,
so these inequalities would depend parametrically on the DR parameter
\epsilon
.
A vector

v=\left[\mathrm{Re}(\nu_{1}),\ldots,\mathrm{Re}(\nu_{n})\right]^{T}

is then in the interior of
\mathbf{N}(s)
if the inequalities are strict,
namely

\{v\cdot x<c\}.

This suggests the following heuristic algorithm for determining a quasi-finite basis:
check whether the criterion (
10.2.3
) is fulfilled during a
bottom-up search in the space
(\nu,\mathrm{D})
of propagator powers and spacetime dimensions
5
5

5

The search space for
\nu
is cut down drastically in case
any
other basis is known,
so as to pinpoint a list of relevant integral sectors.

.

We remark that the alternative quasi-finite basis search
algorithm from
[
190
]
has been
automated in
Reduze2

[
12
]
via the command
find_finite_masters
.

Section 10.3
Tropical Monte Carlo sampling, in detail

We have already briefly explained the idea behind tropical MC sampling in Section (
10.1
).
This algorithm is here described in detail.
The goal is to tropically integrate the terms in the
\epsilon
-expansion (
10.33
),
so let us factorize the integrals in a way analogous to (
10.1.1
):

\displaystyle I_{G}=I^{\mathrm{tr}}\ \frac{\Gamma(\omega_{0}+\epsilon L)}{\prod_{e\in E}\Gamma(\nu_{e})}\ \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\epsilon^{k}}{k!}I^{(k)}\,,

(10.39)

where the
k
th order integral reads as

\displaystyle I^{(k)}=\int_{\mathbb{P}^{|E|-1}_{+}}\frac{\det\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}(x)\ \times\ \prod_{e\in E}(X_{e}/x_{e})^{\nu_{e}}}{\big(\mathcal{U}(X)/\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)\big)^{\mathrm{D}_{0}/2}\ \times\ \big(\mathcal{V}(X)/\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)\big)^{\omega_{0}}}\log^{k}\left[\frac{\mathcal{U}(X)}{\mathcal{V}(X)^{L}}\right]\ \times\ \mu^{\mathrm{tr}}\,,

(10.40)

and the
tropical probability measure
is

\displaystyle\mu^{\mathrm{tr}}=\frac{1}{I^{\mathrm{tr}}}\ \frac{\prod_{e\in E}x_{e}^{\nu_{e}}}{\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)^{\mathrm{D}_{0}/2}\ \times\ \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)^{\omega_{0}}}\ \Omega\,.

(10.41)

The normalization factor
I^{\mathrm{tr}}
ensures that

\int_{\mathbb{P}^{|E|-1}_{+}}\mu^{\mathrm{tr}}=1

(a formula for
I^{\mathrm{tr}}
is given further down in (
10.51
)).

The idea of tropical MC is to sample
N
points

\{x^{(1)},\ldots,x^{(N)}\}

from the measure
\mu^{\mathrm{tr}}
,
then evaluate the integrand of (
10.40
) at those points,
and finally take the average of these evaluations.
Borinsky introduced two different algorithms that sample from
\mu^{\mathrm{tr}}
in
[
40
]
.
The first algorithm
[
40
, Section 5]

works for any projective integral over rational functions.
A key step in the algorithm requires the triangulation of a certain
normal fans
6
6

6

A normal fan is essentially a collection of cones that
can be associated to the normal directions of each face of a given polytope

[
186
]
.

associated to the Newton polytopes of
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{F}
.
The computation of such triangulations unfortunately
becomes a bottleneck when a Feynman diagram has many edges.

The second algorithm
[
40
, Section 6]

takes special features of
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{F}
into account,
thereby avoiding the triangulation step.
Namely,
the Newton polytope of
\mathcal{U}
is always a so-called
generalized permutahedron
,
and the same holds true for the Newton polytope of
\mathcal{F}
given sufficiently generic kinematics.
The triangulation step of the previous algorithm gets traded for a much simpler preprocessing step,
involving the calculation of a certain recursive function called
J(\gamma)
below.

10.3.1 Generalized permutahedra

Let
E
be any finite set.
Consider a function
7
7

7

The notation
2^{E}
stands for the power set of
E
.

z:2^{E}\to\mathbb{R}

that assigns a number to each subset
\gamma
of
E
.
Given such a
z
,
the
base polytope

\mathbf{P}[z]\subset\mathbb{R}^{|E|}
is defined by the set of points

(a_{0},\ldots,a_{|E|-1})\in\mathbb{R}^{|E|}

satisfying

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in E}a_{e}

\displaystyle=z(E)\quad\text{and}\quad

(10.42)

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in\gamma}a_{e}

\displaystyle\geq z(\gamma)\quad\text{for all}\quad\gamma\subsetneq E\,.

The function
z
is called
supermodular
if it satisfies the inequalities

\displaystyle z(\gamma)+z(\delta)\ \leq\ z(\gamma\,\cap\,\delta)+z(\gamma\,\cup\,\delta)\quad\text{for all pairs of subsets}\quad\gamma,\delta\subset E\,.

(10.43)

Given a supermodular function
z
,
Aguiar and Ardila proved that the base polytope
\mathbf{P}[z]
is always a

generalized permutahedron
(GP)
[
194
, Theorem 12.3]

(as a shorthand, we shall often say that a polytope "has the GP property", or simply "is GP").
This is a wonderful class of polytopes defined by Postnikov in
[
195
]
.
For the purposes of this discussion,
we shall simply
define
a GP to a supermodular base polytope.
To build some intuition for these polytopes,
let us nevertheless give a geometric description of Postnikov’s original construction.
To start,
define the
standard permutahedron
by

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}=\texttt{ConvexHull}\!\left[\text{permutations of}\ (1,\ldots,n)\right]\,\subset\,\mathbb{R}^{n}\,.

(10.44)

In words,
we take the convex hull in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of the vector

\mathrm{v}=(1,\ldots,n)

and all its
n!
permutations

\mathrm{v}_{\sigma}=(\sigma(1),\ldots,\sigma(n)).

Equivalently,
each permutation of
\mathrm{v}
corresponds to a vertex.
The dimension of
\Pi_{n}
is
n-1
rather than
n

because its points are all constrained to lie on the hyperplane

1+2+\ldots+n=n(n+1)/2.

Postnikov then defines a GP as a special deformation of a standard permutahedron:
one is allowed to move any facet along its normal ray as long as it does not pass through any vertices.
Equivalently,
one can move any vertex as long as the directions are preserved.
Examples of generalized permutahedra are shown in Figure (
10.2
).

Figure 10.2
:

(I)

The standard permutahedron
\Pi_{3}
. Each vertex is labeled by a permutation of
(1,2,3)
.
Since the vector components
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
of each vertex satisfies
x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}=6
,
the dimension of the polytope drops from 3 to 2.

(II)

Deforming the two red vertices while preserving edge direction gives a GP.

(III)

Continuing to deform the vertices creates another GP.
A facet has now degenerated to a point.

GP property of
\mathcal{U}

Let us now see how this connects to FIs.
Take the set
E
to be the edge set of a Feynman graph
G
.
A subset of
E
corresponds to a subgraph
\gamma
of
G
,
so
z
can be thought of as a collection of numbers for each subgraph of
G
.
The integer
L_{\gamma}
denotes the number of loops of
\gamma
.
It was shown by Schultka
[
196
]
that the function

\displaystyle z_{\mathcal{U}}(\gamma)=L_{\gamma}

(10.45)

is supermodular,
and Newton polytope
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{U}]
of the
\mathcal{U}
-polynomial equals the base polytope
\mathbf{P}[z_{\mathcal{U}}]
.
According to the theorem by Aguiar and Ardila,
this means that
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{U}]
is GP.

GP property of
\mathcal{F}

The situation is much more complicated for the
\mathcal{F}
-polynomial.
Following Brown
[
197
]
,
call a subgraph
\gamma\subset E

mass-momentum spanning

if the
\mathcal{F}
-polynomial restricted to the cograph
G\setminus\gamma
vanishes identically,

\mathcal{F}_{G\setminus\gamma}=0
.
Since the
\mathcal{F}
-polynomial contains all the kinematic scales (
s,t,m^{2}
etc.) of a given FI,
an equivalent definition is to say that
\mathcal{F}_{G\setminus\gamma}
is scaleless.

We further define the notion of
generic kinematics
.
Consider the
\mathcal{F}
-polynomial of a 1-loop bubble graph with two different internal masses:

\mathcal{F}_{\text{bubble}}(x)=(p^{2}-m_{0}^{2}-m_{1}^{2})x_{0}x_{1}+m_{0}^{2}x_{0}^{2}+m_{1}^{2}x_{1}^{2}.

Notice that if the kinematics are tuned to enforce
p^{2}-m_{0}^{2}-m_{1}^{2}=0
,
then the first monomial drops out.
Special choices of kinematics can therefore change the Newton polytope
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}]
,
as its vertices stem from the monomial exponents of
\mathcal{F}
.
The question is now how to avoid this degeneration of
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}]
by
setting a criterion which prevents cancellation among momentum and mass terms in
\mathcal{F}
.
To this end,
begin by recalling the scalar product matrix
\mathcal{P}_{uv}=p_{u}\cdot p_{v}
,
where
u,v\in V
are vertices of
G
.
Call a vertex
v

internal
if
\mathcal{P}_{uv}=0
for all
u\in V
and
external
otherwise.
We then say that a kinematic configuration is
generic
if

\displaystyle\sum_{u,v\,\in\,V^{\prime}}\mathcal{P}_{uv}\ \neq\ \sum_{e\,\in\,E^{\prime}}m_{e}^{2}

(10.46)

for each proper subset of external vertices
V^{\prime}\subsetneq V_{\text{ext}}

and each non-empty subset of edges
E^{\prime}\subset E
.
When the kinematics are not generic,
they are called
exceptional
.
For example,
kinematics are always generic in the Euclidean regime if
1)
m_{e}>0
for all
e\in E
,
or 2)
\sum_{u,v\,\in\,V^{\prime}}\mathcal{P}_{uv}<0
for all
V^{\prime}\subsetneq V_{\text{ext}}
.
See
[
185
, Section 2.2]
for additional details.

Now,
assuming generic kinematics,
it was shown by Schultka
[
196
]
8
8

8

Schultka technically proved this theorem with kinematics in the Euclidean regime.
The same result also holds in the Minkowski regime
because the Newton polytopes coincide in both regimes given the genericity assumption.

that the function

\displaystyle z_{\mathcal{F}}=\begin{cases}L_{\gamma}+1&\text{if $\gamma$ is mass-momentum spanning}\\
L_{\gamma}&\text{else}\,.\end{cases}

(10.47)

is supermodular,
and the Newton polytope
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}]
equals the base polytope
\mathbf{P}[z_{\mathcal{F}}]
.
Therefore,
for generic kinematics,
the Newton polytope
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}]
is GP.
There are reasons to expect that
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}]
is also GP even for
exceptional kinematics in the Euclidean regime
[
185
, Conjecture 3.7]
.

It should be emphasized that
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}]
is generally
not
GP outside of the Euclidean regime.
Indeed,
the polytopes for three box diagrams in Figures
(
10.3(a)
), (
10.3(b)
) and (
10.3(c)
)
are
not
GP.
The GP property
is
fulfilled for the polytope associated to the diagram
(
10.3(d)
)
though.

The story is further complicated by an interesting observation of Arkani-Hamed, Hillman and Mizera

[
36
, Section IV]
:
even for exceptional kinematics,
the Newton polytope of
\mathcal{F}
is often equal to the base polytope
\mathbf{P}[z_{\mathcal{F}}]

with
z_{\mathcal{F}}
given by (
10.47
)!
In fact,
for all four examples in Figure (
10.3
) we have that

\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}]=\mathbf{P}[z_{\mathcal{F}}].

This is expected for the polytope of the last graph (
10.3(d)
) since it is GP,
but how is this consistent with the fact that the first three cases
(
10.3(a)
), (
10.3(b)
) and (
10.3(c)
)
are not GP?
The answer is that
z_{\mathcal{F}}
is not supermodular for those graphs.
So the function
z_{\mathcal{F}}
does
not
fulfil
the inequalities (
10.43
) for the first three boxes,
but it
does
so for the last box.

(a)

On-shell box.

Not GP.

(b)

One off-shell leg.

Not GP.

(c)

Two off-shell legs (adjacent). Not GP.

(d)

Two off-shell legs (crossed). Is GP.

Figure 10.3
:

1-loop box diagrams with massless internal propagators and different combinations
of on- or off-shell external legs.
On-shell (
p^{2}=0
) legs are depicted by dashed lines,
and off-shell (
p^{2}\neq 0
) legs are drawn as solid lines.
In all four cases we have
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}]=\mathbf{P}[z_{\mathcal{F}}]
,
but only (
10.3(d)
) is GP.

While
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}]=\mathbf{P}[z_{\mathcal{F}}]
for generic kinematics,
we are not aware of a set of necessary and sufficient conditions that specify
when
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}]
equals
\mathbf{P}[z_{\mathcal{F}}]
for exceptional kinematics.
It is at least the case that
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}]\subset\mathbf{P}[z_{\mathcal{F}}]
,
because we can only lose monomials in the exceptional case.
A counter example
9
9

9

We thank Erik Panzer for sharing this example with us.

to equality is given by the following 1-loop triangle:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\text{triangle}}=\mathcal{F}\Big(\parbox[c]{75.5864pt}{\includegraphics[]{Figs/1L_exceptional_triangle.pdf}}\Big)=m^{2}(x_{0}^{2}+x_{1}^{2})+(2m^{2}-p^{2})x_{0}x_{1}\,.

(10.48)

The dashed line is massless,
the double line has off-shell momentum
p^{2}\neq 0
,
and every single solid line has mass
m
.
This is an exceptional kinematic configuration.
The Newton polytope of
\mathcal{F}_{\text{triangle}}
is the convex hull of the points

(2,0),(0,2)
and
(1,1)
.
Since they all lie on a line,
the dimension of
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}_{\text{triangle}}]
equals 1.
However,
the base polytope
\mathbf{P}[z_{\mathcal{F}_{\text{triangle}}}]
can be shown to have dimension 2.
Hence

\mathbf{P}[z_{\mathcal{F}_{\text{triangle}}}]\neq\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}_{\text{triangle}}].

Why is this discussion relevant for tropical MC sampling?
The answer is that the sampling algorithm of
Feyntrop
assumes that
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}]
is GP.
Curiously,
although we have highlighted the complications with this assumption above,
it appears not to matter that much in practice.
Indeed,
by evaluating the examples from this section with
Feyntrop
(i.e. quasi-finite versions of the four boxes in Figure (
10.3
) and the triangle from
(
10.48
)),
we find agreement with other numerical codes in all cases.
The catch is that numerical convergence for
Feyntrop
becomes slower in the non-GP cases.
We suspect that
Feyntrop
will fail for more complicated diagrams with exceptional kinematics,
but we did not yet find such an example.

10.3.2 Tropical sampling algorithm

Euclidean

Minkowski

Generic

no deformation / always GP

deformation / always GP

Exceptional

no deformation / always GP

deformation / not always GP

Table 10.1
:

Summary of whether contour deformation is required
and whether the GP property of
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}]
is fulfilled for each kinematic regime.

Feyntrop
employs a modification of
[
40
, Algorithm 4]
to sample from the tropical measure

\mu^{\mathrm{tr}}
given in (
10.41
),
under the assumption that
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}]
is GP.
The algorithm includes a preprocessing step,
to be performed once,
followed by a sampling step.
The preprocessing step scales as
\mathcal{O}(n2^{n})
,
where
n=|E|
is the number of edges in a Feynman diagram.
This scaling is determined from the preparation of a certain
table of rational numbers
J(\gamma)
for each subgraph
\gamma\subset E
.
The sampling step scales as
\mathcal{O}\left(n^{2}\delta^{-2}\right)
,
where
\delta
is the demanded relative accuracy of the result.
This is just ordinary MC scaling.

Because of the preprocessing step,
the time complexity of this algorithm is not fully polynomial.
It is a very interesting question whether a polynomial
time algorithm exists for the numerical evaluation of FIs.

Preprocessing

The first task is to determine the kinematic regime,
namely Euclidean vs. Minkowski and generic vs. exceptional.

The regime is Euclidean if the scalar product matrix
P_{uv}
is negative semi-definite,
and Minkowski otherwise.
If the kinematics are in the Minkowski regime,
then contour deformation needs to be switched on.

If the kinematic point is exceptional and Minkowski,
then
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}]
might not be GP,
and it might not even be equal to
\mathbf{P}[z_{\mathcal{F}}]
.
In this case,

Feyntrop
prints a warning saying that the integration might give the wrong result
(though, as mentioned, we have not yet witnessed erroneous numerics).
The program then bravely continues under the assumption that
\mathbf{N}[\mathcal{F}]=\mathbf{P}[z_{\mathcal{F}}]
.
For generic kinematics,
regardless of whether we are in the Euclidean or Minkowski regimes,
the sampling algorithm is guaranteed to converge to the correct result

[
40
, Proposition 31]
.

A summary of the preceding paragraphs is given in Table (
10.1
).
For brevity,
we have left out a technicality in that table regarding the Euclidean regime:
there is in fact a larger regime,
containing the Euclidean one,
called the
pseudo-Euclidean regime
;
it does not require contour deformation,
but might witness a failure of the GP property for exceptional kinematics.
See
[
185
, Section 2.2]
for details.

The next task is to prepare data associated to each of the
2^{|E|}
subgraphs
\gamma\subset E
:

1.

We calculate the loop number

L_{\gamma}=|\gamma|-|V_{\gamma}|+1,

where
V_{\gamma}
is the vertex set associated to
\gamma
.

2.

We check whether or not
\gamma
is mass-momentum spanning,
i.e. whether
\mathcal{F}_{G\setminus\gamma}=0
.
By definition,

\delta_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{m.m}}=1
if
\gamma
is mass-momentum spanning and

\delta_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{m.m}}=0
otherwise.

3.

We calculate the
generalized degree of divergence

[
40
, Section 7.2]

\displaystyle\omega(\gamma)=\sum_{e\in\gamma}\nu_{e}-\mathrm{D}_{0}L_{\gamma}/2-\omega_{0}\,\delta_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{m.m}}\,,

(10.49)

where
\mathrm{D}_{0}
is the integer part of the spacetime dimension and

\omega_{0}=\sum_{e\in E}\nu_{e}-\mathrm{D}_{0}L/2.

If
\omega(\gamma)\leq 0
for any proper subgraph (i.e. not the whole graph
\gamma=E
),
then
G
has a subdivergence.
It implies that every term of the
\epsilon
-expansion (
10.33
) is divergent,
wherefore
Feyntrop
prints an error and terminates.
In this case,
one has to shift the integrand exponents
(\nu,\mathrm{D})
to obtain a quasi-finite integral
(cf. Section (
10.2.3
)).

4.

If
\omega(\gamma)>0
for all
\gamma\subset E
,
then we
recursively
compute a table of numbers
J(\gamma)
via the formulas

\displaystyle J(\emptyset)

\displaystyle=\omega(\emptyset)=1

\displaystyle J(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in\gamma}\frac{J(\gamma\setminus e)}{\omega(\gamma\setminus e)}\quad\text{for all}\quad\gamma\subset E\,,

(10.50)

where
\gamma\setminus e
means that we delete the edge
e
from
\gamma
.
The last step of the recursion is for
\gamma=E
.
In
[
40
, Proposition 29]
,
Borinsky proved the surprising identity

\displaystyle J(E)=I^{\mathrm{tr}}\,,

(10.51)

where
I^{\mathrm{tr}}
is the tropical normalization factor from (
10.41
).
The rational numbers
J(\gamma)
are intimately related to the values
z_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma)

[
40
, Section 6.1]
.
Indeed,
this is the step during preprocessing wherein the GP assumption is employed.

The data
(L_{\gamma},\,\delta_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{m.m}})
feeds into the computation of
z_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma)
via (
10.47
).
For an exceptional kinematic point in the Minkowski regime,

Feyntrop
checks whether
z_{\mathcal{F}}
is supermodular by testing a
(more efficient)
variant of the inequalities (
10.43
),
see
[
185
, Equation (26)]
.

Feyntrop
prints a message if the inequalities are satisfied to notify that
\mathbf{P}[z_{\mathcal{F}}]
is GP,
and a warning otherwise.

The output of this preprocessing is the data

\bigcup_{\gamma\subset E}\big\{L_{\gamma},\,\delta_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{m.m}},\,\omega(\gamma),\,J(\gamma)\big\}\bigcup\big\{I^{\mathrm{tr}}\big\},

which is stored in computer memory.

MC sampling

The validity of this tropical sampling algorithm was proven in

[
40
, Proposition 31]
.

Initialize the variables
\gamma=E
and
\kappa=U=1
.

1:

while

\gamma\neq\emptyset

do

2:

Pick a random edge
e\in\gamma
with probability

p_{e}^{\gamma}=\frac{1}{J(\gamma)}\frac{J(\gamma\setminus e)}{\omega(\gamma\setminus e)}.

3:

Set
x_{e}=\kappa
.

4:

If
\gamma
is mass-momentum spanning but
\gamma\setminus e
is not, set
V=x_{e}
.

5:

If
L_{\gamma\setminus e}<L_{\gamma}
, multiply
U
with
x_{e}
and store the result in
U
, i.e. set
U\leftarrow x_{e}\cdot U
.

6:

Remove the edge
e
from
\gamma
, i.e. set
\gamma\leftarrow\gamma\setminus e
.

7:

Pick a uniformly distributed random number
\xi\in[0,1]
.

8:

Multiply
\kappa
with
\xi^{1/\omega(\gamma)}
and store the result in
\kappa
, i.e. set
\kappa\leftarrow\kappa\,\xi^{1/\omega(\gamma)}
.

9:

end

while

return

x=[x_{0},\ldots,x_{|E|-1}]\in\mathbb{P}^{|E|-1}_{+}
,
\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)=U
and
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)=V
.

Algorithm 6

Generating a sample
x
distributed as
\mu^{\mathrm{tr}}
from (
10.41
)

The main idea of this algorithm is to interpret
p_{e}^{\gamma}
as a probability distribution
over the edges
e
of a given subgraph
\gamma
.
Indeed,
it follows from the definition of
J(\gamma)
in (
10.50
) that

p_{e}^{\gamma}\geq 0

and

\sum_{e\in\gamma}p_{e}^{\gamma}=1.

Starting from
\gamma=E
,
the algorithm proceeds to
cut
an edge
e
of the graph
G
with probability
p_{e}^{\gamma}
.
This yields a new graph
\gamma\setminus e
with the edge removed.
The cutting process repeats until all edges are gone.

We refer to
[
40
, Section 6.1]
for explanations on why Algorithm (
6
)
yields correct values for the triple
\big\{x,\,\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{tr}}(x),\,\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)\big\}
.

10.3.3 Evaluating deformed Feynman integrands

Algorithm (
6
) explains how to efficiently sample points
x
from
\mu^{\mathrm{tr}}
,
but it does not take care of all the extra factors in the integrand of (
10.33
)
that arose due to contour deformation.
In particular,
we would like to efficiently evaluate the factors

X_{e}=x_{e}\exp\left[-i\lambda\partial_{e}\mathcal{V}(x)\right],\,\mathcal{U}(X),\,\mathcal{V}(X)

and
\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}(x)
for any
x\in\mathbb{P}^{|E|-1}_{+}
.

The non-trivial step in evaluating
X_{e}
is the derivative
\partial_{e}\mathcal{V}(x)
.
Moreover,
the Jacobian
\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}(x)
includes the second-order derivative

\partial_{e}\partial_{h}\mathcal{V}(x)

according to (
10.31
).
For the sake of efficiency,
it would be nice to have formulas for these derivatives based only on linear algebra.
To this end,
begin by defining the
(|V|-1)\times(|V|-1)
matrix

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(x)=\mathcal{L}^{-1}(x)\cdot\mathcal{P}\cdot\mathcal{L}^{-1}(x)\,,

(10.52)

where the Laplacian matrix
\mathcal{L}(x)
and the scalar product matrix
\mathcal{P}
come from (
10.25
).
To avoid confusion,
we clarify that
\mathcal{L}^{-1}_{uv}
means the
(u,v)
th component of the inverse matrix
\mathcal{L}^{-1}
.
Let
e
and
h
be two edges that respectively connect the vertices

(u_{e},v_{e})\text{ and }(u_{h},v_{h}).

Furthermore,
define two matrices component-wise by

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llllllllll}\mathcal{A}_{eh}(x)&=&\dfrac{1}{x_{e}x_{h}}&\Big[\mathcal{M}_{u_{e}\,u_{h}}(x)&+&\mathcal{M}_{v_{e}\,v_{h}}(x)&-&\mathcal{M}_{u_{e}\,v_{h}}(x)&-&\mathcal{M}_{v_{e}\,u_{h}}(x)\Big]\\[9.0pt]
\mathcal{B}_{eh}(x)&=&\dfrac{1}{x_{e}x_{h}}&\Big[\mathcal{L}^{-1}_{u_{e}\,u_{h}}(x)&+&\mathcal{L}^{-1}_{v_{e}\,v_{h}}(x)&-&\mathcal{L}^{-1}_{u_{e}\,v_{h}}(x)&-&\mathcal{L}^{-1}_{v_{e}\,u_{h}}(x)\hskip 5.69046pt\Big]\,,\end{array}

with the convention that

\mathcal{L}^{-1}_{uv}(x)=\mathcal{M}_{uv}(x)=0

if either
u
or
v
equal the vertex
v_{0}
that was removed in the FI
(
10.19
)
by momentum conservation.
Using the identity

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{e}}\mathcal{L}^{-1}_{uv}(x)=\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}(x)\cdot\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}(x)}{\partial x_{e}}\cdot\mathcal{L}^{-1}(x)\right)_{uv}\,,

(10.55)

a tedious but straightforward calculation gives the sought after derivative formulas

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{V}(x)}{\partial x_{e}}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{A}_{ee}(x)+m_{e}^{2}

(10.56)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{V}(x)}{\partial x_{e}\partial x_{h}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\delta_{eh}\mathcal{A}_{ee}(x)}{x_{e}}-2\big(\mathcal{A}(x)\circ\mathcal{B}(x)\big)_{eh}\,,

(10.57)

where the last term denotes the element-wise matrix product (also called the Hadamard product):

\big(\mathcal{A}(x)\circ\mathcal{B}(x)\big)_{eh}=\mathcal{A}(x)_{eh}\times\mathcal{B}(x)_{eh}.

Summary

Let us summarize all the non-trivial steps that must be carried out to evaluate
the integrand of (
10.33
) at a single point.
Cholesky decomposition is used to evaluate determinants and inverses whenever possible,
that is when the matrix in question is Hermitian and positive semi-definite
(see the discussion around equation (
10.26
)).
When those two conditions are invalid,
the less efficient LU decomposition is opted for instead.

1.

Use Algorithm (
6
) to output the triple

\big\{x,\,\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{tr}}(x),\,\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)\big\}.

2.

Compute the Laplacian matrix
\mathcal{L}(x)
via (
10.23
).

3.

Compute the inverse
\mathcal{L}^{-1}(x)
via Cholesky decomposition.

4.

Use
\mathcal{L}(x)
and
\mathcal{L}^{-1}(x)
to evaluate derivatives of
\mathcal{V}(x)
using
(
10.56
) and (
10.57
).

5.

Compute the deformed
X
parameters by

X_{e}=x_{e}\exp\left[-i\lambda\partial_{e}\mathcal{V}(x)\right].

6.

Compute the Jacobian
\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}(x)
using (
10.31
).

7.

Evaluate
\det[\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}(x)]
using LU decomposition.

8.

Compute the deformed Laplacian
\mathcal{L}(X)
.

9.

Compute
\mathcal{L}^{-1}(X)
and
\det[\mathcal{L}(X)]
using LU decomposition.

10.

Employ the formulas (
10.25
) to evaluate

\mathcal{U}(X),\,\mathcal{F}(X)\text{ and }\mathcal{V}(X)=\mathcal{F}(X)/\mathcal{U}(X).

For a kinematic point in the Euclidean regime we are allowed to set
\lambda=0
implying
X=x
,
in which case
Feyntrop
only executes step 1.

Chapter 11
The Program
feyntrop

We have implemented the contour-deformed tropical integration algorithm described in the previous chapter
in a
C++
module named
Feyntrop
​​.
The module is equipped with a
python
interface.
The
C++
codebase is an upgrade to the one developed by Borinsky in
[
40
]
.

Feyntrop
relies on
Eigen3

[
198
]
for optimized linear algebra routines,
on
OpenMP

[
199
]
for parallelization of MC sampling,
and on
xoshiro256+

[
200
]
for pseudorandom number generation.

Feyntrop
has been verified against

AMFlow

[
34
]
and
pySecDec

[
176
]

for roughly 15 different 2- to 5-point Feynman diagrams between 1 and 3 loops at varying kinematic points,
both in the Euclidean and Minkowski regimes.
Numerical agreement was found in all cases within the given uncertainty bounds.
In the Euclidean regime,
the code was checked up to
\mathcal{O}(10)
loop-orders in
[
40
]
against analytic results
for conformal 4-point functions from
\varphi^{4}
-theory.

In this chapter we explain how to use the code,
and thereafter present state-of-the-art examples of numerical evaluations of FIs.

Section 11.1
Basic usage of
Feyntrop

Source code for
Feyntrop
is available in the
github
repository

\hrefhttps://github.com/michibo/feyntrop\,.

To download and install it,
the following commands can be run in a Linux terminal:

⬇

git

clone

https
://
github
.
com
/
michibo
/
feyntrop
.
git

cd

feyntrop

make

clean

&&

make

(It should also work in a
macOS
environment,
although
C++
compiler issues may ensue.
See the
README.txt
document on
github
).

Feyntrop
is loaded in a
python
environment by importing the file
py_feyntrop.py

located in the top directory.
To test whether the installation was successful,
the commands

⬇

cd

tests

python

test_suite
.
py

will compute six examples with 1-2 loops and 2-5 legs and compare the results with precomputed values.

The
python
interface serves three purposes:

1.

It simplifies the specification of vertices
V
and edges
E
of a Feynman diagram,
in comparison with the more verbose data structures of
C++
.

2.

It allows for self-chosen momentum variables given by a list of replacement rules,
rather than having to manually input the full matrix of scalar products

\mathcal{P}_{uv}

from (
10.25
).

3.

The numerical results for the
\epsilon
-expansion are printed in an easily readable format.

It is also possible to directly interface with the
C++
module via
.json
files.
See the
README.txt
document in the
github
repository for more details on this.

11.1.1 Tutorial

The basic workflow of
Feyntrop
is illustrated here by means of an example.
The code can be inspected with
jupyter

[
201
]
by calling

⬇

jupyter

notebook

tutorial_2L_3pt
.
ipynb

in the top directory of the
Feyntrop
package.
We will evaluate the 2-loop 3-point function shown in
Figure (
11.1
)
in
\mathrm{D}=2-2\epsilon
dimensions.

Figure 11.1
:
2-loop 3-point function in the
Feyntrop
tutorial.

The dashed lines represent massless on-shell external momenta:

p_{0}^{2}=p_{1}^{2}=0
.
The solid,
internal lines each have mass
m
.
The double line denotes an off-shell external momentum
p_{2}^{2}\neq 0
.
The four vertices

V=V_{\text{ext}}\sqcup V_{\text{int}}=\{0,1,2\}\sqcup\{3\}

and four edges

E=\{0,1,2,3\}

have been explicitly labeled.
The convention of
Feyntrop
requires one to label external vertices
before
internal ones.

By the conventions of (
10.19
),
we have the following momentum space representation with edge weights
\nu_{0}=\ldots=\nu_{4}=1
:

\displaystyle\pi^{-2+2\epsilon}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2-2\epsilon}\ell_{0}\,\mathrm{d}^{2-2\epsilon}\ell_{1}}{(q_{0}^{2}-m^{2}+i\varepsilon)(q_{1}^{2}-m^{2}+i\varepsilon)(q_{2}^{2}-m^{2}+i\varepsilon)(q_{3}^{2}-m^{2}+i\varepsilon)(q_{4}^{2}-m^{2}+i\varepsilon)}\,,

where the
\delta^{(D)}
-functions of
(
10.19
)
have been integrated out by setting

\displaystyle q_{0}=\ell_{0}\,,\quad q_{1}=\ell_{0}+p_{1}\,,\quad q_{2}=\ell_{0}+\ell_{1}+p_{1}\,,\quad q_{3}=p_{0}-\ell_{0}-\ell_{1}\,,\quad q_{4}=\ell_{1}\,.

(11.1)

We evaluate the integral at the phase space point

\displaystyle m^{2}=0.2\,,\quad p_{0}^{2}=p_{1}^{2}=0\,,\quad p_{2}^{2}=1\,,

(11.2)

which is in the Minkowski regime given that
p_{2}^{2}>0
.

The
Feyntrop
computation begins by importing
py_feyntrop.py
in a
python
environment:

⬇

from

py_feyntrop

import

*

Here it is assumed that the files

feyntrop.so
and
py_feyntrop.py

are both in the working directory.
The graph in Figure (
11.1
) is defined by providing a list of edges,
edge weights
\nu_{e}
and squared masses
m_{e}^{2}
in the format

\displaystyle\Big[\big(\big(u_{0},v_{0}\big)\,,\,\nu_{0}\,,\,m_{0}^{2}\big)\,,\,\ldots\,,\,\big(\big(u_{|E|-1},v_{|E|-1}\big)\,,\,\nu_{|E|-1}\,,\,m_{|E|-1}^{2}\big)\Big]\,.

(11.3)

The notation
(u_{e},\,v_{e})
means that there is an edge
e
connecting the vertices
u_{e}
and
v_{e}
.
The vertex ordering
(u_{e},\,v_{e})
is insignificant.
For the example at hand,
we thus write

⬇

edges

=

[((0,1),

1,

’
mm
’),

((1,3),

1,

’
mm
’),

((2,3),

1,

’
mm
’),

((2,0),

1,

’
mm
’),

((0,3),

1,

’
mm
’)]

The edge weights were here set to
\nu_{e}=1
for all
e
.
The chosen symbol
mm
stands for
m^{2}
,
and will be replaced by its value
0.2
later on
1
1

1

It is also allowed to input numerical values for the masses already in the
edges
list.
The first entry,
for instance,
would then be
((0,1), 1, ’0.2’)
.

.

The next step is to fix the momentum variables.
By the zero-indexing convention,
we have external momenta

\big\{p_{0},\ldots,p_{|V_{\text{ext}}|-1}\big\}.

But the last momentum
p_{|V_{\text{ext}}|-1}
is automatically inferred by
Feyntrop
using momentum conservation,
leaving

\big\{p_{0},\ldots,p_{|V_{\text{ext}}|-2}\big\}

to be fixed by the user.
A momentum configuration is then specified by providing a collection of scalar products

\displaystyle p_{u}\cdot p_{v}\quad\text{for all}\quad 0\leq u\leq v\leq|V_{\text{ext}}|-2\,.

(11.4)

In the code,
we must give a replacement rule for each scalar product in terms of some variables of choice.
In the present example there are
|V_{\text{ext}}|=3
external vertices,
so we ought to provide replacement rules for

\{p_{0}^{2},p_{1}^{2},p_{0}\cdot p_{1}\}.

We have
p_{0}^{2}=p_{1}^{2}=0
,
and by momentum conservation,

\displaystyle p_{2}^{2}=(-p_{0}-p_{1})^{2}=2p_{0}\cdot p_{1}\implies p_{0}\cdot p_{1}=p_{2}^{2}/2\,.

(11.5)

In the syntax of
Feyntrop
we then write

⬇

replacement_rules

=

[(
sp
[0,0],

’0’),

(
sp
[1,1],

’0’),

(
sp
[0,1],

’
pp2
/2’)]

The
s
calar
p
roduct between
p_{u}
and
p_{v}
is represented by the symbol
sp[u,v]
,
and
pp2
corresponds to
p_{2}^{2}
.
Since we will eventually substitute values for the two parameters
pp2
and
mm
,
we further define

⬇

phase_space_point

=

[(’
mm
’,

0.2),

(’
pp2
’,

1)]

which fixes
m^{2}=0.2
and
p_{2}^{2}=1
.

The final data to be provided are

⬇

D0

=

2

eps_order

=

5

Lambda

=

7.6

N

=

int
(1
e7
)

D0
is the integer part of
\mathrm{D}=\mathrm{D}_{0}-2\epsilon
.
We expand up to,
but not including,

eps_order
.

Lambda
is the contour deformation parameter in (
10.27
).

N
denotes the number of MC sampling points,
so increasing this value will yield better numerical accuracy
(the error on MC sampling scales as
\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\texttt{N}})
according to (
10.4
)).

Tropical Monte Carlo integration is finally performed by calling

⬇

trop_res
,

Itr

=

tropical_integration
(

N
,

D0
,

Lambda
,

eps_order
,

edges
,

replacement_rules
,

phase_space_point
)

If no error is printed,
then
trop_res
will contain the
\epsilon
-expansion (
10.33
)

without
the prefactor

\Gamma(\omega)/\big(\Gamma(\nu_{0})\cdots\Gamma(\nu_{|E|-1})\big)=\Gamma(2\epsilon+3).

Itr
is the value of the tropical normalization factor from (
10.51
).
Running this code on a laptop gives the output

⬇

Prefactor
:

gamma
(2*
eps

+

3).

(
Effective
)

kinematic

regime
:

Minkowski

(
generic
).

Generalized

permutahedron

property
:

fulfilled
.

Analytic

continuation
:

activated
.

Lambda

=

7.6

Started

integrating

using

8

threads

and

N

=

1
e
+07

points
.

Finished

in

6.00369

seconds

=

0.00166769

hours
.

--

eps
^0:

[-46.59

+/-

0.13]

+

i

*

[

87.19

+/-

0.12]

--

eps
^1:

[-274.46

+/-

0.55]

+

i

*

[111.26

+/-

0.55]

--

eps
^2:

[-435.06

+/-

1.30]

+

i

*

[-174.47

+/-

1.33]

--

eps
^3:

[-191.72

+/-

2.15]

+

i

[-494.69

+/-

2.14]

--

eps
^4:

[219.15

+/-

2.68]

+

i

*

[-431.96

+/-

2.67]

trop_res
contains the printed
\epsilon
-expansion coefficients in the format

\big[\left(\left(\mathrm{re}_{0},\,\sigma_{0}^{\mathrm{re}}\right)\,,\,\left(\mathrm{im}_{0},\,\sigma_{0}^{\mathrm{im}}\right)\right)\,,\,\ldots\,,\,\left(\left(\mathrm{re}_{4},\,\sigma_{4}^{\mathrm{re}}\right)\,,\,\left(\mathrm{im}_{4},\,\sigma_{4}^{\mathrm{im}}\right)\right)\big]\,,

where

\mathrm{re}_{0}\pm\sigma_{0}^{\mathrm{re}}

is the real part of the
0
th order term,
and so forth.
To obtain the
\epsilon
-expansion of the integral with the prefactor included,
one may call

⬇

eps_expansion
(
trop_res
,

edges
,

D0
)

which gives

⬇

174.3842115*
i

-

93.17486662

+

eps
*(-720.8731714

+

544.3677186*
i
)

+

eps
**2*(-2115.45025

+

496.490128*
i
)

+

eps
**3*(-3571.990969

-

677.5254794*
i
)

+

eps
**4*(-3872.475723

-

2726.965026*
i
)

+

O
(
eps
**5)

On the choice of
\lambda

A few comments are in order regarding the choice of the deformation parameter
\lambda

(what was called
Lambda
in the code).
The analytic expression for the integral (
10.32
) is independent of
\lambda

(so long as the deformed contour doesn’t hit any poles),
but the numerical error coming from MC sampling depends rather sensitively on its value.
Moreover,
the optimal value for
\lambda
will generally vary depending on the phase space point.
It is currently up to the user to find a suitable value by trial and error,
e.g. by performing several integrations with low sampling numbers
N
.
A heuristic starting value is
\mathcal{O}(1/\Lambda^{2})
,
where
\Lambda^{2}
is the largest kinematic scale in the
\mathcal{F}
-polynomial
(for instance some mass
m^{2}
).

In the future,
it would be beneficial to automate the choice of
\lambda
either by
1) minimizing the sampling variance
\sigma_{f}
,
given in (
10.4
),
by solving for
\lambda
in

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}\sigma_{f}=0\,,

(11.6)

or 2) by tightening the tropical approximation bounds in (
10.34
).

Section 11.2
Examples of Feynman integral evaluations

In this section,
we use
Feyntrop
to numerically evaluate many FIs of interest.
Each script is available in the
/examples
folder in the
gitub
repository.
When possible,
the results have been verified against
AMFlow
and/or
pySecDec
2
2

2

We thank Vitaly Magerya for help with verifying results using
pySecDec
.

.
The exception is the example from Section (
11.2.1
),
as we were not been able to re-compute these values with any other software in reasonable time -
though it might be possible now with the new release of
pySecDec

[
177
]
.

Each example can be computed on a laptop within a few minutes.
Though to properly showcase the potential of
Feyntrop
​​,
we shall only include results obtained from a high-performance machine:
a single
AMD EPYC 7H12
64-core processor using all cores.
With
N=10^{8}
MC sampling points each example only took a few seconds to finish.
The relative accuracies of the results fall between
10^{-2}
to
10^{-4}
.

The phase space points are chosen to be away from any kinematic thresholds to ensure numerical convergence.
In all but the last example, the
\epsilon
-expansion goes up to and including
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2L})
.

11.2.1 5-loop 2-point zigzag diagram

Here we evaluate a 5-loop 2-point function with all masses different in
\mathrm{D}=3-2\epsilon
dimensions:

The edge set is

⬇

edges

=

[((0,6),

1,

’1’)

,

((0,5),

1,

’2’),

((5,6),

1,

’3’),

((6,4),

1,

’4’)

,

((5,3),

1,

’5’),

((5,4),

1,

’6’),

((4,3),

1,

’7’)

,

((4,2),

1,

’8’),

((3,2),

1,

’9’),

((3,1),

1,

’10’),

((2,1),

1,

’11’)]

Above we already input the chosen mass values

\displaystyle m_{0}^{2}=1\,,\quad m_{1}^{2}=2\,,\quad\ldots,\quad m_{10}^{2}=11\,.

(11.7)

The remaining kinematic variable to be specified is the incoming momentum.
We set
p_{0}^{2}=100
in
Feyntrop
by writing

⬇

replacement_rules

=

[(
sp
[0,0],

’
pp0
’)]

=

[(’
pp0
’,

100)]

\lambda=0.02
turns out to yield small errors.
This is of order
\mathcal{O}(1/p_{0}^{2})
,
in accordance with the heuristic described near the end of the previous section.

MC sampling points,
the command

in

gives

(5*

11.2.2 3-loop 4-point envelope diagram

This example is a non-planar,
3
-loop
4
-point diagram in
\mathrm{D}=4-2\epsilon
dimensions:

The dots on the crossed lines represent squared propagators rather than vertices;
this choice of edge weights makes the diagram quasi-finite.
The edge data are

⬇

edges

=

[((0,1),

1,

’
mm0
’),

((1,2),

1,

’
mm1
’),

((2,3),

1,

’
mm2
’),

((3,0),

1,

’
mm3
’),

((0,2),

2,

’
mm4
’),

((1,3),

2,

’
mm5
’)]

Note that the edge weights
\nu_{4}
and
\nu_{5}
equal 2.

We define two-index Mandelstam variables by

s_{ij}=(p_{i}+p_{j})^{2}.

They are input into replacement rules for
Feyntrop
by writing

(sp[i,j], ’(sij - ppi - ppj)/2’)

with

0\leq i\leq j\leq 2.

The chosen phase space point is

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cccccccccc}&p_{0}^{2}&=&1.1\,,&p_{1}^{2}&=&1.2\,,&p_{2}^{2}&=&1.3\,,\\
&s_{01}&=&2.1\,,&s_{02}&=&2.2\,,&s_{12}&=&2.3\,,\\
&m_{0}^{2}&=&0.05\,,&m_{1}^{2}&=&0.06\,,&m_{2}^{2}&=&0.07\,,\\
&m_{3}^{2}&=&0.08\,,&m_{4}^{2}&=&0.09\,,&m_{5}^{2}&=&0.1\,.\end{array}

Setting
\lambda=1.24
and
N=10^{8}
,
we get the result

⬇

Prefactor
:

gamma
(3*
eps

+

2).

(
Effective
)

kinematic

regime
:

Minkowski

(
generic
).

Finished

in

5.12

seconds
.

11.2.3 2-loop 4-point
\mu e
-scattering diagram

We evaluate a non-planar,
2
-loop
4
-point diagram that contributes to muon-electron scattering.
It is quasi-finite in
\mathrm{D}=6-2\epsilon
dimensions.
The diagram was evaluated for vanishing electron mass in
[
202
]
,
but here we keep the electron massive.

The dashed lines represent photons.
The solid lines are electrons with mass
m
.
The double lines denote muons with mass
M\sim 200m
.
The set of edges is

⬇

edges

=

[((0,1),

1,

’0’),

((0,4),

1,

’
MM
’),

((1,5),

1,

’
mm
’),

((5,2),

1,

’
mm
’),

((5,3),

1,

’0’),

((4,3),

1,

’
MM
’),

((4,2),

1,

’0’)]

where
mm
and
MM
stand for
m^{2}
and
M^{2}
respectively.
We choose a phase space point similar to that of
[
202
, Section 4.1.2]
:

\displaystyle p_{0}^{2}=M^{2}=1\,,\quad p_{1}^{2}=p_{2}^{2}=m^{2}=1/200\,,\quad s_{01}=-1/7\,,\quad s_{12}=-1/3\,.

(11.12)

By momentum conservation,
this fixes

\displaystyle s_{02}=2M^{2}-2m^{2}-s_{01}-s_{12}=2.49\,.

(11.13)

With additional settings
\lambda=1.29
and
N=10^{8}
,
the result comes out to

⬇

Prefactor
:

gamma
(2*
eps

+

1).

(
Effective
)

kinematic

regime
:

Minkowski

(
exceptional
).

Finished

in

6.53

seconds
.

eps
^0:

[1.16483

+/-

0.00083]

+

i

*

[0.24155

+/-

0.00074]

--

eps
^1:

[5.5387

+/-

0.0086

]

+

*

[2.2818

+/-

0.0093

]

[15.171

+/-

0.058

]

+

[10.079

+/-

0.064

]

[

0.32

]

+

[

0.28

]

--

eps
^4:

[
