11.2.7 An elliptic, conformal, 4-point integral

The final example is a 1-loop 4-point conformally invariant integral
[
203
]
.
The analytic result for this diagram with edge weights

\nu_{0}=\nu_{1}=\nu_{2}=\nu_{3}=1/2

and dimension
\mathrm{D}=2
was calculated in
[
204
, Section 7.2]
:

\displaystyle\parbox[c]{103.6653pt}{\includegraphics[]{Figs/1L_3pt_conformal.pdf}}\ =\ \frac{4}{\sqrt{-p_{2}^{2}}}\big[K(z)K(1-\bar{z})+K(\bar{z})K(1-z)\big]\,.

(11.19)

This result features the elliptic integral
K
given by

\displaystyle K(z)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\sqrt{(1-x^{2})(1-z^{2}x^{2})}}

(11.20)

and kinematic variables

\displaystyle z\bar{z}=\frac{p_{0}^{2}}{p_{2}^{2}}\,,\quad(1-z)(1-\bar{z})=\frac{p_{1}^{2}}{p_{2}^{2}}\,.

(11.21)

The denominator of (
11.19
) differs from
[
204
, Equation (7.6)]

because we have used conformal symmetry to send
x_{3}\to\infty
,
where
x_{3}
is a dual momentum variable
(defined by
p_{i}=x_{i}-x_{i+1}
).
This reduces the kinematic space to that of a 3-point function.

In
Feyntrop
we thus specify an edge list for a 1-loop graph with 3 external legs:

⬇

edges

=

[((0,1),

1/2,

’0’),

((1,2),

1/2,

’0’),

((2,0),

1/2,

’0’)]

All internal masses are set to zero,
otherwise conformal symmetry would be broken,
and every edge weight has been set to
1/2
.
It is most natural to study conformal integrals in the Euclidean regime,
so let us pick

\displaystyle p_{0}^{2}=-2\,,\quad p_{1}^{2}=-3\,,\quad p_{2}^{2}=-5\,.

(11.22)

With
N=10^{8}
sampling points,
we obtain

⬇

(
Effective
)

kinematic

regime
:

Euclidean

(
generic
).

Finished

in

1.34

seconds
.

--

eps
^0:

[9.97192

+/-

0.00027]

+

i

*

[0.0

+/-

0.0]

This result agrees with the analytic formula (
11.19
).
We hope that our code can furnish further study into conformal integrals,
as they are important for
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM and the cosmological bootstrap.

Chapter 12
Conclusion and Outlook

Conclusion

This thesis has been an investigation into the mathematical structure of Feynman integrals.
Its overarching goal has been to develop novel techniques for evaluating these integrals.

In
Part I
of this thesis,
we studied integration-by-parts identities and the method of Pfaffian DEQs for evaluating Feynman integrals.
When the Feynman integrals in question admit a solution in terms of multiple polylogarithms,
we showed how the Magnus expansion can bring the Pfaffian system into canonical form,
whose solution is readily obtained via the path-ordered exponential.

These techniques were used to compute form factors for a leptophillic dark matter model.
The calculation involved a class of 2-loop 3-point Feynman integrals with massive internal propagators,
which were solved for in terms of multiple polylogarithms.

In
Part II
,
we delved deeper into the mathematics of DEQs by formalizing the notion of a
\mathcal{D}
-module.
A
\mathcal{D}
-module encapsulates the algebraic properties of differential operators
that annihilate a given function such as a Feynman integral.

We studied a broad class of functions called Euler integrals containing Feynman integrals as a special case.
Euler integrals are annihilated by a known collection of
higher-order differential operators called the GKZ system.
We showed how to connect the GKZ system to the more
manageable first-order Pfaffian system through the Macaulay matrix.

In some cases,
the Euler integral matched exactly with a Feynman integral.
In other cases,
the Euler integral had too many unfixed parameters.
The latter case was treated by developing two new
\mathcal{D}
-module restriction methods.
One of those methods drove us to establish an algorithm for
obtaining logarithmic series solutions to Pfaffian systems.

In
Part III
,
we formulated a numerical integration scheme for the evaluation of Feynman integrals
which combined Monte Carlo integration with tropical geometry.
The algorithm relied on the assumption that the Newton polytope of the

\mathcal{F}
Symanzik polynomial is a generalized permutahedron,
and we gave a detailed discussion of when this can be expected to hold.
The
i\varepsilon
-prescription was incorporated into the algorithm via contour deformation,
which allowed us to evaluate Feynman integrals in physical regions of kinematic space.
The algorithm was implemented in a publicly available program named
feyntrop
,
whose validity was verified against existing numerical codes.
We used
Feyntrop
to numerically evaluate Feynman integrals between 1-5 loops and 0-5 legs.

Outlook

There are several avenues of research to pursue based on the results of this thesis.

Applying the Macaulay matrix to other ideals.

Algorithm (
1
) takes a holonomic ideal
\mathcal{I}

together with a
\mathcal{D}
-module basis as input,
feeds it through the Macaulay matrix,
and outputs a Pfaffian system.
In all examples of this thesis,
we worked with the GKZ system
\mathcal{I}=H_{A}(\beta)
simply
because
H_{A}(\beta)
and a basis of standard monomials
are easily determined given an Euler integral.
However,
there might very well be other situations where an annihilating ideal is known.
If a basis is not known,
it could be guessed by executing Algorithm (
1
)
(with parameters set to numbers)
for different choices of standard monomials until it succeeds.

A non-GKZ example is for instance the conformally invariant,
massless,
1-loop 3-point function studied in
[
139
]
.
An annihilating ideal was there determined from the generators of conformal symmetry.
It would be interesting to study these operators with the Macaulay matrix,
as well as those coming from the larger Yangian symmetry group
[
205
]
.

Another route would be to employ the algorithms from

[
206
,
180
,
207
]

to compute Picard-Fuchs ideals for Feynman integrals;
these algorithms were effective for rather complicated diagrams,
e.g. at 2 loops and 7 points.
As an aside,
it would be interesting to see whether a canonical Feynman integral has any nice
structure when it is represented as an operator inside the
\mathcal{D}
-module
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{I}
,
where
\mathcal{I}
is the Picard-Fuchs ideal.

More complicated examples of logarithmic restriction.

An algorithm for the calculation of logarithmic series solutions to
Pfaffian systems was presented in Section (
8.2.4
).
We gave results for 1-loop Bhabha scattering in Section (
9.2
),
and partial results for the 2-loop Bhabha double-box in Section (
9.3
).
It would be instructive to first complete the 2-loop calculation;
this involves the determination of boundary constants and power corrections
(m^{2}/s)^{n}
.

Afterwards, it would be interesting to see how far this approach can go.
The complexity of the restricted Pfaffian systems for the
2-loop Bhabha double-box was miniscule compared to today’s standards,
indicating that more complicated diagrams could potentially be evaluated with this method.

Expansion around singular locus.

Recall the form of the logarithmic expansion ansatz (
8.54
).
There we chose a distinguished singularity
z_{1}=0
to expand around.
It would be interesting to reformulate the logarithmic restriction procedure
to instead expand around the entire singular locus
L(z)
of a Pfaffian system,
where
L(z)
is a product of irreducible hypersurfaces.
For instance,
the singular locus for Appell’s
F_{4}
function is

L(z)=z_{1}z_{2}[(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}-2(z_{1}+z_{2})+1]
.
For Feynman integrals,

L(z)
would correspond to the
Landau variety

[
188
]
.

The series ansatz is now roughly represented as
(see
[
157
, Equation (12.15)]
for details)

\displaystyle\vec{I}(z)\quad\sim\quad L(z)^{\lambda}\times\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{M}\vec{I}\,^{(n,m)}(z)\times L(z)^{n}\times\log[L(z)]^{m}\,,

(12.1)

where
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Analytical solutions to Feynman integrals are often hard to numerically evaluate near Landau singularities,
but a solution written in this fashion could potentially have better numerical stability.

Applications in algebraic statistics.

Looking beyond physics,
one finds that GKZ systems and Euler integrals also
frequently appear in the course of Bayesian inference
in algebraic statistics
[
208
, Section 3]
.

The article
[
209
]
gave an initial
application of the Macaulay matrix in this context;
it is natural to expect that this research direction can be pushed even further
with the more sophisticated Macaulay matrix method developed here.
Further, it appears that the Pfaffian-level restriction protocol
from Section (
8.2
) can be used to study
contingency tables
1
1

1

These tables are a standard tool in statistics
to record interrelations between random variables.

in statistics
[
210
]
.

Numerators and
\epsilon
-divergent integrals in
Feyntrop
​​.

The program
Feyntrop
presented in Chapter (
11
) has two main
shortcomings which would be beneficial to eliminate in future versions:

1.

It only works for scalar Feynman integrals.
But in gauge theories such as QED and QCD,
one encounters numerators in momentum space representation of the form

(p\cdot\ell)^{\nu}
,
where
p
and
\ell
are external and loop momenta respectively.

2.

It assumes a quasi-finite integral representation (cf. Section (
10.2.2
)).

By the formulas of
[
189
, Appendix A]
,
numerators can be written in the Symanzik representation by including an extra
(homogeneous) polynomial
\mathcal{N}
inside the integrand.
There is no reason to expect that this polynomial would
enjoy the generalized permutahedron property though,
so it might not be straightforward to include it in
the MC sampling of Algorithm (
6
).

The quasi-finite condition can be lifted by following the protocol in

[
191
, Page 5]
,
where explicit formulas are given for factoring out
\epsilon
-poles in front of finite integrals.
These formulas would involve derivatives of the
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{F}
Symanzik polynomials,
and we fortunately have efficient expressions for those (cf. Section (
10.3.3
)).

With these two additions,
we envision that
Feyntrop
could be used in a wide range of phenomenological calculations.
Especially pertaining to scattering amplitudes involving many mass scales,
which are the hardest ones to treat analytically with DEQs due
to the proliferation of functions beyond the class of multiple polylogarithms
2
2

2

Feyntrop
has already been used to check results for a non-planar 3-loop
calculation related to Higgs+jet production
[
211
]
.

.

A canonical contour deformation.

In Section (
10.2.1
),
we implemented the
i\varepsilon
-prescription via a contour deformation of the Schwinger parameters

x_{e}\to x_{e}\exp\left[-i\lambda\partial_{e}\mathcal{V}\right].

The small, but finite, number
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is a free parameter.
An explicit value has to be specified by the user in
Feyntrop
​​,
and the accuracy of the result depends quite sensitively on this choice.

It would be a significant development to determine a
canonical
choice for
\lambda
,
depending on the data of the Feynman integral in question as well as the given phase space point.
This would not only improve the user experience in
Feyntrop
​​,
but also constitute the first example of a
contour deformation for Feynman integrals without any free parameters.

Appendix A
Details on
\mathcal{D}
-modules

Here we collect some additional details on
\mathcal{D}
-modules that are referred to in the main text.

Section A.1
Basis change in a holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-module

The goal in this section is to construct the Pfaffian system for the basis
e

\displaystyle\partial_{i}e=P_{i}^{(e)}\cdot e

(A.1)

given a known system in the standard monomial basis

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\mathrm{Std}=P_{i}^{(\mathrm{Std})}\cdot\mathrm{Std}\,.

(A.2)

e\in(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{I})^{R}
is here an
R
-dimensional vector of
(equivalence classes of) differential operators.

Observe that,
if we could find a matrix
G
relating the two bases by

\displaystyle e=G\cdot\mathrm{Std}\,,

(A.3)

then the problem would be solved by a gauge transformation
1
1

1

Note that
G
is invertible under the assumption that
e
and
\mathrm{Std}
are both bases.

\displaystyle P_{i}^{(e)}=\left[\partial_{i}\bullet G\hskip 5.69046pt+\hskip 5.69046ptG\cdot P_{i}^{(\mathrm{Std})}\right]\cdot G^{-1}\,.

(A.4)

To construct
G
,
we begin by writing the decomposition

\displaystyle e=\sum_{q}G^{(1)}_{q}\cdot\left(\partial^{q}\,\mathrm{Std}\right)\,,

(A.5)

where

G_{q}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{C}(z)^{R\times R}
.
In other words,
we write each element of
e
as a sum of rational functions
times derivatives
\partial^{q}
that act on
\mathrm{Std}
.
The idea is now to transform the expression
\partial^{q}\,\mathrm{Std}
into the form

G_{q}^{(2)}\cdot\mathrm{Std},

for some

G_{q}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{C}(z)^{R\times R},

by repeated application of (
A.2
).
This is best illustrated by means of an example.

Example A.1.
Let

q=\{1,1,0,0,\ldots,0\}.

Then
\partial^{q}=\partial_{1}\partial_{2}
.
Applying (
A.2
) twice,
the expression
\partial^{q}\,\mathrm{Std}
becomes

\displaystyle\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\,\mathrm{Std}

\displaystyle=\partial_{1}\left[P_{2}^{(\mathrm{Std})}\cdot\mathrm{Std}\right]

(A.6)

\displaystyle=\left[\partial_{1}\bullet P_{2}^{(\mathrm{Std})}\right]\cdot\mathrm{Std}\hskip 5.69046pt+\hskip 5.69046ptP_{2}^{(\mathrm{Std})}\cdot\left[\partial_{1}\,\mathrm{Std}\right]

(A.7)

\displaystyle=\left[\partial_{1}\bullet P_{2}^{(\mathrm{Std})}\right]\cdot\mathrm{Std}\hskip 5.69046pt+\hskip 5.69046ptP_{2}^{(\mathrm{Std})}\cdot P_{1}^{(\mathrm{Std})}\cdot\mathrm{Std}\,.

(A.8)

The matrix

\displaystyle G_{q}^{(2)}=\partial_{1}\bullet P_{2}^{(\mathrm{Std})}\hskip 5.69046pt+\hskip 5.69046ptP_{2}^{(\mathrm{Std})}\cdot P_{1}^{(\mathrm{Std})}\,,

(A.9)

hence satisfies the sought after relation

\partial^{q}\,\mathrm{Std}=G_{q}^{(2)}\cdot\mathrm{Std}

.

\blacksquare

We conclude that the gauge transformation matrix
G
in (
A.3
) takes the form

\displaystyle G=\sum_{q}G_{q}^{(1)}\cdot G_{q}^{(2)}

(A.10)

where
G_{q}^{(2)}
is built from matrix products between Pfaffian matrices
P_{i}^{(\mathrm{Std})}

and derivatives thereof.

Section A.2
Basis change without derivatives

We concluded above that the gauge transformation matrix (
A.10
)
is constructable from derivatives of Pfaffian matrices
P_{i}(z)
.
In the GKZ setting we might,
however,
like to fix some of the
z
-variables to numbers.
Then we no longer have the luxury of taking derivatives.
As a remedy to this,
let us derive a formula for
\partial_{i}\bullet P_{j}
which is based solely on matrix multiplication.

Using the same setup as in Section (
6.6
),
we set

\displaystyle f(\beta)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\beta_{0}+1)}\int_{\Gamma}g(z|x)^{\beta_{0}}x^{-\beta^{\prime}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x}\quad\text{and}\quad F(\beta)=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{Std}_{1}\bullet f(\beta)\\
\vdots\\
\mathrm{Std}_{R}\bullet f(\beta)\end{array}\right]\,.

As described in Section (
6.6
),
there are two ways of expressing the derivative of
F(\beta)
:

\displaystyle\partial_{j}\bullet F(\beta)

\displaystyle=P_{j}(\beta)\cdot F(\beta)

(A.14)

\displaystyle\partial_{j}\bullet F(\beta)

\displaystyle=F(\beta-a_{j})\,.

(A.15)

Differentiating (
A.14
) w.r.t.
z_{i}
,
we have

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\,\partial_{j}\bullet F(\beta)

\displaystyle=(\partial_{j}\bullet P_{i})\cdot F+P_{j}\cdot(\partial_{i}\bullet F)

(A.16)

\displaystyle=(\partial_{j}\bullet P_{i})\cdot F+P_{j}\cdot P_{i}\cdot F\,,

(A.17)

where we omitted the
\beta
-dependence for clarity.
On the other hand,
differentiating (
A.15
) yields

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\,\partial_{j}\bullet F(\beta)

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}\bullet F(\beta-a_{j})

(A.18)

\displaystyle=F(\beta-a_{j}-a_{i})

(A.19)

\displaystyle=P_{j}(\beta-a_{i})\cdot P_{i}(\beta)\cdot F(\beta)\,,

(A.20)

where the identity

P_{k}(\beta)\cdot F(\beta)=F(\beta-a_{k})

was applied twice in the final step.
Equating (
A.17
) with (
A.20
) and isolating
(\partial_{i}\bullet P_{j})
,
we conclude that

\displaystyle(\partial_{i}\bullet P_{j})(\beta)=\big[P_{j}(\beta-a_{i})-P_{j}(\beta)\big]\cdot P_{i}(\beta)\,.

(A.21)

The RHS of this equation can then be inserted into the
G
-matrix of (
A.10
).

Section A.3
General formula for homogeneity rescaling

We gave an example in Section (
6.5
) of how to rescale
the GKZ variables
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{N})
so as to fix
n+1
of them to unity
(cf. equation (
6.83
)).
This section presents a general formula.

To start,
given an
(n+1)\times N
matrix

A=\big[a_{1}\ |\ \ldots\ |\ a_{N}\big]

and a list of
n+1
rescaling parameters

t=\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{n+1}\},

we construct a list of
N
exponentials

\displaystyle t^{A}:=\{t^{a_{1}},\ldots,t^{a_{N}}\}\quad\text{where}\quad t^{a_{i}}:=\prod_{j=1}^{n+1}t_{j}^{(a_{i})_{j}}\,.

(A.22)

This matrix exponentiation notation obeys the rule

(t^{A})^{B}=t^{A\cdot B}.

For two sets
r
and
s
,
both of length
N
,
the product
r\times s
is defined to be

\displaystyle r\times s:=\{r_{1}s_{1},\ldots,r_{N}s_{N}\}\,.

(A.23)

In this language,
the homogeneity property (
6.77
) reads

\displaystyle f_{\beta}(t^{A}\times z)=t^{\beta}f_{\beta}(z)\,.

(A.24)

Now fix two subsets

\displaystyle\sigma\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}\quad\text{and}\quad\eta=\{1,\ldots,N\}\setminus\sigma\,,

(A.25)

where
\sigma
has size
|\sigma|=n+1
,
and
\eta
is its complement.
Let
A_{\sigma}
denote the submatrix of
A
whose columns are labeled by
\sigma
.
We assume that
\sigma
is chosen such that
A_{\sigma}
is invertible,
i.e.
\det(A_{\sigma})\neq 0
.

Returning to (
A.24
),
a judicial choice for
t
turns out to be

\displaystyle t=z_{\sigma}^{-A_{\sigma}^{-1}}\,,

(A.26)

where
z_{\sigma}
picks out the variables of
z=\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{N}\}
labeled by
\sigma
.
The homogeneity relation then becomes

\displaystyle f_{\beta}\big(z_{\sigma}^{-A_{\sigma}^{-1}\cdot\,A}\times z\big)=z_{\sigma}^{-A_{\sigma}^{-1}\cdot\,\beta}f_{\beta}(z)\,.

(A.27)

For this choice of
t
,
all the arguments on the LHS labeled by
\sigma
are set to unity:

\displaystyle\Big\{z_{\sigma}^{-A_{\sigma}^{-1}\cdot\,A}\times z\Big\}_{i}=\begin{cases}1\,,&i\in\sigma\\
z_{\sigma}^{-A_{\sigma}^{-1}\cdot\,a_{i}}\,z_{i}\,,&i\in\eta\,.\end{cases}

(A.28)

As desired,
the function on the RHS of (
A.27
)

\displaystyle g_{\beta}(w):=z_{\sigma}^{-A_{\sigma}^{-1}\cdot\,\beta}f_{\beta}(z)

(A.29)

now effectively only depends on
N-(n+1)
cross ratios

\displaystyle w:=\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{N-(n+1)}\}:=z_{\sigma}^{-A_{\sigma}^{-1}\cdot\,A_{\eta}}\times z_{\eta}\,.

(A.30)

What’s left is to rewrite the GKZ system in terms of the
w
-variables.
To this end,
we summarize a result which is proved in
[
82
, Appendix A]
.
Given two positive integers
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
we first define the
rising
and
falling factorials
by

\displaystyle(a)_{b}

\displaystyle:=a(a+1)(a+2)\cdots(a+b+1)

(A.31)

\displaystyle[a]_{b}

\displaystyle:=a(a-1)(a-2)\cdots(a-b+1)\,.

(A.32)

Given an
N
-dimensional vector
u
and an integer vector
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}_{\geq 0}
,
we extend these definitions to

\displaystyle(u)_{v}

\displaystyle:=(u_{1})_{v_{1}}\cdots(u_{N})_{v_{N}}

(A.33)

\displaystyle[u]_{v}

\displaystyle:=[u_{1}]_{v_{1}}\cdots[u_{N}]_{v_{N}}\,.

(A.34)

In what follows,
we shall set

\displaystyle u=\theta_{w}=\big[\theta_{w_{1}},\,\ldots,\,\theta_{w_{N-(n+1)}}\big]^{T}\,,

(A.35)

where
\theta_{i}=z_{i}\partial_{i}
.

To rewrite the GKZ system in terms of
w
,
it suffices to find a relation between

\partial_{z}^{v}\bullet f_{\beta}(z)

and
g_{\beta}(w)
for
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}_{\geq 0}
,
The master formula works out to

\displaystyle\partial_{z}^{v}\bullet f_{\beta}(z)=

(A.36)

\displaystyle(-1)^{|v_{\sigma}|}z_{\sigma}^{-A_{\sigma}^{-1}\cdot\big(\beta+A\cdot v\big)}\left\{w^{-v_{\eta}}\ \big(A_{\sigma}^{-1}\cdot\beta+A_{\sigma}^{-1}\cdot A_{\eta}\cdot\theta_{w}\big)_{v_{\sigma}}\ [\theta_{w}]_{v_{\eta}}\ \bullet\ g_{\beta}(w)\right\}\ \Big|_{w=\text{\eqref{w_cross_ratios}}}\,,

where
|v_{\sigma}|=\sum_{i\in\sigma}v_{i}
.
Although this expression looks somewhat daunting,
it has fortunately been implemented in the
asir
package
mt_gkz
.

Example A.1.
For the
{}_{2}F_{1}
case of Example (
6.5
),
the following script will output a GKZ system in rescaled variables:

⬇

A

=

[

[1,1,1,1],

[0,1,0,1],

[0,0,1,1]

];

Beta

=

[
b1
,
b2
,
b3
];

Sigma

=

[1,2,3];

Ideal

=

mt_gkz
.
gkz_b
(
A
,

Beta
,

Sigma

|

partial

=

1);

The choice of
Sigma
means that we choose to rescale
z_{1}=z_{2}=z_{3}=1
.
The option
partial = 1
indicates that the ideal is to be written in terms of

\partial_{i}
rather than
\theta_{i}
symbols.
The list
Ideal
will contain the single operator (
6.88
)
(albeit in variables
x_{i}
rather than
z_{i}
, as this is the convention for GKZ variables in
asir
).

\blacksquare

The formula (
6.64
) for the operator
d_{q}
,
which maps a differential form
[\omega_{q}]
to its corresponding
\mathcal{D}
-module element,
has also been implemented with rescaled variables via the command
mt_gkz.rvec_red2(...)
.

Section A.4
GKZ systems are isomorphic to twisted cohomology groups

Section (
6.3.2
) mentioned an isomorphism between the
GKZ system
\mathcal{D}_{N}/H_{A}(\beta)
and the twisted de Rham cohomology group
\mathbb{H}^{k}_{A}(\beta)
.
This section provides a very rough sketch of the proof behind this theorem.
Heavy machinery from category theory is required to prove this in detail,
which is outside the scope of this thesis,
so we will content ourselves with summarizing the key steps.
The idea is to show that the GKZ system and the twisted cohomology group
are both isomorphic to another
\mathcal{D}
-module
\int_{\pi}\mathcal{L}
,
to be described shortly,
wherefore
\mathcal{D}/H_{A}(\beta)
and
\mathbb{H}^{k}_{A}(\beta)
must also be isomorphic.

Begin by recalling the space
\mathcal{O}(X)
from (
6.52
).
It is equipped with the structure of an abelian group under the addition of functions.
As mentioned in Section (
4.1
),
we can turn an abelian group into a
\mathcal{D}
-module by specifying an action of the Weyl algebra
\mathcal{D}
.
Given a function
h\in\mathcal{O}(X)
,
the action is here

\displaystyle\partial_{z_{i}}\bullet h

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial h}{\partial z_{i}}+\frac{\beta_{0}}{g(z|x)}\frac{\partial g(z|x)}{\partial z_{i}}h

(A.37)

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i}}\bullet h

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{i}}+\frac{\beta_{0}}{g(z|x)}\frac{\partial g(z|x)}{\partial x_{i}}h-\frac{\beta_{i}}{x_{i}}h\,.

(A.38)

The resulting
\mathcal{D}
-module is denoted by the symbol
\mathcal{L}
instead of
\mathcal{O}(X)
.

Let

\pi:(z,x)\to z

be a projection map.
The
direct image

\mathcal{D}
-module

\int_{\pi}\mathcal{L},

has an abstract categorical definition
2
2

2

In categorical terms,
given a morphism

f:X\to Y

between two complex manifolds
X
and
Y
,
the direct image
\int_{f}
is a
functor
depending on
f
.
Loosely speaking,
it acts as a certain
pushforward
from sheaves on
X
to sheaves on
Y
.
See
[
23
, Section 1.3]
for details.

,
but for the case of a projection it can be concretely realized as a quotient space that we proceed to build.
Write

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{N+n}=\mathbb{C}\langle z_{1},\ldots,z_{N},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},\partial_{z_{1}},\ldots,\partial_{z_{N}},\partial_{x_{1}},\ldots,\partial_{x_{n}}\rangle

(A.39)

for the ring of differential operators in
N+n
variables.
The subring

\mathcal{D}_{N}

contains operators in the
z
-variables only.
It is possible to write

\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{D}_{N+n}/\mathcal{I},

where
\mathcal{I}
is the annihilating ideal of the Euler inte
grand

\displaystyle U=g(z|x)^{\beta_{0}}x^{\beta^{\prime}}\,.

(A.40)

By differentiating
U
w.r.t.
(z,x)
and recalling the expression for
g=g(z|x)
from
(
6.2
),
a short calculation shows that
\mathcal{I}
is generated by

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{D}\big[g\,\partial_{z_{i}}-\beta_{0}\,x^{\alpha_{i}}\big]\hskip 5.69046pt+\hskip 5.69046pt\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{D}\Big[x_{i}\,g\,\partial_{x_{i}}-\beta_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(\alpha_{j})_{i}\,z_{j}\,x^{\alpha_{j}}+\beta_{i}\,g\Big]\,,

(A.41)

where
(\alpha_{j})_{i}
denotes the
i
th component of the multi-index
\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
The direct image
\mathcal{D}
-module then turns out to be represented by the quotient

\displaystyle\int_{\pi}\mathcal{L}=\frac{\mathcal{D}_{N+n}}{\mathcal{I}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{x_{i}}\mathcal{D}_{N+n}}\,.

(A.42)

This is now a
\mathcal{D}_{N}
-module,
as the
x
-variables have been "projected out" by the map
\pi
.
The RHS of (
A.42
) is also called the
integration module

[
86
, Chapter 6]
3
3

3

There are several
asir
packages for computing annihilation ideals of Euler integrands

[
212
]
.
An algorithm for computing integration modules has been implemented in the package

nk_restriction

[
149
]
.

.
It is an algebraic counterpart to the familiar integration operation in calculus for the following reason:
writing

\displaystyle\int_{\pi}\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{D}_{N}/\mathcal{J}

(A.43)

for some
\mathcal{J}
,
then the generators of
\mathcal{J}
can be shown to annihilate the Euler inte
gral
itself

[
85
, Chapter 5]
.
It is rather surprising that the integration of a function,
which is generally a highly transcendental operation,
can be captured in purely algebraic terms!

Now,
it is a classical result that the twisted cohomology group

\mathbb{H}^{k}_{A}(\beta)

is isomorphic to the direct image
\int_{\pi}\mathcal{L}
as a
\mathcal{D}
-module

[
23
, Chapter 4]

[
213
]
.
The addition of differential forms forms an abelian group,
and
\mathbb{H}^{k}_{A}(\beta)
indeed becomes a
\mathcal{D}
-module under the action

\displaystyle\partial_{z_{i}}\bullet[\omega(z)]=\left[\frac{\partial\omega(z)}{\partial z_{i}}+\beta_{0}\frac{x^{\alpha_{i}}}{g(z|x)}\omega(z)\right]\,,

(A.44)

where

[\omega(z)]\in\mathbb{H}^{k}_{A}(\beta)

denotes an equivalence class of differential forms.
What is more,
Gel’fand, Kapranov and Zelevinsky
proved that the annihilating ideal
\mathcal{J}
defined above precisely equals
H_{A}(\beta)

[
42
]
.
In other words,

\int_{\pi}\mathcal{L}
is isomorphic to the GKZ system.

Since
\int_{\pi}\mathcal{L}
is isomorphic to both the GKZ system
and
the twisted cohomology group,
we finally infer that

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{N}/H_{A}(\beta)\quad\cong\quad\mathbb{H}^{k}_{A}(\beta)\,.

(A.45)

We emphasize that this isomorphism only holds true when the
z
-variables
are indeterminate and the
\beta
-parameters are non-resonant.
